
[PDF page 1]
Low-Overhead Quantum Error Correction with Boundary-Connected Planar Modules
Oscar Higgott, 1,∗ Hasan Sayginel, 1,† Francisco J.H. Heras, 2 Zhiyang He, 1, 3
Tomas Jochym-O’Connor,1 Andrew W. Senior, 2 Lei M. Zhang, 2 Thomas Edlich,2 James S. Spencer, 2
Matt McEwen,1 Craig Gidney, 1 Johannes Bausch,2 Pushmeet Kohli, 2 and Hartmut Neven 1
1Google Quantum AI
2Google DeepMind
3Department of Mathematics, Massachusetts Institute of Technology
(Dated: October 5, 2026)
Realizing practical quantum computers requires quantum error correction, but the most widely
implemented approach, the planar surface code, demands a substantial physical qubit overhead.
Here, we demonstrate that partitioning quantum processors into manageable, flat modules with
non-local inter-module connections provides a natural solution. By wiring sparse, static boundary
connections between Euclidean planar modules, we construct modular hyperbolic surface and color
codes, based on new families of semi-hyperbolic codes. Using modular syndrome extraction circuits
and efficient neural network and matching decoders, our circuit-level simulations show that this
modular memory design can reduce the physical qubit overhead by tenfold or more compared to the
surface code, even under elevated inter-module seam error rates. Projecting to larger system sizes,
we find modular codes with encoding ratek/n= 1/16 and distanced≥22, exceeding both the rate
and distance of the [[288,12,18]] two-gross bivariate bicycle code while using mostly nearest-neighbor
gates within planar modules, and implying an over 30×overhead reduction relative to surface codes.
We develop fault-tolerant walking circuits for implementing logic via code automorphisms, which we
co-design with low-weight, highly symmetrical logical bases and a modular extractor system that is
≈4.5×smaller than the code itself. Combining these methods, our modular memory can be used
as dense storage in a universal fault-tolerant architecture, unlocking the efficiency of high-rate codes
without sacrificing the fabrication advantages of planar modules.
Quantum computers can solve certain computational
problems exponentially faster than any known classical
algorithm [1, 2], with anticipated applications in quan-
tum simulation for material science, drug design and
other areas [3, 4], while also presenting significant chal-
lenges to current cryptographic infrastructure [2]. How-
ever, realizing these applications requires quantum er-
ror correction (QEC), in which many physical qubits are
encoded into logical qubits to protect information from
inevitable noise and imperfections in the hardware.
The surface code has led experimental efforts in this
direction, owing to its compatibility with a square-grid
layout on a planar chip, and recently enabled the first ex-
perimental demonstrations of exponential suppression of
the logical error rate at physical error rates below thresh-
old [5]. However, the surface code requires a substantial
resource overhead to achieve logical error rates relevant
to practical applications, with utility-scale fault-tolerant
quantum computers expected to require around 100k to
1 million qubits [6, 7]. Furthermore, fabricating a mono-
lithic chip at this scale becomes impractical, due to fun-
damental constraints on fabrication, yield and coupler
density (see Figure 1a), motivating modular architectures
in which smaller units are networked together [8–10].
An alternative path to fault-tolerant quantum compu-
tation with lower overhead lies in the use of more general,
high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC)
∗ oscarhiggott@google.com
† hasansayginel@google.com
codes [12]. In principle these codes can substantially re-
duce resource overheads relative to the surface code [11],
however their implementation in solid state systems re-
quires a high density of long-range crossing couplers (see
Figure 1b).
Here we demonstrate that the practical necessity of
partitioning a quantum computer into modules can be
leveraged to dramatically reduce the resource require-
ments of QEC. We show that high encoding rates can
be achieved using flat Euclidean modules of around 100
qubits, with sparse and fixed non-local couplers connect-
ing qubits at module boundaries (see Figure 1c). This
connectivity is motivated by state-of-the-art supercon-
ducting quantum computers, for which local planar gates
now operate below the surface code threshold [5], while
link resonators connecting chips through cables have been
demonstrated to achieve error rates approaching 1% [13–
15].
Our constructions are derived from color codes and
surface codes on high-genus hyperbolic surfaces [16–20].
Each module forms a locally flat Euclidean tile of the
overall QLDPC code. These tiles are connected using
inter-module couplers to form the global connectivity of
the hyperbolic tiling [20, 21]. We design modular syn-
drome extraction circuits for these codes that are tailored
to our distributed hardware model and develop efficient
and accurate machine learning [22, 23] and matching de-
coders [24]. Our circuit-level simulations demonstrate a
10×reduction in physical qubit overhead compared to
the surface code at a logical error rate of 10 −10, assum-
ing physical error rates expected for near-term experi-

ments (SI1000 noise model withp= 0.1%, see Meth-
ods) and inter-module gates that are ten times noisier
(pseam = 1%). This is achieved with a modular hy-
perbolic surface code of 120 modules of 116 qubits each
(see Figure 3), using multi-rate syndrome extraction cir-
cuits that show substantial tolerance to slow and noisy
inter-module couplers. Projecting to larger system sizes,
we find these savings scale to an over 30×overhead re-
duction, requiring only 36 physical qubits per logical
qubit, including measure qubits, to achieve a distance
of 20–24. This is both lower overhead and higher dis-
tance than the [[288,12,18]] two-gross bivariate bicycle
code (48 physical qubits per logical qubit, including check
qubits) [11, 25], despite requiring much simpler connec-
tivity that is mostly local in 2D. By comparison, a Eu-
clidean surface code at the same distance requires over
1000 physical qubits per logical qubit. Previous work
using 2D nearest-neighbor gates [7, 26–29] or hyperbolic
codes [20, 30, 31] has demonstrated qubit overhead re-
ductions of up to around 5×at the circuit level.
Furthermore, we show how our modular QLDPC codes
can be augmented to an architecture capable of imple-
menting universal fault-tolerant computation. We design
fault-tolerant circuits for implementing logical gates via
code automorphisms, corresponding to symmetries of the
hyperbolic tiling that permute the modules, using only
nearest-neighbor gates within each module and one ad-
ditional seam of inter-module couplers on each side of a
module. We further design low-weight, highly symmet-
rical logical bases, as well as modular extractor systems
that can measure logical Pauli operators via code surgery.
These techniques are combined to enable a single extrac-
tor system to access hundreds of distinct logical Pauli
operators, allowing logical qubits to be teleported into
and out of the code, and enabling universal computation
with low space overhead. All of these methods can be
implemented using hardware components that have al-
ready been demonstrated with fidelity very close to those
assumed here [5, 13–15].
I. MODULAR HYPERBOLIC CODES
Our codes are defined on tilings of closed hyperbolic
surfaces, i.e. closed surfaces of constant negative curva-
ture, which have genusg≥2. Codes defined on such
tilings encodeklogical qubits intonphysical qubits
with a constant encoding ratek/n[16–18]. While other
QLDPC code families are known to have constant rate
asymptotically [32–34], the rate for hyperbolic codes is
unusually high in practice, approaching 1/2 for color
codes on 12.12.12 tilings [17, 18]. For example, our search
finds [[n, k, d]] = [[648,328,8]] and [[672,172,10]] hyper-
bolic color codes (see Table S10), wheredis the code
distance, which have rates comparable to recently pro-
posed ultra-high-rate QLDPC codes [35, 36]. Although
their distance grows logarithmically withn, for practical
system sizes it is only a factor of two to three below the
distances of around 20–24 needed for utility-scale quan-
tum computation.
We close this gap by fine-graining: we replace each face
of the base hyperbolic tiling with a patch of a Euclidean
lattice of side lengthL(withL= 1 recovering the base
tiling) to obtain asemi-hyperbolictiling, from which we
construct a new family of semi-hyperbolic surface codes,
distinct from those of Ref. [19], and, for the first time,
semi-hyperbolic color codes. Fine-graining leaveskun-
changed while increasingdby a factor of roughlyLand
nby a factor of roughlyL 2, trading encoding rate for dis-
tance while approximately preserving the figure of merit
kd2/n. Since the base hyperbolic codes have such a high
rate, the resulting codes retain high encoding rates (as
high ask/n= 1/16 among the codes we find) at large
distances (d≥22).
Fine-graining also flattens the tiling locally, allowing
the semi-hyperbolic code to be partitioned into Euclidean
planar modules. Semi-hyperbolic Floquet codes on the
same class of tilings, including their modular implemen-
tation, have previously been studied [20, 21] (the latter

using a generic graph-partitioning heuristic), but color
codes have not previously been constructed on them. We
refer to the resulting modular semi-hyperbolic codes sim-
ply asmodular hyperboliccodes, and find many new ex-
amples by searching over suitable hyperbolic tilings, as
described below.
A modular hyperboliccolorcode is derived from a
tiling of a hyperbolic surface with triangular faces. An
example of such a tiling of the infinite hyperbolic plane
with 8 triangles meeting at each vertex is shown in Fig-
ure 2a. The vertices of the tiling are three-colorable, with
one vertex of each color per triangle, and we assign each
edge the third color absent from its two vertices. A tiling
of a finite, closed surface can be obtained by cutting a
polygon (called thefundamental polygon) out of this infi-
nite plane and identifying (i.e. gluing) pairs of its bound-
ary edges together to fold the flat polygon into a closed
manifold. The fundamental polygon of the genus-2 Bolza
surface is shaded in Figure 2a, with the identified pairs
of edges labeled in Figure 2b. Each triangular face is re-
placed with a Euclidean hexagonal tiling of side length
Lto form a semi-hyperbolic tiling of hexagons and oc-
tagons [20]. AnXstabilizer and aZstabilizer generator
are then defined on each face of the resulting three-face-
colorable tiling.
We partition a semi-hyperbolic color code into modules
by deriving each module from a contiguous set of triangu-
lar faces in the base hyperbolic tiling. In Figure 2b-c we
show an example of aquadrilateralmodule partition, for
which each module is defined by a pair of triangles in the
base tiling that share an edge of a chosen color (e.g. red
edges). By combining the eight triangles together that
share a vertex of a given color (e.g. red vertices), we can
also obtain a largeroctagonmodule partition.
We also construct modular hyperbolicsurfacecodes,
derived instead from a hyperbolic tiling of quadrilateral
faces. Each quadrilateral face is replaced with a square-
grid tiling of side lengthL. Rather than placing data
qubits on the edges of this fine-grained tiling [19], we
take the dual tiling and bicolor its faces red and blue.
We then associate a data qubit with each vertex of this
dual tiling, anXstabilizer with each red face and aZ
stabilizer with each blue face (see Figure 3b), analogous
to the rotated surface code. Compared with placing data
qubits on edges, this has two advantages. First, for oddL
it distributes the high-weight corner stabilizers between
XandZtypes (which are related by a symmetry when
the tiling is regular, see Section S11 A), which can help
balance theXandZdistances and improve the code pa-
rameters. Second, no data qubits lie on the edges of the
base quadrilaterals, which allows uniform module parti-
tions.
Logical operators are supported on non-trivial cycles
around a handle of the surface, with a genus-gtiling sup-
porting 4glogical qubits for the hyperbolic color codes
and 2glogical qubits for the surface codes [37, 38]. By
the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the genus of a closed hyper-
bolic surface grows linearly with its area. Consequently,

[PDF page 4]
4
for a given code family, the number of logical qubits in-
creases linearly with the number of modules, alongside an
increasing distance (which grows with the tiling’s combi-
natorial systole). The negative curvature responsible for
this scaling is absent within any module, each of which
is a flat Euclidean patch, and arises entirely from the
inter-module couplers connecting them.
II. MODULAR SYNDROME EXTRACTION
We design fault-tolerant syndrome extraction cir-
cuits that respect the connectivity constraints of the
boundary-connected modular architecture, and bench-
mark them under a circuit-level noise model with elevated
seam noise. For the modular hyperbolic color codes, we
place a pair of measure qubits in each hexagonal plaque-
tte in the bulk and use them to prepare a Bell state.
The Bell state is coupled to the data qubits and used
to extract the plaquette’sXstabilizer, then measured to
obtain a stabilizer measurement along with a flag mea-
surement [39–41]. The process is then repeated in the
opposite basis for theZstabilizers. Stabilizers that span
modules are measured using cat states (Greenberger–
Horne–Zeilinger states) supported onmmeasure qubits,
which are prepared using inter-module couplers. Once
prepared, them-qubit cat state is then coupled to the
stabilizer’s data qubits using in-module gates and then
measured to obtain the stabilizer measurement andm−1
flag measurements.
The circuits for the modular hyperbolic surface codes
are similar, with some important differences. A single
measure qubit (instead of a Bell state) is used to measure
each square plaquette stabilizer in the bulk. TheXandZ
stabilizers are also interleaved, leading to commutativity
constraints. We find valid low-depth extraction schedules
by solving the relevant constraint satisfaction problem
on the base tiling and lifting to translationally-invariant
schedules within the modules. Additionally, we design
multi-rate circuits that measure the high-weight inter-
module stabilizers less frequently (once every three QEC
rounds), which we find leads to improved logical error
rates while allowing for slower inter-module gates.
To decode efficiently we develop and train a variant
of the AlphaQubit decoder (AQ) for the modular hyper-
bolic color codes [23]. For the modular hyperbolic surface
codes we decode using a correlated minimum-weight per-
fect matching (MWPM) decoder [24].
Our circuit-level simulations are presented in Figure 3.
For the modular hyperbolic color codes (decoded with Al-
phaQubit) our simulations show an 8×density improve-
ment when compared to the Euclidean surface code par-
titioned into modules of a similar size. By projecting to
larger system sizes (details in Methods), we find that the
savings increase to an over 30×overhead reduction, with
a modular hyperbolic color code requiring only 36 physi-
cal qubits per logical to achieve a distance of 20–24 using
planar 72-qubit modules (see Table M2). For modular
hyperbolic surface codes, our simulations demonstrate a
10×reduction in the number of physical qubits per log-
ical qubit needed to reach a logical error rate of 10 −10,
increasing to a 15×saving when extrapolating to larger
system sizes. While the modular hyperbolic surface codes
are not as dense as their color code counterparts, they
have the potential practical advantage of supporting fast
and accurate MWPM decoding. We find that our circuits
for both hyperbolic color and surface codes are highly tol-
erant to noisy inter-module gates, with even 10×higher
seam errors than bulk errors leading to only a modest
increase in logical error rate (see Figure M4).
III. LOGICAL CONTROL AND COMPUTATION
To operate on the logical information stored in the
modular hyperbolic memory we combine two ingredi-
ents: code surgery and code automorphisms. In code
surgery [42–47], a generalization of lattice surgery [48,
49], an ancilla system of data and measure qubits is cou-
pled to the memory code in order to measure its logical
operators. These measurements implement fault-tolerant
logic and allow logical information to be moved into and
out of the memory [19, 25]. Code automorphisms are
symmetries that permute the physical qubits while pre-
serving the codespace. Here they are global rotations
and translations of the hyperbolic tiling, and they allow
a single ancilla system to measure many different logical
operators.
Combining these two ingredients with low overhead re-
quires co-designing the logical basis of the code with both
the surgery ancilla system and the circuits that imple-
ment automorphisms. To teleport an individual logical
qubit into and out of the memory, we must be able to
measure single-qubit logical X i and Z i operators (see
Section M L). These single-qubit logical operators of the
code are derived from acanonical homology basisof the
underlying hyperbolic surface. On an orientable genus-
gsurface, a full canonical homology basis consists ofg
symplectic pairsof homologically non-trivial cycles that
cross each other an odd number of times (see Figure 4a
for one such pair), while cycles from different pairs cross
an even number of times. In a hyperbolic color code,
restricting a cycle to edges of one color defines a logical
operator, and two colors give four independent, commut-
ingXandZlogical operators per cycle. With anticom-
muting partners on the other cycle, each symplectic pair
encodes four logical qubits [37] (two for a hyperbolic sur-
face code), whose operators can be deformed onto module
boundaries. While it is known that a shortest homology
basis can be found in polynomial time [50], such a basis
does not ensure that cycles form symplectic pairs, nor
that they can be mapped onto each other via automor-
phisms. To satisfy these additional constraints, we enu-
merate low-weight homologically non-trivialseedcycles
and generate the orbit of each seed cycle under the ac-
tion of the automorphisms we can implement. We then

search forg ′ ≤gsymplectic pairs within the orbit of each
single seed loop (Methods). For the [[6400,104,30]] code
(g= 26) and the [[3600,104,22]] code derived from the
same tiling withL= 3, the orbit of the seed cycle in
Figure 4a containsg ′ = 25 symplectic pairs, covering 100
of the 104 logical qubits (Table M2).
We access this seed cycle by attaching a modularrib-
bon extractor, an extractor system [47] that we design
for boundary-connected modules. The name comes from
the fact that this system, which extracts logical informa-
tion from the code, is a closed loop of non-trivial width
attached to a cycle that wraps around the base code.
Here we focus on ribbon extractors for modular hyper-
bolic color codes with the quadrilateral module partition;
we expect the same methods to apply to other module
partitions and to modular hyperbolic surface codes. Like
the memory itself, the extractor is built from planar mod-
ules joined at their boundaries, and it attaches to the
memory along the cycle that supports the logical opera-
tors, using inter-module couplers at module boundaries
as in the rest of the architecture. A single extractor can
fault-tolerantly measure all 24 −1 = 15 non-trivial prod-
ucts of the four logical Pauli operators on the cycle it
is attached to. We prove (Supplementary Information)
that this construction preserves the full distanced. For
both the [[3600,104,22]] and [[6400,104,30]] codes, the
extractor is about 4.5×smaller than the memory itself.
To measure the other logical operators with the ribbon
extractor we implement code automorphisms using fault-
tolerantwalkingcircuits. Two rotationsρandσabout
neighboring vertices generate all orientation-preserving
rotations and translations of the tiling (Figure 4a). For
quadrilateral modules,ρandσeach map every module
onto another module with the same internal layout and
orientation. Since the modules are in general not mapped
to their neighbors in the code by these global rotations,
we add an additional inter-module seam of couplers from
the north boundary of each module to the south bound-
ary of its image inρ. AnO(L)-step walking circuit of
SWAP gates implements the move while all stabilizers
originally internal to a module continue to be measured
at every step, and simulations of a simplified model of
the resulting stabilizer dislocation suggest only a mod-
est increase in logical error rate (Methods). We im-
plement theσrotation in a similar manner, using the
east and west boundaries instead. For the larger octagon
modules, which would be internally rotated byρandσ,
we find that most tilings we consider admit an alterna-
tive, smaller group of symmetries that still moves every
module onto every other while preserving internal mod-
ule orientation, typically needing one additional seam on
each side of a module (Supplementary Information). This
smaller group requires two to four seed cycles rather than
one to cover the same logical basis, which can still be
measured by a single ribbon extractor by adding addi-
tional seams to its boundaries. Bringing any basis cycle

Logical Pauli operators are supported on the monochromatic substrings of a cycle, obtained by restricting it to edges of a single
color. Two of the three colors (e.g. blue and red) give four independent logical operators per cycle. Operators on the same
cycle commute, and anticommuting pairs are supported across the symplectic pair, so each pair defines four logical qubits. For
the [[6400,104,30]] code, the orbit of a single seed cycle under these rotations generates a symplectic basis for 100 of the 104
logical qubits. A single extractor then suffices for 100 qubits: it measures the operators on the seed cycle, and every other cycle
in the orbit is rotated onto it.b, A ribbon extractor attached to a non-trivial cycle of the memory code, shown in detail in
the inset. The extractor is itself boundary-connected and modular, containing ancilla qubits and ancilla checks. It couples to
the memory code modules only along the cycle, at the boundaries. It can fault-tolerantly measure all 2 4 −1 = 15 non-trivial
products of the four logical Pauli operators on the cycle, using roughly 22% additional physical qubits relative to the memory.
of the [[3600,104,22]] or [[6400,104,30]] code under the
ribbon extractor, starting from any other, takes a mean
of 4.4 and at most sixρ,ρ −1,σorσ −1 moves (Table M2),
with each move transporting four logical operators simul-
taneously.
Because every logical qubit in this canonical basis has
both its X i and Z i cycles in the orbit of the seed cycle,
any individual logical qubit can be selectively teleported
into and out of the hyperbolic memory by coupling the
ribbon extractor to a small compute region, such as a
Euclidean surface or color code, enabling universal com-
putation with low space overhead.
As a benchmark, we consider a widely used arithmetic
subroutine in quantum algorithms and compile a 50-bit
ripple-carry adder [51] onto the [[3600,104,22]] (L= 3)
and [[6400,104,30]] (L= 4) memories derived from the
same tiling, each using a single ribbon extractor to tele-
port operands into and out of a small compute region
(Methods). Since the symmetry group is non-Abelian,
the number of moves between two cycles depends on
where the extractor is attached (Methods). By choosing
the extractor location so that the two cycles of every sym-
plectic pair are only a single automorphism move apart,
and storing operands that are accessed in close succession
in the same symplectic pair, we bound the time for the
ribbon measurements and automorphisms by 21,402 and
28,930 QEC cycles respectively (428 and 579 per bit).
IV. DISCUSSION AND OUTLOOK
While partitioning quantum processors into manage-
able modules in solid state systems is known to be im-
portant for alleviating constraints on fabrication, yield
and coupler density, our architecture demonstrates this
modularity can also be exploited to deliver substantial
efficiency gains in quantum error correction. Realizing
these savings in a superconducting quantum computer
will require a fully integrated system that simultaneously
operates high-performance local grids of qubits [5] and
high-fidelity inter-module couplers [13–15]. These sys-
tems must also be resilient to fabrication defects, such
as broken qubits or couplers. However, we expect that
existing methods for handling fabrication defects in pla-
nar color codes [52] and surface codes [53–56] should be
naturally adaptable to modular hyperbolic codes, since
the local code structure is similar.
While our implementation of neural network and corre-
lated matching decoders demonstrates that efficient and
accurate decoding is possible, real-time decoding at scale
will require parallelization or specialized hardware im-
plementations. Since the full distance of a modular hy-
perbolic code is distributed across many modules, sub-
distance parallelization strategies will likely be necessary,
which generally requires parallelizing the decoding algo-
rithm itself [5, 57] rather than relying on parallel win-

[PDF page 7]
7
dowing [58, 59].
Utility-scale quantum computation requires the ability
to implement universal logic fault-tolerantly. We have
demonstrated that this is possible by providing the in-
gredients for a low-overhead quantum memory, using the
ribbon extractor for logical measurement along with cir-
cuits for implementing automorphisms. This architecture
is universal and a natural fit for serial quantum algo-
rithms and subroutines, such as addition circuits. How-
ever, we see several opportunities to improve on the cur-
rent design. First, our ribbon extractor does not fully
exploit the non-local connectivity available at module
boundaries. This leaves open the possibility of building
more space-efficient extractors that use this connectivity
[47]. Second, it would be desirable to design extractor
systems capable of measuring more complex high-weight
logical Pauli operators, either in serial or in parallel, in
order to implement certain subroutines such as QROM
lookup with lower time overhead. Finally, these logi-
cal primitives should be co-designed with compilations of
full quantum algorithms, building on our adder estimate
(Methods), to provide a complete resource estimation of
boundary-connected module architectures.
In summary, boundary-connected modules turn a con-
straint of scalable hardware into a resource for quan-
tum error correction. Our architecture builds hyper-
bolic codes from flat Euclidean modules, requiring con-
nectivity similar to that of current superconducting pro-
cessors designed for the surface code, with the hyper-
bolic geometry realized by the inter-module couplers at
their boundaries. Together with the modular syndrome
extraction circuits, decoders and logical measurement
methods we develop for this setting, this unlocks the ef-
ficiency of high-rate QLDPC codes on superconducting
devices. Therefore, boundary-connected modular archi-
tectures substantially lower the cost of reaching utility-
scale fault-tolerant quantum computation using near-
term hardware.

METHODS
A. Symmetry groups of hyperbolic tilings
Symmetry groups of hyperbolic tilings are used
throughout this work for constructing the tilings, parti-
tioning codes, finding logical bases and implementing au-
tomorphisms. In this section, we introduce the relevant
terminology and explain how the tilings are constructed.
For a more comprehensive overview, see the Supplemen-
tary Information as well as Refs. [17, 20, 60, 61].

For the modular hyperbolic surface codes we use
quadrilateral tilings, for which each face is instead a
quadrilateral. We construct many of these usingquadri-
lateralgroups□(r, s, t, u), which are instead generated
by reflections across the sides of a hyperbolic quadrilat-
eral, see Section S1 for details. These include non-regular
quadrilateral tilings with face configurationV2r.2s.2t.2u,
substantially enlarging the space of tilings beyond the
regular{p, q}tilings used in previous hyperbolic surface
code constructions [17, 19]. In particular, the big square
module partition described below requires each tile to
have a corner at which four quadrilaterals meet, which is
impossible for a regular hyperbolic{4, q}tiling (see Ta-
bles S11, S15 and S16 for examples of codes constructed
from these tilings).
B. Modular hyperbolic color codes
Our modular hyperbolic color codes are constructed
by fine-graining a triangular hyperbolic tilingT △ (Sec-
tion M A) and replacing each triangular face with a Eu-
clidean hexagonal lattice of side lengthL. This family of
tilings was used to construct Floquet codes in [20], but
has not previously been studied for constructing color
codes. We associate a data qubit with each vertex and
define anXandZstabilizer on each face of the tiling,
see Supplementary Information for more details on the
code construction.
Table M1 summarizes the different module layouts for
modular hyperbolic codes, giving the total number of
qubits per module as a function of the fine-graining pa-
rameter (side-length)Lfor each module type. The color
code modules partition the triangular tiles of the base tri-
angular tiling (T △) into triangle (K= 1), quadrilateral
(K= 2) or octagon (K= 8) modules. Triangular mod-
ules are formed from a single triangle of the base tiling
T △, in bijection with the elements ofG(r, s, t). Each
quadrilateral module is a pair of triangles sharing an edge
of a chosen color (e.g. green, the images ofbmirrors; the
other two choices are analogous), each labeled by a left
cosetg⟨b⟩(whereg∈G(r, s, t)) or equivalently by an ele-
ment ofG +(r, s, t). Each octagon module is formed from
8 triangles at a vertex type (e.g. meeting at vertexb∩c,
labeled by a left cosetg⟨b, c⟩forg∈G(r, s, t)).

C. Modular Hyperbolic Surface Codes
Our modular hyperbolic surface codes are derived from
tilings of closed hyperbolic surfaces by quadrilateral faces
(Figure M2a). We fine-grain each face of the quadrilat-
eral tilingT □ with anL×LEuclidean square grid to form
a semi-hyperbolic tiling. Rather than placing qubits on
the edges of the tiling, as done in [19], we instead take its
dual tiling, bicolor the faces red and blue and associate
a data qubit with each vertex, anXstabilizer with each
red face and aZstabilizer with each blue face. This
corresponds to replacing each quadrilateral face ofT □
with a patch of square stabilizers resembling a rotated
surface code in the bulk, so our construction could be
considered a “rotated” analog of [19]. Stabilizers in the
bulk of a tile then have weight four, locally identical to
a planar surface code, while those at the corners of the
tiles inherit the higher weight set by the vertex degree of
FIG. M2. Construction of modular hyperbolic surface codes.
a, A modular hyperbolic surface code derived from a quadri-
lateral tiling, in which six squares meet at each vertex (a
regular{4,6}tiling), shown in the Poincar´ e disk model. The
code is partitioned into modules, each derived from a single
square of the base quadrilateral tiling; two neighboring mod-
ules are outlined with dashed green and blue lines.b, The
two outlined modules ofashown in detail.
T □, which is six for the{4,6}tiling shown in Figure M2.
Compared with Ref. [19], where all high-weight corner
stabilizers have the same Pauli type, for oddLour con-
struction distributes them betweenXandZtypes (see
Section S11 A), and since no data qubits lie on the edges
of the base quadrilaterals, the tiles can be grouped into
uniform modules.
We partition the code into modules by grouping con-
tiguous quadrilateral tiles: a single tile gives asquare
module (K= 1), each labeled by an element of
G(r, s, t, u) :=□(r, s, t, u)/Γ. Arectanglemodule (K=
2) is formed from a pair of quadrilateral tiles sharing an
edge (labeled by an element of the orientation-preserving
subgroupG +(r, s, t, u)≤G(r, s, t, u)). If the quadrilat-
eral tiling has face configurationV a.b.c.dwith at least
one ofa, b, c, dequal to 4, i.e. each quadrilateral tile has
at least one corner at which four tiles meet, then we
can form abig squaremodule (K= 4) by choosing one
such corner type and combining the four quadrilaterals
that meet at each vertex of that type inT □ (labeled
by a left coset of the corresponding vertex stabilizer in
G(r, s, t, u)). See Table M1 for diagrams of the modules,
as well as the sizes of the modules. Qubits within a mod-
ule are coupled by nearest-neighbor gates on a 2D grid,
and only the stabilizers straddling tile boundaries require
inter-module couplers (Figure M2b).

E. Distance finding
Distances of the hyperbolic color codes and surface
codes are computed by finding the minimum length ho-
mologically non-trivial cycle (combinatorial systole) of
certain tilings associated with the codes. For the color
codes, our string-like distance upper boundd string ≥d
is computed as twice the length of the smallest systole
among the three shrunk lattices [37]. In Section S10
we prove that a lower bound on the distance is given
byd res := median
 

C is the
systole of the restricted lattice [64, 65] of colorC∈
{R, G, B}. Finally, theXandZdistances of the hyper-
bolic surface codes are computed exactly as the systole of
a cell complex associated with the tiling. All systoles are
computed using a tree-cotree decomposition [66], acceler-
ated by restricting the source nodes to a single fundamen-
tal domain (tile) of the tiling, which retains full accuracy
due to the symmetries of the tiling. In Section S10 we
explain our methods in more detail and show that our
distance finding methods empirically scale linearly with
code size and are orders of magnitude faster than alterna-
tive available methods for codes of sizen >10 3–104. Fur-

F. Modular Syndrome Extraction Circuits
We design syndrome extraction circuits compatible
with the planar module layout. In the bulk of each mod-
ule, stabilizers have weight four for surface codes and
weight six for color codes. We add one measure qubit
per bulk face for surface codes and two measure qubits
per bulk face for color codes, with both cases giving a
planar layout in which every qubit couples to at most
four nearest neighbors on a 2D Euclidean grid. At the
edges of modules, stabilizers span two modules; at the
corners, a weight-wstabilizer spansmmodules with one
measure qubit per module, forming a linear chain ofm
measure qubits. We place all measure qubits using a
graph flooding algorithm that preserves this layout.
To measure the higher-weight corner stabilizers while
respecting the planar module layout and existing inter-
module connectivity, we develop cat-flagged circuits.
We prepare a cat state (a Greenberger–Horne–Zeilinger
state) across the linear chain using the inter-module links,
Ideal operation Noisy implementation

CZ, inter-module DEP 2(pseam)·CZ
Single-qubit gate or idle DEP 1(p/10)·U
Reset,RXERR(2p)·R
Measurement,MDEP 1(p)·MERR(5p)
Idle duringM,RDEP 1(2p)
TABLE M4. The superconducting-inspired modular SI1000
noise model, where inter-module CZ gates carry an indepen-
dent error ratep seam; settingp seam =precovers standard
SI1000. Here DEP 1(q) and DEP 2(q) are one- and two-qubit
depolarizing channels of strengthq, XERR(q) appliesXwith
probabilityq, and MERR(q) flips the reported measurement
outcome with probabilityq. The single parameterpsets the
two-qubit gate error rate, with all other rates defined relative
to it.
couple each measure qubit to its local data qubits to ex-
tract the stabilizer, and measure the cat state. The linear
chain gives a cat-state preparation depth ofO(m).
For a particular stabilizer on a face, one measure qubit
reads out the syndrome and the remainingm−1 act as
flag qubits, which can detect hook errors. Figure M3
shows a weight-eight corner stabilizer spanning four mod-
ules with four measure qubits. For surface codes, we
interleaveXandZstabilizer extraction, ensuring that
their interactions with shared data qubits are consistently
ordered. We find such a schedule by solving a constraint
satisfaction problem on the base tiling, explained in Sup-
plementary Information. For color codes,XandZsta-
bilizers are extracted sequentially. We schedule each ex-
traction stage using a translationally invariant plaquette
schedule, assigning the same up-middle-down ordering to
every bulk plaquette within a module.
All stabilizers can be measured every cycle in the
uniform-rate (measurement-aligned) variant of our cir-
cuits. Inter-module stabilizers require additional time
for cat or Bell state preparation and measurement. In
the multi-rate variant, we spread their preparation, ex-
traction and measurement across three consecutive cy-
cles, interleaving them with bulk stabilizer extraction so
that the noisy inter-module links are used less frequently.
We find that the multi-rate circuits have improved log-
ical error rates relative to the uniform-rate circuits, see
Section S4 for more details.
We benchmark these circuits under the modular
SI1000 noise model of Table M4. Circuits are first tran-
spiled into a gate set of CZ gates, single-qubit rotations,
Z-basis resets andZ-basis measurements, and noise is
applied to the transpiled circuit. Every CZ gate acting
between qubits in different modules is assigned the el-
evated error ratep seam, while all other operations take
their bulk rates regardless of their position relative to a
module boundary.
Figure M4 shows the logical error rate aspseam is swept
from 0.1% to 2% with the bulk error rate fixed atp=
0.1%. For modular hyperbolic codes across all module
partitions and all module sizes, the logical error rate is
only weakly sensitive top seam. This tolerance follows
from boundary connectivity, since only the small fraction
of gates that cross a module boundary is affected by the
elevated error rates.
G. Neural Network Decoding for Hyperbolic Color
Codes
AlphaQubit (AQ) is a family of recurrent, attention-
based decoders, mapping the error correction syndrome
into a predicted probability that each logical observable
is flipped [22, 23]. We use here a variant that differs from
what was previously published [23] in several regards.
The first difference is that the network is trained to
produce several binary classification predictions, one per
logical measurement in the circuit. We train each of them

FIG. M4. Logical error rate as a function of the inter-module error ratep seam, swept from 0.1% to 2% with the bulk error rate
fixed atp= 0.1%.a, Modular hyperbolic color codes withL= 2 octagon modules.b, Modular hyperbolic color codes with
quadrilateral modules atL= 2,3,4.c, Modular hyperbolic surface codes derived from two different tilings.
using a binary cross-entropy loss to independently predict
each observable.
The second difference is that, rather than performing
attention across all the stabilizer measurements, we can
perform it across all triggered stabilizers (events) in a
time window. Because the circuits have a few rounds
only (16 rounds), here the time window corresponds to
the whole experiment.
The third major difference is the auxiliary targets we
use during training. Instead of simulating stopping the
experiment at every point in time [23], we adapt an
approach similar to [69]. In the detector error model,
the prediction for each of thekobservables results from
XOR’ing together the predictions for several error mecha-
nisms. By assigning a time to each error mechanism (the
time of the first event that it triggers), we can partition
the error mechanisms that can flip each observable. By
XOR’ing the errors in each partition, we produceT×k
auxiliary binary targets forTrounds. Predicting the aux-
iliary targets during training via auxiliary heads both im-
proves training time and performance, even though those
labels are not fault-tolerant observables in isolation.
Another difference is our use of graph-derived space-
time coordinates (combining the round index with scaled
spatial coordinates) for both input features and rotary
position embeddings [70]. Unlike Poincar´ e projections,
which introduce metric distortions and artificial bound-
ary cuts, our spatial coordinates are obtained from a
spectral embedding of the single-round detector error
model hypergraph (using the leading non-trivial eigen-
vectors of a normalized diffusion operator), so that de-
tectors that frequently co-occur in the noise model are
placed close together in Euclidean space.
The resulting AQ network has around 55 million pa-
rameters. We train a different instance per circuit, using
up to 3 billion simulated examples, from a mixture of
SI1000 noise levels. After training, AQ can decode each
16-round example (wall time for the forward pass, on a
single v6e Trillium TPU) from 140µs to 6000µs, de-
pending on the size of the circuit.
H. Correlated Matching Decoding for Hyperbolic
Surface Codes
For the hyperbolic surface codes, we use a variant
of the correlated sparse blossom decoder in pymatch-
ing [24, 71]. We use three passes, finding that this slightly
improves accuracy relative to two-pass matching.
We use a custom decomposition of the detector er-
ror model by classifying the detectors in the circuit into
four classes:Xflags,Xstabilizers,Zflags andZsta-
bilizers. TheX(Z) stabilizer detectors are the ones
formed from consecutive measurements ofX(Z) sta-
bilizers in the circuit. The flag detectors are formed
from single measurements of qubits in the cat (or Bell)
states that do not correspond to measurements of the
code’s stabilizers. For example, when measuring anX
stabilizer using ann-qubitXcat state (stabilized by
⟨X ⊗n, Z0Z1, Z1Z2, . . . , Zn−2Zn−1⟩), the cat state is un-
encoded into a product state followed by single-qubit
measurements, of which one is anXstabilizer measure-
ment and the remainingn−1 areZflag measurements.
Given these four detector classes, we decompose the
detector error model (DEM) so that each component of
a decomposed error triggers only detectors from a single
class. Concretely, for each error mechanismein the DEM
that triggers detectors belonging to more than one class,
we decomposeeinto componentse=e 1 ⊕e2 ⊕ · · ·, where
eache i triggers detectors from only one of the four classes
{X-stab, Z-stab, X-flag, Z-flag}. Each componente i is
required to also appear as an independent error elsewhere
in the DEM, which determines its detector and observ-
able signature. The observable assignments are chosen so
that the symmetric difference of the observables across
all components matches the observables of the original
undecomposed error.
This decomposition is finer than the standard two-class

[PDF page 13]
13
basis decomposition used for planar surface codes (which
separates detectors only intoX-type andZ-type). The
finer four-class decomposition is necessary to ensure that
each component of a decomposed error is matchable, i.e.
that it triggers at most two detectors of its class. In
each basis, a DEM error (or error component) is guaran-
teed to trigger two stabilizer detectors (since locally the
code resembles a standard Euclidean surface code) and
either one or two flag detectors (as shown by at most
two flag detecting regions being supported on each qubit
in Figure S19). Without separating flag detectors from
stabilizer detectors, a single component could trigger, for
example, twoXstabilizer detectors together with one or
twoXflag detectors, giving a component with three or
four detectors that cannot be represented as an edge in
the matching graph.
The decomposed DEM is decoded using the correlated
minimum-weight perfect matching decoder in PyMatch-
ing [24], which builds a single matching graph from the
full decomposed DEM and accounts for correlations be-
tween the components of decomposed errors when com-
puting the minimum-weight matching. We run the de-
coder for three passes, following the iterative re-weighting
approach of Ref. [71]: after each pass, the edge weights
in the matching graph are updated based on the errors
identified in the previous pass, and the matching is re-
computed. We find that three passes slightly improves
accuracy relative to two passes, with diminishing returns
beyond three.
I. Simulation methodology
All logical error rates are estimated by Monte Carlo
sampling of separateXandZbasis memory experiments
under the modular SI1000 noise model (Table M4) with
p= 0.1% and, unless stated otherwise,p seam = 1%, us-
ing Stim [72]. From the shot failure probabilityP L we
report the logical error rate per logical qubit per round,
ϵ= 1−(1−P L)1/(kr), which is≈P L/(kr) in the low-error
regimeP L ≪1 relevant here, and report 95% Clopper–
Pearson confidence intervals. Surface codes (hyperbolic
and Euclidean) user= 30 and the correlated match-
ing decoder (Section M H). Hyperbolic color codes use
r= 16 and AlphaQubit (Section M G). While the num-
ber of rounds differs between code families, we do not
expect this to impact our comparisons, since we normal-
ize the logical error rate by the number of rounds. For
the hyperbolic surface codes in Figure 3 we use multi-
rate circuits, alternate the extraction order forward and
backward between rounds, and use a ZZ schedule within
each module (see Section M F and Section S4 B).
We compare to rotated surface codes of odd distance
3≤d≤25 using a standard NZ schedule. Each code is
partitioned into modules of 7×7 data qubits (98 qubits
per module), every inter-module CZ gate is assigned
pseam, and decoding uses the same correlated matching
decoder. For simplicity, the simulated Euclidean circuits
measure each stabilizer cut by a module boundary with
a single measure qubit, coupled to the data qubits in
neighboring modules by inter-module CZ gates, rather
than using the cat-state and flag construction of our hy-
perbolic circuits. A modular partition of the code, how-
ever, needs a measure qubit in every module the stabilizer
touches, for example to prepare the Bell pairs that me-
diate the inter-module gates, so to compare qubit counts
fairly we count one extra measure qubit for each addi-
tional module touched by a cut stabilizer. This gives
2d2−1+2wd+w 2 qubits, withL= 7 andw=⌊(d−1)/L⌋
cuts in each direction: each cut is crossed bydstabiliz-
ers, and the stabilizer at each of thew 2 cut intersections
touches four modules. The hyperbolic circuits include
the corresponding qubits explicitly.
The overhead of a code is its total number of physical
qubits (including ancilla qubits) divided byk. For a sim-
ulated hyperbolic code, we compute the density improve-
ment against the largest-distance surface code whoseϵis
no lower than that of the hyperbolic code (the improve-
ment is that surface code’s overhead divided by the hy-
perbolic code’s overhead). Hollow symbols mark codes
that are too large to simulate or decode. We plot these
at the measuredϵof the Euclidean surface code with the
largest odd distance not exceedingd. This extrapolation
is supported by the fact that every simulated hyperbolic
code except the smallest, [[64,8,6]], reaches a lowerϵ
than the odd-distance Euclidean surface code at distance
dord−1. For the color codes,dis the stringlike upper
bound of Section S10, which we expect to be tight.
J. Fault-Tolerant Automorphism Gates
A code automorphism is a permutation of the physical
qubits that preserves the stabilizer group, while imple-
menting a (potentially nontrivial) logical operation [73–
77]. For hyperbolic codes, the code automorphisms are
derived from the symmetries of the hyperbolic tiling. In
this section we explain how certain code automorphisms
can be implemented usingwalking circuits.
Let’s consider modular hyperbolic color codes with a
quadrilateral module partition as an example, for which
the modules are in bijection with the elements ofG +.
Left multiplication by an elementh∈G + defines a rigid
motion (isometry) of the tiling, mapping modulegto
hg(see Figure M5). We define a consistent internal co-
ordinate system of each module, such that (g, x, y) de-
notes a qubit at coordinate (x, y) in moduleg. Notice
that any automorphismh∈G + permutes the modules
while preserving their internal coordinate system, map-
ping (g, x, y)→(hg, x, y). We can implement such an
automorphism by using inter-module couplers to connect
the north boundary of modulegto the south boundary of
modulehg, using a walking circuit (consisting of SWAP
gates) to map the quantum information in modulegto
modulehg.
We can similarly implement an arbitrary automor-

[PDF page 14]
14
a b
FIG. M5. Modules and inter-module connectivity as group
elements and action.a, The quadrilateral modules are in bi-
jection with the elements of the group generated by the two
rotationsρ(green) andσ(purple), shown here in the Poincar´ e
disk with each module labeled by its group element. Taking
one module as the identitye, every other module is labeled by
a sequence of rotations mappingeonto it.b, The two kinds
of inter-module connection required at a moduleg. Right
multiplication (blue) gives the four modules adjacent togin
the tiling, whose shared boundaries carry the couplers used
to measure stabilizers spanning two modules. Left multipli-
cation (red) gives the modules thatgis mapped onto by the
rotations, whose shared boundaries carry the couplers used
by the walking circuits that implement the automorphisms.
FIG. M6. A permutation of the modules is implemented using
walking circuits and additional seams on opposing boundaries,
shown here for a single moduleg. Under the global rotation
ρ, modulegis mapped ontoρg: the north seam ofgconnects
to the south seam ofρg. The east and west seams implement
σin the same way, and the two rotations together generate
the full orientation-preserving symmetry group. Grey arrows
mark the four modules reached by left multiplication.
phism inG + by adding a second seam to the east and
west boundaries of each module. SinceG + is generated
by the two elementsρ :=abandσ :=bc, we add addi-
tional couplers at the north and south boundaries to im-
plement the automorphismg→ρgand use the east and
west boundaries to implementg→σg, see Figure M6.
With these additional couplers we can use walking cir-
cuits to implementanyautomorphism inG + using a se-
quence of automorphisms in{σ, σ −1, ρ, ρ−1}. Since the
existing seams required for code connectivity (stabilizer
measurement) correspond to right multiplication byρ
andσ, the overall inter-module connectivity corresponds
to a left-right Cayley graph ofG +, see Figure M5.
Implementing a single automorphismρorσ(an auto-
morphismmove) with a walking circuit takesO(L) steps,
during which only the stabilizers originally fully internal
to a module can be repeatedly measured. The stabilizers
originally on the boundary of the module become dislo-
cated until the move is completed, after which they can
be measured again. We describe the automorphism walk-
ing circuits in more detail in Section S7. In Section S7 C
we also simulate a simplified model in which the stabi-
lizers on module boundaries are not measured for several
rounds, providing evidence that this stabilizer dislocation
causes only a modest increase in logical error rates.
For the larger octagon color code modules, each con-
taining eight triangles, this construction requires modi-
fication. The octagon modules are in bijection with the
left cosetsG/D={gD|g∈G}, whereD :=⟨b, c⟩ ≤G
is a dihedral subgroup of order eight,|D|= 8. The auto-
morphismσ=bc∈G + would rotate the identity module
⟨b, c⟩rather than leaving the internal coordinates fixed,
which is not compatible with our walking circuits using
fixed inter-module couplers. We resolve this by finding
acomplementΛ≤GofD≤G, which by definition
satisfies
ΛD=G,Λ∩D={e}.(M3)
Each elementg∈Gcan then be factorized asg=λd,
whereλ∈Λ labels the octagon module that triangleg
belongs to andd∈Dis theslotofgin moduleλ∈Λ.
We refer to the identity slote∈Din each module as
itsframe, which is used to define a fixed, consistent in-
ternal coordinate system (x, y) for each moduleλ∈Λ.
Left multiplication by any elementλ ′ ∈Λ then maps
(λd, x, y)→(λ ′λd, x, y), corresponding to an automor-
phism that permutes modules while leaving the inter-
nal coordinate system fixed, as desired. Using additional
seams this can be implemented using walking circuits in
a similar way to how we described for quadrilateral mod-
ules.
It is nota prioriobvious whether a complement Λ≤G
ofD≤Gexists for a given tilingT △. Additionally, it
is desirable for Λ to be two-generated, such that only
one additional seam of inter-module couplers is required
on each of the four boundaries of the module. In Sec-
tion S7 E we provide an efficient method to enumerate
the complements Λ ofDand find that complements do
exist for many of the tilings in Table M2, including those
with symmetry group△(4,4,4) i fori∈ {32,40,58,109},
but not for△(4,4,4) 96 (Table S4). Moreover, for each
of these four tilings, we find a complement requiring at
most two generators.
K. Low-weight Symmetrical Logical Basis
Logical operators are associated with homologically
non-trivial cycles of the tiling, and identifying acanonical

[PDF page 15]
15
logical basisreduces to finding acanonical homology ba-
sisfor the underlying hyperbolic tiling. Two non-trivial
cycles form a symplectic pair if they cross each other an
odd number of times. Such a pair supports two logical
qubits for surface codes and four for color codes. A com-
plete canonical homology basis consists ofgsymplectic
pairs, where cycles within each pair cross an odd number
of times and cycles belonging to distinct pairs cross an
even number of times. This givesk= 2glogical qubits
for surface codes andk= 4gfor color codes, where each
cycle additionally supports operators of two independent
colors. Shorter cycles give lower-weight logical opera-
tors, enabling more efficient Pauli measurements. Find-
ing a shortest homology basis is tractable in polynomial
time [50], and any homology basis can be brought into
canonical form by Gaussian elimination overF 2 also in
polynomial time. However, Gaussian elimination com-
bines cycles, which may increase their length and gener-
ally yields cycles that are no longer related to one another
by code automorphisms.
We develop algorithms that address this by exploit-
ing the symmetry structure of the tiling. We enumerate
short homologically non-trivial cycles from every vertex
by breadth-first search, retaining the shortest representa-
tive per homology class. One or two of the shortest cycles
are selected as seed cycles. Their full orbit underG + is
generated, producing a candidate set of cycles related by
the tiling symmetries. We then identify mutually com-
patible symplectic pairs by a backtracking search on the
candidate set. For most hyperbolic tilings we consider,
one or two seed cycles suffice to generate a complete or
near-complete basis yielding logical operators at or near
minimum weight (see Table M2). The resulting basis is
lifted from the hyperbolic tiling to the semi-hyperbolic
tiling to obtain low-weight logical operators compatible
with the module partition. Full details are in the Sup-
plementary Information.
Since logical operators in the basis are related byρ
andσ, a single extractor system [47] suffices per seed
cycle. Applying walking circuits rotates between all log-
ical operators in the orbit of that seed. Since the length
of a homologically non-trivial cycle on a hyperbolic sur-
face grows logarithmically with its area, each extractor
system couples to only a logarithmic fraction of the to-
tal modules, provided the cycles in the basis are close to
minimum length. With one or two seed cycles, a constant
number of extractor systems, each coupled toO(logM)
of theMmodules, is therefore sufficient to access all log-
ical operators. If the extractor is attached to the cycle
hγ, whereγis the seed cycle andh∈G +, then the cy-
clexγis brought under it by the automorphismhx −1,
so switching from measuringxγto measuringyγtakes
|h x−1y h−1|moves, where| · |is the word length in the
generators{ρ ±1, σ±1}. Forh=ethis is the distance be-
tweenxandyin the right Cayley graph ofG +, rather
than the left Cayley distance|yx −1|(the length of the
automorphism that mapsxγontoyγ). SinceG + is in
general non-Abelian, the choice ofhaffects the number
of moves and can be optimized (Section S8). For the
[[6400,104,30]] code, bringing any basis cycle under the
extractor, starting from any other, takes a mean of 4.4
and at most six moves (Table M2).
L. Logical Measurement
Given a choice of seed cycle, we can build an ancillary
extractor system to measure out the set of non-trivial log-
ical Pauli operators associated with the given seed cycle.
One important fact is that for any logical operator be-
longing to a given seed cycle in a semi-hyperbolic surface
or color code tiling, we can always choose a represen-
tative that is only supported on the data qubits at the
boundary of individual modules forming the hyperbolic
code, see Figure 4b for an example.
We will construct an extractor system that will be itself
formed of a collection of modules that will be connected
through their respective boundary links. The extractor
will then couple to the base code along the boundaries
of the modules that support the logical operators corre-
sponding to the chosen seed cycle. This coupling to the
base code will use its own set of boundary links, and lo-
cally will have many of the features that are common to
lattice surgery, see Figure 4b and Section S6 for further
details. In particular, there will be a new set of stabiliz-
ers that will have to be measured across qubits belonging
to the measurement ancilla and base code, in a similar
fashion to those measured across two different tiles in the
base semi-hyperbolic code.
Overall, the simplest such extractor will be topologi-
cally equivalent to a long ribbon, whose base connects
to the base code in the aforementioned way. The rib-
bon extractor itself will be equivalent to a long strip of a
closed surface code, built from a linear chain of connected
modules, such that the product of all of the stabilizers
of one type will determine the value of the desired logi-
cal measurement [42]. Such an extractor would be able
to measure any logical operator that is supported on the
seed cycle, which for the color code tilings would be any
of the 15 non-trivial logical Pauli representatives.
To quantify the physical overhead of the ribbon ex-
tractor, we detail an explicit construction for a tiling
with side lengthL= 4 for the [[6400,104,30]] hyperbolic
color code derived from aV8.8.8 tiling, as given explic-
itly in Figure 4b and detailed in Section S6 why such
a ribbon extractor is distance-preserving. Each extrac-
tor module is built in layers, where the first layer will
have 4 ancilla (data) qubits and 6 ancilla check qubits
and the second layer will have 6 qubits of each. In or-
der for the extractor to be provably distance preserving,
we used/2 odd layers andd/2−1 even layers (this is
a well-studied approach called thickening [42, 44, 47]),
thus totaling 10d/2 + 12(d/2−1) = 11d−12 qubits per
extractor module. There will be one such extractor mod-
ule for each tile boundary that is a part of the seed loop,
which in the case of the given lattice is 10 such mod-

[PDF page 16]
16
ules. This gives an overhead of 3180 qubits (both ancilla
data and check qubits) for the ribbon extractor, which
results in about a 22% overhead as the number of phys-
ical (data and measurement) qubits in the base code is
14400, see Table S9. ChoosingL= 3 instead, the same
seed cycle gives the [[3600,104,22]] code with 8400 phys-
ical qubits (Table S8), for which the ribbon extractor re-
quires 1880 qubits, about 22% of the memory. Using the
L= 2 octagon partition of this tiling ([[1600,104,14–16]],
3600 physical qubits), the ribbon extractor would require
1040 qubits (about 29% of the memory). Here the smaller
group of implementable automorphisms splits the same
basis into four seed cycles rather than one, but since each
extractor module uses only one seam of inter-module cou-
plers to attach to the memory, a single extractor can serve
all four by adding additional seams on the sides of the
ribbon, one to each seed cycle (Section S7 E). Since the
complement is smaller thanG + (50 rather than 200 ele-
ments), bringing any basis cycle under the extractor then
takes a mean of 2.9 and at most four moves (Table S4).
It should be noted that this is a rather overly-cautious
bound on the physical overhead, as the number of layers
of thickening is taken to bed/2 which is in all likeli-
hood superfluous for the purpose of a rigorous distance
proof. Namely, if we have some guarantee of the overlap
of other logical operators with the target logical operator
being measured, then this overhead could be significantly
reduced.
Finally, in addition to measuring the aforementioned
logical qubits, the ribbon extractor can be used to fa-
cilitate the joint measurement of a logical qubit from
the base code and another code patch [43]. This is
particularly useful if one were to treat the hyperbolic
code as a form of logical memory and have some other
code, say a surface code, that would be used as a com-
pute region. The ribbon extractor would be used to
perform joint logical measurements of the form X mX c
and Z mZ c, where P m and P c are logical Pauli oper-
ators for the memory and compute codes, respectively.
The ribbon extractor would achieve this by coupling to
the base hyperbolic code as discussed while additionally
coupling its upper boundary to the compute code, for
example a surface code, thereby jointly measuring the
logical operators across both codes. In order to com-
plete both of these measurements, one would perform an
automorphism gate between measurements to rotate the
appropriate conjugate basis element from the hyperbolic
code to facilitate this joint measurement between code
blocks. Together, these joint logical Pauli measurements
enable fault-tolerant logical state and gate teleportation
between the memory and compute blocks [78]. Univer-
sal quantum circuits can then be compiled and executed
via Pauli-based computation [79, 80], consuming logical
magic states supplied to the compute region via magic
state distillation [81, 82] or cultivation [83].
M. Compiling an adder onto the hyperbolic
memory
We compile then-bit CDKM ripple-carry adder [51]
onto the [[3600,104,22]] (L= 3) and [[6400,104,30]]
(L= 4) modular hyperbolic color codes derived from
the△(4,4,4) 109 8.8.8 tiling, storing theaandbregisters
in the memory and applying all gates in a compute re-
gion withCoperand slots. Qubits are teleported between
the two using the ribbon extractor (Section M L): a load
uses the measurementsM X qX c
andM Zq
, and a store uses
MX qX c
. Retaining operands in the compute region be-
tween blocks, the adder requiresN R = 3(4n−C) ribbon
measurements, and automorphism moves (Section M J)
bring each logical operator under the extractor. With
αdcycles per ribbon measurement andκLper move,
this takesT=αdN R +κLN A cycles, whereN A is the
number of moves. Every symplectic pair in our basis is
related by the same automorphism, so with an appro-
priate extractor location, switching between the two cy-
cles of a pair takes a single move. Performing the two
measurements of each load consecutively, and using at
mostD= 6 moves (the diameter of the Cayley graph of
G+ with generators{ρ ±1, σ±1}) for every other measure-
ment, givesN A ≤(2D+1)(4n−C). We further store the
four operandsa 2i, b2i, a2i+1, b2i+1 in the same symplectic
pair (α i, βi), with each X q onα i and each Z q onβ i.
WithC= 3, the loads and stores before each block then
act on at most two pairs, each costing at most one jump
of≤Dmoves and one switch, givingN A ≤4(D+ 1)n
withN R = 3(4n−3). For a 50-bit adder withα= 1
andκ= 2, this givesT≤21,402 cycles (428 per bit) at
L= 3 andT≤28,930 cycles (579 per bit) atL= 4,
compared with 28,080 and 37,830 cycles without group-
ing (C= 5), and 33,930 and 45,630 cycles for the coarse
boundN A ≤DN R. The compute-region logic can largely
run in parallel with the extractor measurements and au-
tomorphisms. See Section S8 for details.

and the Euclidean surface code circuits used for com-

qubit layouts, the hyperbolic surface code circuits and
the distance-finding methods, adapted correlated match-
ing to the hyperbolic surface codes, designed the auto-
morphism walking circuits, performed the final numerical
simulations, and compiled the ripple-carry adder. Z.H.
and T.J.-O., with support from O.H., designed the rib-
bon extractor and proved that it preserves the code dis-

S1. HYPERBOLIC TILINGS AND SYMMETRY GROUPS
We detail the construction of hyperbolic and semi-hyperbolic codes derived from closed hyperbolic tilings. Through-
out this work, the symmetry groups of these tilings play a central role. Beyond code construction, they guide module
partitioning, inter-module connectivity, and the implementation of fault-tolerant logical gates.
A tiling of the hyperbolic planeH 2 is a covering of the space by closed polygonal faces that intersect only along
their edges. Due to the plane’s constant negative curvature, the interior angles of a hyperbolic polygon are smaller
than those of its Euclidean counterpart, permitting vertex configurations that cannot be realized on flat surfaces.
Hyperbolic tilings can be generated from subgroups of the isometry group ofH 2. Such a group acts on the plane
with afundamental domain, a closed region whose images under the group cover the plane and overlap only along
their boundaries. The tiling is the orbit of this domain under the group, with each tile a distinct image of it, so that
the tiles are in bijection with the group elements and the combinatorial structure of the tiling is determined by the
structure of the group.
The tilings we consider arise fromCoxeter groupsacting on a space of constant negative curvature. Their generators
are order-two reflections across the edges of aCoxeter polygon. This polygon serves as a fundamental domain whose
interior angles are of the formπ/n i for integersn i ≥2. The geometry completely determines the group’s relations,
since composing reflections across two mirrors intersecting at angleπ/n i yields a rotation of ordern i. In this work,
we focus on two of the simplest cases of these structures:triangle groupsandquadrilateral groups.
A. Triangle Groups
A hyperbolic triangle group, denoted△(r, s, t), is generated by reflectionsa, b, cin the three sides of a hyperbolic
triangle with interior anglesπ/r, π/s, π/tsatisfying 1/r+ 1/s+ 1/t <1. Taking this triangle as the fundamental
domain and repeatedly reflecting it across its edges produces an infinite tiling (see Figure S1). In the resulting tiling,
2r, 2sand 2ttriangles meet at the respective vertices, and each triangle copy corresponds uniquely to a group element
of△(r, s, t). The group presentation is given by:
△(r, s, t) =

a, b, c
 a2 =b 2 =c 2 = (ab)r = (bc)s = (ca)t =e

,(S1)
wherea,b,care reflections in the three edges of the triangle andeis the identity element. Composing two adjacent
reflections yields a rotation about their shared vertex. This defines the corner rotationsρ=ab,σ=bc,τ=caby
angles 2π/r, 2π/s, 2π/trespectively. The reflectionsa,b,care orientation-reversing and the rotationsρ,σ,τare
orientation-preserving, therefore△(r, s, t) contains both orientation-preserving and orientation-reversing isometries.
The orientation-preserving subgroup, denoted△ +(r, s, t), is an index-2 subgroup generated by the corner rotations.
Sinceρστ= (ab)(bc)(ca) =e, the third rotation is redundant,τ= (ρσ) −1, and two generators suffice:
△+(r, s, t) =

.(S2)
Geometrically,△ +(r, s, t) acts on a fundamental domain consisting of two reflected copies of the original triangle
glued along a shared edge, so that each of its elements is a word of even length ina, b, c(see Figure S7).
i. Wythoff Construction
Uniform tilings ofH 2 can be generated from a triangle group by theWythoff kaleidoscopic construction. A uniform
tiling can be described by its vertex configurationp1.p2 . . . pk, which specifies the sequence ofpi-sided polygons meeting
in cyclic order around every vertex. Alternatively, for regular tilings composed of a single polygon type withqregular
p-gons meeting at every vertex, we can denote them more compactly by the Schl¨ afli symbol{p, q}.
The Wythoff construction proceeds by placing a vertex inside the fundamental triangle, called thegenerator point,
and drawing a perpendicular line from it to each triangle edge. The orbit of the generator point and its associated
perpendicular lines under the group action of△(r, s, t) produces the vertex and edge sets of the tilingT= (V, E, F).
The specific Wythoff tiling produced depends on the placement of the generator point relative to the triangle’s edges,
as summarized in Table S1. When the point lies in the interior of the fundamental triangle, it rests on no mirrors.
Consequently, every corner contributes its full ring of reflected copies, yielding faces with 2r,2s,and 2tsides. This
generates the omnitruncated tiling with a vertex configuration of 2r.2s.2t, meaning polygons with 2r,2s,and 2tsides
meet at every vertex in cyclic order. Figure S1 illustrates this interior placement for the△(4,4,4) group, where
r=s=t= 4 yields the regular 8.8.8 (or{8,3}) tiling in which three octagons meet at every vertex.

[PDF page 23]
4
Moving the generator point onto a mirror (triangle edge) or into a corner (triangle vertex) produces the other
uniform tilings of the group. When the point lies on a single mirror, it remains fixed under the corresponding
reflection. As a result, the polygonal rings around the two corners on that mirror are halved, while the ring at the
opposite corner remains full. If the point sits precisely at a corner, it lies on two mirrors simultaneously: the rings
at the other two corners are halved, and the occupied corner itself no longer generates a face. These seven possible
generator positions and their corresponding tilings are detailed in Table S1. As an illustrative example, Figure S2
depicts the complete Wythoff family for the△(2,4,6) group.
FIG. S1. The△(4,4,4) triangle group and its associated three-colorable infinite 8.8.8 (or{8,3}) hyperbolic tiling.(Left)The
fundamental triangle with internal anglesπ/4,π/4,π/4 and generator point in the interior.(Right)The tiling generated by
the orbit of the fundamental triangle under△(4,4,4), shown in the Poincar´ e disk. Each triangle corresponds to a unique group
element, labeled by a word in the generatorsa,b,c, with the identity elementemarked.
Generator point Vertex configuration Type

B. Quadrilateral Groups
A quadrilateral group is generated by reflectionsa, b, c, dacross the four sides of a quadrilateral with interior angles
π/r,π/s,π/t,π/u. The group presentation is
□(r, s, t, u) =

a, b, c, d
 a2 =b 2 =c 2 =d 2 = (ab)r = (bc)s = (cd)t = (da)u =e

,(S3)
wherer, s, t, uare the orders of the corner rotationsρ=ab,σ=bc,τ=cd,υ=darespectively, withρστ υ=e. The
quadrilateral is hyperbolic when 1/r+ 1/s+ 1/t+ 1/u <2. The orientation-preserving subgroup□ +(r, s, t, u) is an
index-2 subgroup, with presentation
□+(r, s, t, u) =

ρ, σ, τ
 ρr =σ s =τ t = (ρστ) u =e

,(S4)
where we have usedυ= (ρστ) −1. Reflecting the fundamental quadrilateral in its four sides produces a tiling with
face configurationV2r.2s.2t.2u, where every vertex degree is even (i.e. the vertex degrees of a quadrilateral tile are
2r, 2s, 2t, 2u). Each tile corresponds to a unique group element of□(r, s, t, u), labeled by a word in the generators
a, b, c, d(see Figure S3).
We note that quadrilateral tilings of the hyperbolic plane can also arise from triangle groups, as we discuss in
Section S1 E. The two families of groups are complementary: quadrilateral groups produce tilings whose vertex
degrees are always even, including non-regular tilings such asV4.4.8.8 from□(2,2,4,4) (Figure S3), whereas triangle
groups can produce only regular or quasi-regular (dual) quadrilateral tilings but can accommodate both even and odd
vertex degrees. We also note that a Wythoff construction on the quadrilateral group is also possible, giving the other
uniform tilings of the group, but we do not use it in this work.

C. Dual Tilings
Every tilingT= (V, E, F) has a dual tilingT ∗, obtained by placing a vertex at the center of each face ofTand
connecting two such vertices whenever the corresponding faces share an edge. This exchanges the roles of vertices
and faces: the faces ofTbecome the vertices ofT ∗, the vertices ofTbecome the faces ofT ∗, and each edge ofTis
crossed by exactly one dual edge ofT ∗. Taking the dual twice recovers the original tiling, (T ∗)∗ =T. We sometimes
use the face-configuration notation to name dual tilings: ifThas vertex configurationr.s.t, we writeV r.s.tfor its
dualT ∗. Figure S4 shows the primal 8.8.8 (or{8,3}) tiling, in which three octagons meet at each vertex, together
with its dualV8.8.8 (or{3,8}), in which eight triangles meet at each vertex.
a 
 b c

denotes the vertex configuration in which three octagons meet at each vertex; the prefixVdenotes the dual, whose vertices
correspond to the faces of 8.8.8.aThe primal tilingT= 8.8.8.bThe dual tilingT ∗ =V8.8.8.cTandT ∗ superimposed.

D. Color Code Tilings
To define a valid color code on a two-dimensional tiled surface, the tiling must satisfy two geometric conditions.
The tiling must betrivalent(three edges meeting at each vertex) and its faces must bethree-colorablesuch that no
two faces that share an edge have the same color [1]. We refer to tilings that satisfy these specific geometric properties
ascolor code tilings.
To construct hyperbolic color codes, we systematically obtain valid color code tilings for hyperbolic surfaces using
the Wythoff construction explained in Section S1 A [2, 3]. As shown in Table S1, the interior generator point yields
a tiling with the vertex configuration 2r.2s.2t. Since exactly three faces meet at every vertex, the resulting graph
is trivalent. Moreover, the faces of the omnitruncated tiling correspond directly to the three distinct corners of the
fundamental triangle. This natural tripartite division guarantees that the faces are three-colorable, making them color
code tilings. Figure S1 illustrates an example of a color code tiling with octagon faces. The dual of the color tiling is
the underlying triangular tiling which we denoteT △. The triangular tiles ofT △ are associated with group elements
of△(r, s, t). Equivalently, the vertices and edges of the color code tilingTform the Cayley graph of△(r, s, t): the
vertices are the group elements and they are joined by an edge whenever they are related by a generatora,borc.
E. Quadrilateral Tilings
Aquadrilateral tilingT □ is a tiling of quadrilateral faces. In this work, we use quadrilateral tilings to construct
hyperbolic surface codes. Specifically, we define a hyperbolic surface code on the dualT= (T □)∗ of a quadrilateral
tilingT □ by associating stabilizer generators with the faces ofT. ForTto support a valid surface code, its faces must
betwo-colorable: each face is assigned one of two colors (e.g. red and blue) such that no two faces sharing an edge
receive the same color. This ensures thatX- andZ-type stabilizer generators can be assigned to faces of different
color, so that stabilizers of opposite type with overlapping support always share an even number of data qubits and
therefore commute. The faces ofTare two-colorable whenever the vertices ofT □ are two-colorable, i.e. its edges
form a bipartite graph. On the infinite plane this is always the case: every face ofT □ has even length, so every cycle
has even length and the graph is bipartite. On the closed surfaces of Section S1 F this can fail since the tiling on the
surface now has homologically non-trivial cycles, which may sometimes have odd length. Therefore, we check each
closed quadrilateral tiling and keep only those that remain bipartite.
Quadrilateral tilingsT □ of the hyperbolic plane can arise from triangle groups, quadrilateral groups, or in many
cases from both, and we construct codes using tilings from both symmetry groups.
The first class of tilings we consider are those that have quadrilateral reflection symmetry. These are generated
by a quadrilateral group□(r, s, t, u) and correspond to tilings where every vertex degree is even, giving the face
configurationV2r.2s.2t.2u. In this case, a single quadrilateral face is a valid Coxeter polygon and can be taken as the
fundamental domain. The tiles are in bijection with the group elements of□(r, s, t, u), and adjacent tiles are related
by right multiplication by a generator (see Figure S3).
The second class of tilings are regular quadrilateral tilings{4, q}(orV q.q.q.q), whereqquadrilaterals meet at every
vertex andqmay be odd. Whenqis odd (such as{4,5}), the quadrilateral cannot act as the fundamental domain for
a quadrilateral reflection group. Nevertheless,{4, q}tilings can be systematically constructed from triangle groups,
such as from△(2,4, q) groups via the Wythoff construction by placing the generator point at the cornerc∩a(see
Figure S2c for the construction of{4,6}from△(2,4,6)). Algebraically, the quadrilateral tiles in this case correspond
to the left cosets△(2,4, q)/Dof the subgroupD=⟨b, c⟩ ∼= D4 of order 8, with each quadrilateral formed by the
union of the 8 images of the fundamental triangle belonging to a cosetgDfor group elementsg∈ △(2,4, q).
Regular tilings with even vertex degrees support both algebraic descriptions simultaneously. For example, the
{4,8}tiling (Figure S5) can be generated either from the quadrilateral group□(4,4,4,4) or from the triangle group
△(2,4,8).□(4,4,4,4) is an index-8 subgroup of△(2,4,8). Figure S5 shows how the tiles are labeled by cosets. The
fundamental triangle is the identitye, and every other triangle is an image of it under someg∈ △(2,4,8). The 8
images forming one quadrilateral are exactly those differing by an element ofD, so they share a cosetgDand the tile
is labeled by a chosen representativeg, e.g. the central tile iseD=D. Each quadrilateral is thus both an image of
the fundamental domain of□(4,4,4,4) and a coset ofDin△(2,4,8).

[PDF page 27]
8
FIG. S5. The{4,8}quadrilateral tiling of the hyperbolic plane and its coset decomposition under the triangle group△(2,4,8).
(Left)The tiling generated on the Poincar´ e disk. Each quadrilateral tile (black lines) is formed by the union of 8 triangles with
interior anglesπ/2, π/4, π/8 (thin orange lines) and corresponds to a left cosetgD∈ △(2,4,8)/Dof the dihedral subgroup
D=⟨b, c⟩ ∼= D4. This tiling can equivalently be generated as the orbit of the fundamental domain of the quadrilateral group
