correspond to the cornerc∩awith interior angleπ/8.
F. Closed Hyperbolic Surfaces from Quotient Groups
The triangle and quadrilateral groups both tile the infinite hyperbolic plane, and closed hyperbolic surfaces can
be obtained from their quotient groups. We describe the construction for a triangle group△(r, s, t) here. The
quadrilateral case is identical, with□(r, s, t, u) in place of△(r, s, t) throughout.
To obtain a finite tiling on a closed surface we identify a finite-index normal subgroup Γ⊴△(r, s, t) and form the
quotient Σ =H 2/Γ, which identifies points ofH 2 that lie in the same Γ-orbit. For Σ to be a smooth closed surface,
Γ must act freely, that is without fixed points. Reflections fix their mirror lines and rotations fix their centers, so a
free Γ can contain no reflections or rotations. Γ can consist only of the fixed-point-free isometries ofH 2 which are
hyperbolic translations and glide reflections. The identifications imposed by such a Γ fold the infinite tiling into a
finite tiling on the compact surface Σ.
The quotient groupG(r, s, t) =△(r, s, t)/Γ acts as the symmetry group of the tiled closed hyperbolic surface, with
presentation
G(r, s, t) =

a, b, c
 a2 =b 2 =c 2 = (ab)r = (bc)s = (ca)t =e,Γ-relations

,(S5)
where the Γ-relations are the additional identifications that renderGfinite and correspond to generators of Γ. Each
element ofGcorresponds to a distinct copy of the fundamental triangle and the total number of triangles in the finite
tiling equals the index|G|= [△(r, s, t) : Γ]. Adjacent triangles are related by right multiplication by a generator (see
Figure S7), so the adjacency structure of the tiling is the Cayley graph ofGwith respect to{a, b, c}.
We use two complementary computational methods to enumerate quotient groups:
1.Low-Index Normal Subgroups (LINS): The first approach is an exhaustive search based on Todd–Coxeter
coset enumeration [3, 4]. We use theLINSpackage [5] inGAP[6] to systematically find all normal subgroups
Γ⊴△(r, s, t) up to a given index. This guarantees that we enumerate every finite tiling up to that size, but it
must enumerate all normal subgroups to do so, and there are many of them, so reaching large index (large|G|)
becomes prohibitively expensive.
2.Epimorphism Search: To reach larger indices than the exhaustive search can, we instead search for epimor-
phisms onto a chosen target group. We fix a candidate finite groupH(such as a projective special linear group
PSL(2, q)) and search for a surjective homomorphism, orepimorphism,ϕ:△(r, s, t)→H. Its kernel Γ = ker(ϕ)
is a normal subgroup with quotientG(r, s, t) =△(r, s, t)/Γ ∼= H. By targeting a specificHwe obtain a large
quotient directly, without enumerating subgroups up to its index.

[PDF page 28]
9
i. Topology and Orientability of the Surface
The topology of the closed surface Σ =H 2/Γ follows from the Gauss–Bonnet theorem. In the constant negative
curvature ofH 2 each fundamental triangle has areaπ(1−1/r−1/s−1/t), and Σ is tiled by|G|images of it, so
Area(Σ) =|G|π

1− 1
r − 1
s − 1
t

=−2πχ(Σ),(S6)
whereχ(Σ) is the Euler characteristic. Since 1/r+ 1/s+ 1/t <1 the area factor is positive, and|χ|grows linearly
with|G|.
The surface Σ isorientablewhen Γ preserves orientation, and non-orientable when it does not. Every isometry of
H2 is either orientation-preserving (translations, rotations) or orientation-reversing (reflections, glide reflections), the
latter being the odd-length words ina, b, c. Thus Σ is orientable if and only if Γ contains only even-length words, that
is Γ≤ △ +(r, s, t), the index-2 orientation-preserving subgroup. A free Γ that meets this condition contains neither
rotations (excluded by freeness) nor glide reflections (excluded by orientation), leaving only hyperbolic translations.
Our enumeration produces both orientable and non-orientable quotients, and we construct hyperbolic color and surface
codes on both. We report their parameters in Section S12.
Orientable and non-orientable surfaces constructed from the same tiling look identical locally and differ only in how
they close up. This affects the structure of the homologically non-trivial cycles of the surface, which, as we explain in
Section S5, are associated with the logical operators of the code, so the difference carries over to the logical structure.
The number of independent homologically non-trivial cycles is
dimH 1(Σ;F 2) = 2−χ(Σ),(S7)
which gives 2gcycles for an orientable surface of genusg(whereχ= 2−2g) andccycles for a non-orientable surface
with cross-cap numberc(whereχ= 2−c). Figure S6 shows a torus and a Klein bottle as the smallestEuclidean
examples of an orientable and a non-orientable surface, each with its fundamental polygon. The distinction lies in
how their cycles cross: on the orientable torus the non-trivial cycles form asymplectic pair, crossing each other an
odd number of times and any other representative of their own class an even number of times, whereas on the non-
orientable Klein bottle a cycle can cross another representative of its own class an odd number of times, so no such
pairing exists. The hyperbolic surfaces we use are the higher-genus and higher-cross-cap analogues of these two.
In this work, we mostly focus on the orientable surfaces, for which we build a canonical logical basis by finding
symplectic pairs of homologically non-trivial cycles (Section S5). In the basis we design, non-trivial cycles are also
related to one another by the orientation-preserving symmetries of the tiling, so that the corresponding logical op-
erators can be mapped onto each other by the fault-tolerant walking circuits of Section S7 for logical measurement
(Section S6). The symplectic pairing of cycles and their symmetries are what makes this basis well-defined.
The non-orientable surfaces also yield codes with favorable parameters, in some cases outperforming those of
orientable surfaces of comparable size (see Section S12). We expect most of the methods developed in this work to
apply to them directly, however we leave the finer details of a modular QEC architecture based on non-orientable
surfaces to future work.
ii. Orientable Surfaces
For an orientable Σ, the quotient groupGcorresponds to a compact surface Σ g of genusg≥2 withχ= 2−2g,
and its orientation-preserving symmetries form the index-2 subgroup

FIG. S6. The torusT 2 and the Klein bottleKas the simplest orientable and non-orientable Euclidean closed surfaces. Each is
shown as a surface and as a fundamental polygon whose opposite edges are identified according to the arrows.aThe torus is
orientable: both pairs of opposite edges are glued without a flip (all arrows on identified edges point in the same direction). The

forces them to cross each other an odd number of times. Therefore, the non-trivial cycles do not arrange into symplectic pairs
as they do on the torus.
iii. Double Covers
Double covers provide an additional method to construct closed surfaces, enlarging the pool of tilings found by
the epimorphism search. A double cover of Σ is a surface eΣ with a two-to-one map eΣ→Σ, with two points of eΣ
lying above each point of Σ. We use double covers to obtain orientable surfaces from non-orientable ones and to
enforce the vertex two-colorability (bipartiteness) required for valid surface codes on closed quadrilateral tilings (see
Section S1 E), thereby enlarging the pool of candidate tilings.
Orientable double cover.Algebraically, the orientable double cover discards the orientation-reversing elements of
Γ and replaces Γ with
˜Γ := Γ∩ △ +(r, s, t),(S10)
which retains only the orientation-preserving elements. Since ˜Γ≤ △ +(r, s, t) by construction, the quotienteΣ =H 2/˜Γ
is orientable. When Σ is non-orientable, ˜Γ has index 2 in Γ (since [△:△ +] = 2), so it imposes half as many
identifications and yields an orientable surface with twice as many tiles,| ˜G|= 2|G|. When Σ is already orientable,
Γ≤ △ + and the construction returns ˜Γ = Γ unchanged.
Computationally, we obtain ˜Γ by constructing the group ˜G≤G×Z 2 generated by ˜a= (a,1), ˜b= (b,1), and
˜c= (c,1), where 1 is the non-identity element ofZ 2. TheZ 2 factor tracks word-length parity, ensuring that an
element of△(r, s, t) maps to the identity in ˜Gif and only if it belongs to Γ and has even word length. Consequently,
the kernel of the epimorphism△(r, s, t)→ ˜Gis precisely Γ∩ △ +(r, s, t).
Bipartite double cover.For regular quadrilateral tilings{4, q}constructed from a triangle group△(2,4, q), the
generatorsaandcboth fix the vertices of the quadrilateral tiling, and onlybmoves between vertices (see Figure S5).
The vertex two-colorability required for a valid surface code (Section S1 E) therefore depends on the parity ofbin each
element of Γ: the closed tiling is bipartite if and only if every element of Γ contains an even number ofbgenerators.
When this condition fails, we can restore bipartiteness by taking a double cover that twistsbby the non-trivial element
ofZ 2. Specifically, we construct ˜G≤G×Z 2 generated by ˜a= (a,0), ˜b= (b,1), and ˜c= (c,0), so that theZ 2 factor
tracks only the parity ofb. We note that even when the base quadrilateral tiling is not bipartite, the fine-grained
semi-hyperbolic tilings that we introduce later in Section S2 B can still be bipartite ifLis even, in which case valid
semi-hyperbolic surface codes can be defined without taking a double cover.
We apply these double cover constructions to the quotients found by our searches, keeping both the original tilings
and the covers as code tiling candidates.
G. Left and Right Multiplication in Hyperbolic Symmetry Groups
As explained in Section S1 A, when the hyperbolic surface is tiled by a fundamental domain of a triangle group,
we obtain a tiling with face configurationV2r.2s.2t(referred to as the dual tiling in Figure S4b). As illustrated in

[PDF page 30]
11
Figure S7, the fundamental triangle is labeled by the identity elemente. Any other triangle in the tiling is reached
by applying a specific sequence of reflections, corresponding to a unique group elementg∈G.
The algebraic structure of the quotient groupG=△(r, s, t)/Γ and its orientation-preserving subgroupG + defines
both the local geometric structure and global symmetries of the corresponding hyperbolic tilings. This dual role can
be understood through the distinction between left and right group multiplication.
FIG. S7. Example of a quotient triangle group△(4,4,4)/Γ and its orientation-preserving subgroup. The full quotient triangle
group has order 128 and defines a hyperbolic color code with parameters [[128,36,8]].(Left)The full quotient group defines a
V8.8.8 tiling of a closed hyperbolic surface. Each group element is associated with a distinct triangle. Right multiplication by
a generator (a,borc) corresponds to a reflection across a local mirror to an adjacent triangle, defining the local connectivity.
Left multiplication corresponds to a global isometry (reflection, rotation, or translation) of the entire tiled surface.(Right)
The orientation-preserving subgroup△ +(4,4,4)/Γ. Its fundamental domains are quadrilaterals, each formed by joining a
pair of adjacent triangles (related by abreflection). The subgroup generatorsρ :=abandσ :=bcare corner rotations.
Left multiplication by each of these subgroup generators acts as a global isometry (as illustrated by the arrows connecting
quadrilaterals), transforming the entire tiled surface while preserving its structure. Right multiplication instead defines the
local adjacency between quadrilaterals.
Left multiplication defines global symmetries of the tiling. As per convention, the group acts on the tiled surface
via left multiplication. Consider the operation that maps every tilegin the tiling tohg, whereh∈Gis a fixed group
element. Geometrically, this left multiplication applies a global isometry (a translation, rotation, or reflection) to the
entire tiled surface, mapping the tiling onto itself.
Right multiplication defines local adjacency, corresponding to a local move to an adjacent tile. Considering a specific
triangular tilegas an example, its immediate neighbors are the triangles that share its edges. The reflections across
the three edges of the fundamental triangleeare the generatorsa,bandc. The reflections across the corresponding
edges of the displaced trianglegare given instead by the conjugate operatorsgag −1,gbg −1 andgcg −1. To see why,
consider the action of reflecting across an edge ofg: one first mapsgback to the identitye(viag −1), performs the
reflection (e.g.,a), and then maps back tog(viag). Applying one of these local reflections to the tileggives its
neighbor (gag −1)g=ga. Therefore, right multiplication by a generator corresponds to crossing a local mirror (tile
boundary) to an adjacent tile.
This geometric interpretation of left and right multiplication generalizes in the expected way to the orientation-
preserving subgroupG + =△ +(r, s, t)/Γ, shown in the right panel of Figure S7. Here, the fundamental domain is
no longer a single triangle but a quadrilateral formed by joining a pair of adjacent triangles (specifically the identity
triangleeand its neighborb, related by a reflection). Each quadrilateral in this new tiling is identified with an element
p∈G +, representing a pair of trianglespandpb. Left multiplication by elements ofG + defines the global, orientation-
preserving isometries of the closed surface. For the two rotation generatorsρ :=abandσ :=bc, multiplying on the
left corresponds to a global rotation (withρandσeach centered on different vertices), while right multiplication by
the same generators corresponds to a local move to an adjacent tile.
In the context of modular hyperbolic codes, as discussed later in this supplement, we will see that the global

[PDF page 31]
12
symmetries define automorphisms of the code used for implementing logic (and the connectivity required for this)
whereas right multiplication defines the inter-module connectivity required to implement syndrome extraction circuits.
S2. HYPERBOLIC AND SEMI-HYPERBOLIC CODE CONSTRUCTIONS
In this section we explain how we construct hyperbolic codes from tilings of closed hyperbolic surfaces, and how we
obtain semi-hyperbolic codes by fine-graining the base hyperbolic tiling with a Euclidean tiling.
A. Hyperbolic and Semi-Hyperbolic Color Codes
Color codes are defined on color code tilings, with data qubits associated with vertices and both anX- and a
Z-type stabilizer generator supported on each face [1]. We start with a color code tilingT= (V, E, F) on a closed
hyperbolic surface Σ, whose dual is the base triangular tilingT △ = (V △, E△, F △) associated with the symmetry
groupG=△(r, s, t)/Γ. We write|F △|for the number of base triangular tiles. Each triangle face inT △ corresponds
to a group elementg∈G, so the number of triangular tiles is equal to the order of the quotient group|F △|=|G|.
The tilingThas vertex configuration 2r.2s.2tand defines a hyperbolic color code, which we label 2r.2s.2t-|F △|. Since
T= (T △)∗ is the dual of the base triangular tiling, its vertices, edges and faces are those ofT △ with the roles of
vertices and faces exchanged,|V|=|F △|,|E|=|E △|and|F|=|V △|. Using|F △|=|G|, thatT △ is a triangulation
(|E△|= 3

The hyperbolic color code hasn=|V|=|F △|data qubits, one on each vertex ofT. Since each homologically

On an orientable surface of genusgEquation (S13) reduces tok= 4g. The code distance of hyperbolic color codes

We construct semi-hyperbolic color codes by fine-graining the hyperbolic tiling [3], illustrated in stages in Figure S8.
We note that these semi-hyperbolic tilings were used previously to construct semi-hyperbolic Floquet codes [3], but
have not previously been used to construct color codes. Following [3], we start from the base triangular tilingT △
(Figure S8b), the dual of the color code tilingT(Figure S8a). We subdivide every edge intoL≥1 segments, where
Lis the fine-graining parameter (side-length), and fill each triangular tile with a Euclidean{3,6}tiling, giving the
fine-grained tilingT △
L (Figure S8c). Its dual gives the semi-hyperbolic color code tilingT L = (T △
L )∗ (Figure S8d),
on which we place the color code (Figure S8e). AtL= 1 no fine-graining takes place and we recover the hyperbolic
color code, whileL≥2 gives a semi-hyperbolic tiling and the corresponding semi-hyperbolic color code. We label the
resulting codes as 2r.2s.2t-|F △|-L.
The faces of the semi-hyperbolic color code tilingT L are of two types. Bulk faces lie in the interior of a fine-grained
region and are hexagons, supporting weight-six stabilizers locally identical to a Euclidean planar 6.6.6 color code.
Corner faces, on the other hand, are inherited from the vertex configuration of the hyperbolic tilingTsupporting
stabilizers of weight 2r, 2sor 2t. The number of high-weight corner stabilizers is fixed by the underlying hyperbolic
surface and does not grow withL.
Fine-graining does not change the topology of Σ, so dimH 1(Σ;F 2) and hencekare unchanged, while the number
of physical data qubits grows quadratically inL. On the other hand, code distance scales roughly linearly withL.

B. Hyperbolic and Semi-Hyperbolic Surface Codes
Surface codes can be defined on two-colorable tilings, with data qubits associated with vertices,X-type stabilizer
generators supported on each face of one color (e.g. red faces) andZ-type stabilizer generators on each face of
another color (e.g. blue faces). We start with a base quadrilateral tilingT □ = (V □, E□, F □) on a closed hyperbolic
surface Σ and define the hyperbolic surface code on its dualT= (T □)∗. We write|F □|for the number of base
quadrilateral tiles. As explained in Section S1 E, we use quadrilateral tilings with face configurationV2r.2s.2t.2uthat
arise from quadrilateral groups or regular{4, q}quadrilateral tilings from a triangle group. The underlying symmetry
group determines how the number of quadrilateral tiles|F □|relates to the quotient order|G|. In either case|F □|
is the quantity associated with the number of physical data qubits. For generality, let us useV a.b.c.dfor the face
configuration of an arbitrary quadrilateral tilingT □ in this section, wherea, b, c, dare the vertex degrees of a tile. We
label the hyperbolic surface codeV a.b.c.d-|F □|. For the regular tilings{4, q}, we also use the shorthand{4, q}-|F □|.
SinceT= (T □)∗ is the dual of the base quadrilateral tiling, its vertices, edges and faces are those ofT □ with the
roles of vertices and faces exchanged,|V|=|F □|,|E|=|E □|and|F|=|V □|. Using thatT □ has quadrilateral faces
(|E□|= 2|F □|) and vertices of degreea,b,cord,

We construct semi-hyperbolic surface codes by fine-graining the hyperbolic tiling as for the color codes, but with
square tilings instead of triangular ones, illustrated in stages in Figure S9. Our fine-graining procedure is similar to
that of Ref. [9], but the resulting codes differ: we place data qubits on the vertices of (T □
L )∗, with the two stabilizer
types on faces of different color, whereas Ref. [9] places data qubits on the edges ofT □
L , analogous to the distinction
between the rotated and unrotated planar surface codes. With data qubits on the edges ofT □
L , every stabilizer of
one type is a weight-four face, while all high-weight corner stabilizers (on vertices of degree greater than four) are
of the other type, and the resultingXandZdistances generally differ (see Table 1 of Ref. [9]). In contrast, for
oddLour construction distributes the high-weight corner stabilizers between bothXandZtypes, and for regular
tilings theXandZstabilizers are related by a symmetry of the tiling (see Section S11 A). Furthermore, with data
qubits on the edges ofT □
L , those on the edges of the base quadrilaterals would lie on module boundaries, with
no natural choice of module to assign them to, leading to non-uniform modules, whereas in our construction every
data qubit lies strictly inside a base quadrilateral. We start from the base quadrilateral tilingT □ (Figure S9b),
the dual of the surface code tilingT(Figure S9a). We subdivide every edge intoL≥1 segments, whereLis the
fine-graining parameter, and fill each quadrilateral tile with a Euclidean square tiling, giving the fine-grained tiling
T □
L (Figure S9c). Its dual gives the semi-hyperbolic surface code tilingT L = (T □
L )∗ (Figure S9d), on which we place
the surface code (Figure S9e). AtL= 1 no fine-graining takes place and we recover the hyperbolic surface code, while
L≥2 gives a semi-hyperbolic tiling and the corresponding semi-hyperbolic surface code. We label the resulting codes
asV a.b.c.d-|F □|-L(or{4, q}-|F □|-Lfor regular tilings).
As in the color-code case, the faces of (T □

to a Euclidean planar surface code, while the corner faces inherited from the vertices ofT □ carry higher-weight
stabilizers of weighta,b,cord. The number of high-weight corner stabilizers is fixed by the underlying hyperbolic
surface and does not grow withL.
Fine-graining does not change the topology of Σ, so dimH 1(Σ;F 2) and hencekare unchanged, while the number
of data qubits grows quadratically inL. Since (T □

A caveat concerns which fine-grained tilings define valid surface codes. The surface code requires the tiling (T □

S3. MODULE PARTITIONS AND ANCILLA PLACEMENT
The hyperbolic code constructions of Section S2 are defined on closed hyperbolic surfaces. In this section we
describe how to map these codes onto a modular QEC architecture of boundary-connected planar chips. We present
the available module partitions for color codes and surface codes. The module partitions and the inter-module
connectivity follow from the algebraic structure of the symmetry groups of the tiling as we will see below. Modular
implementations of semi-hyperbolic Floquet codes were discussed in Ref. [3], using modules similar to the triangle
partition below, and simulated in Ref. [10], using recursive spectral bisection of the code’s connectivity graph, which
does not in general produce identical modules or a layout with nearest-neighbor gates on a 2D grid. In contrast, each
of our modules is a small grouping of base tiles that can be laid out on a 2D grid with nearest-neighbor couplers.

[PDF page 36]
17
A. Semi-Hyperbolic Color Code Modules
The semi-hyperbolic color codes are constructed from a base triangular tilingT △, whose|F △|=|G|tiles are in
bijection with elements of the quotient triangle groupG=△(r, s, t)/Γ. A module partition groups the triangular
tiles, and their associated data qubits and ancilla qubits into planar modules. Figure S10 illustrates three color code
module types and how they are derived from the base triangular tilingT △. The top row shows the module partition
on the base tiling in the Poincar´ e disk and the bottom row shows the corresponding fine-grained planar modules. We
describe the different partitions with parameterK, which corresponds to the number of triangular tiles grouped into
a single module. Figure S10a-ccorrespond to triangle (K= 1), quadrilateral (K= 2) and octagon (K= 8) partitions
respectively.
FIG. S10. Module partition options for semi-hyperbolic color codes, illustrated on theV8.8.8 base triangular tiling from
G=△(4,4,4)/Γ with|G|= 128 (the same tiling shown with group element labels in Figure S7).Top row:The base
tiling shown in the Poincar´ e disk, with the red dashed lines tracing the boundaries of a single module.Bottom row:The
corresponding fine-grained planar module (shown here forL= 3), with data qubits (filled circles) and ancilla qubits (open
circles). Solid black lines indicate in-module couplers whereas dashed gray lines indicate inter-module couplers.aTriangle
module(K=1): each triangular tile forms its own module and is an imagegFof a chosen fundamental domainFofG, so the
modules are in bijection with the elements ofG.bQuadrilateral module(K=2): pairs of adjacent triangles are merged across an
edge of the chosen edge color (e.g. here it is a blue edge), each an image of a fundamental domain of the orientation-preserving
subgroupG +.cOctagon module(K=8): eight triangles meeting at a vertex are grouped together, each module corresponding
to a coset.
i. Triangle Modules (K= 1)
The simplest partition assigns each tile ofT △ to its own module, giving|G|triangular modules (Figure S10a).
Each tile is an imagegFof a chosen fundamental domainFof the quotient groupG, so the modules are in bijection
with the elements ofG. After fine-graining with parameterL, each triangular module containsL 2 data qubits and
N= 2L 2 + 2Ltotal qubits including ancillas. This is the finest possible partition: it minimizes module size but
maximizes the number of inter-module seams.

[PDF page 37]
18
ii. Quadrilateral Modules (K= 2)
The quadrilateral partition we use is associated with the orientation-preserving subgroupG + =△ +(r, s, t)/Γ and
pairs adjacent triangles into quadrilateral modules (Figure S10b). The construction exploits the three-edge-coloring
of the base triangular tilingT △, where the three edge colors correspond to the three reflection generatorsa,b,cof
△(r, s, t), so that two adjacent tiles sharing a color-medge (m∈ {a, b, c}) are related by right multiplication by the
reflectionm.
Choosing any one of the three colors, or equivalently one of the three reflectionsm∈ {a, b, c}, and pairing each tile
gwith its neighborgmacross the corresponding edge, partitions the|G|tiles into|G|/2 left cosetsgD={g, gm}of
the order-two subgroupD=⟨m⟩ ≤G.
Becausemreverses orientation, each coset{g, gm}contains exactly one elementg∈G + =⟨ρ, σ⟩, so each module
is an image of a fundamental domain ofG + and can be labeled by its orientation-preserving elementg∈G + (see
Figure S7). The resulting quadrilateral modules each contain 2L 2 data qubits andN= 4L 2 + 2Ltotal qubits.
Figure S11 illustrates how a quadrilateral module grows with the fine-graining parameterL.
This partition is the one we use most, because the bijection between modules and elements ofG+ allows us to design
fault-tolerant circuits with minimal additional inter-module connectivity in order to implement orientation-preserving
symmetries of the tiling. As explained in Section S1 G, neighboring modules are reached by right multiplication by
the generatorsρandσ, which fixes the inter-module connectivity, while a global symmetry of the surface acts by left
multiplication and permutes the modules as whole units. A global automorphism therefore maps each module onto
another module rather than cutting across module boundaries, and this allows it to be realized as an automorphism
circuit (Section S7). We use these automorphism circuits to map between the logical operators of the code by choosing
a suitable symmetrical canonical logical basis (Sections S5 and S5 A) and design a ribbon extractor for logical readout
(Section S6). We explain these constructions in the later sections. We can also choose to leave some of the paired
triangles split, keeping them as two separate triangle modules rather than merging them into a quadrilateral. This
introduces additional module boundaries where more inter-module seams can be attached, which is useful when we
need extra connectivity for particular modules, for instance for logical readout using an extractor.
Each quadrilateral module can be laid out on the square grid in two ways as shown in Figure S12. The honeycomb
layout uses honeycomb plaquettes and results in a rhombus-shaped module, while the diagonal rectangle layout uses
diagonal couplers to pack the qubits into a rectangular footprint. Both layouts lead to modules with an equal number
of qubits, and the choice affects only the physical arrangement of qubits and couplers.
FIG. S11. Quadrilateral (honeycomb) hyperbolic color code modules with increasing fine-graining parameterL.
iii. Octagon Modules (K= 8)
A coarser partition groups eight triangles into a single octagonal module (Figure S10c). These eight tiles are the
ones meeting at a vertex ofT △ of a chosen color, or equivalently the eight triangles making up one octagonal face
of the color code tilingTof a chosen color, for instance a red face. Associating red, green and blue edges witha,
bandcmirrors, respectively, a red face corresponds to eight triangular tiles ofT △ related bybandcreflections
(right multiplication bybandc). Each such group of tiles is a left cosetgD=g⟨b, c⟩of the order-eight dihedral
subgroupD=⟨b, c⟩ ∼= D4 ≤G, so in general the octagonal modules are in bijection with the|G|/8 left cosets ofD

[PDF page 38]
19
FIG. S12. Two plaquette template options for color code modules shown on a quadrilateral module atL= 5.aHoneycomb
layout.bDiagonal rectangle layout.
rather than with single group elements ofG +. After fine-graining, each octagon module contains 8L 2 data qubits and
N= 16L 2 + 8 total qubits.
This module is available only when the color code tiling actually has octagonal faces, which happens for a 2r.2s.2t
tiling when one ofr,s,tequals 4, so that the corresponding face has 8 sides. For the 8.8.8 family all three face
colors are octagons, and any of them may be used. Grouping tiles into an octagon module reduces the number of
inter-module seams at the cost of a larger planar chip. Octagon modules require a modified set of automorphisms that
permute modules without rotating them internally. In Sections M J and S7 E we explain how we find these, together
with symmetric logical bases, for the tilings we consider.
B. Semi-Hyperbolic Surface Code Modules
The semi-hyperbolic surface codes are constructed from a base quadrilateral tilingT □. A module partition groups
the quadrilateral tiles, together with their data and ancilla qubits, into planar modules, and we again label the
partitions by the numberKof base tiles grouped into each module. Figure S13 shows the three surface code module
types, withK= 1,K= 2 andK= 4 giving the square, rectangle and big square modules respectively. As in
the color code case, the module structure follows from the symmetry group of the tiling, though here the details
depend on whether the quadrilateral tiling comes from a quadrilateral group or from a triangle group, as discussed in
Section S1 E.
i. Square Modules (K= 1)
The simplest partition assigns each quadrilateral tile ofT □ to its own module, giving one square module per tile
(Figure S13a). When the tiling comes from a quadrilateral group, each tile is an imagegFof a chosen fundamental
domainFofG=□(r, s, t, u)/Γ, so the modules are in bijection with the group elements. When the quadrilateral
tiling instead arises from a triangle group, each tile is associated with a coset rather than a single group element (see
Section S1 E). After fine-graining, each square module containsL 2 data qubits andN= 2L 2 + 2L+ 1 total qubits.
Like the triangle module for the color code, this is the finest partition we use, minimizing module size.
ii. Rectangle Modules (K= 2)
The rectangle partition pairs two adjacent quadrilateral tiles into a single module (Figure S13b). When the tiling
has quadrilateral reflection symmetry, each pair is an image of a fundamental domain of the orientation-preserving
subgroupG +, and the modules are in bijection with its elements. In this case the same picture as for the color code
applies, with adjacent modules reached by right multiplication and automorphisms acting by left multiplication, so
we can again implement automorphism circuits, described with a connectivity given by the left Cayley graph ofG +.
After fine-graining, each rectangle module contains 2L 2 data qubits andN= 4L 2 + 3L+ 1 total qubits.
Pairing the square tiles into rectangles is not always possible when the quadrilateral tiling does not have quadrilateral
reflection symmetry. We use a maximum matching on the Cayley graph of the tiles in those cases and any tile left
unmatched remains a lone square (K= 1) module.

[PDF page 39]
20
FIG. S13. Module partition options for semi-hyperbolic surface codes, illustrated on aV4.6.4.6 base quadrilateral tiling.Top
row:The base tiling shown in the Poincar´ e disk, with the red dashed lines tracing the boundaries of a single module.Bottom
row:The corresponding planar module (shown here forL= 5), with data qubits (filled circles) and ancilla qubits (open
circles). Solid black lines indicate in-module couplers whereas dashed gray lines indicate inter-module couplers.aSquare
module(K=1): each quadrilateral tile forms its own module.bRectangle module(K=2): pairs of adjacent quadrilateral tiles
are merged across a shared edge.cBig square module(K=4): this requires a base tilingV a.b.c.dwith at least one ofa, b, c, d
equal to 4; choosing one such corner type, the four tiles meeting at each vertex of that type are grouped together.
iii. Big Square Modules (K= 4)
The coarsest surface code partition collects four quadrilateral tiles into a single big square module (Figure S13c).
After fine-graining, each big square module contains 4L2 data qubits andN= 8L 2 +4L+1 total qubits. This module
requires the four tiles to meet in a way that forms a larger square, which is available only for base quadrilateral tilings
V a.b.c.din which one ofa,b,c,dequals 4.
S4. MODULAR CAT-FLAGGED SYNDROME EXTRACTION CIRCUITS
We now explain the modular fault-tolerant syndrome extraction circuits we use to benchmark the performance
of the semi-hyperbolic (L≥2) color and surface codes as a quantum memory. In all the circuits we design, every
two-qubit gate is either local to a module and acts between neighboring qubits, or it is a long-range gate between
modules acting via an inter-module coupler.
A. Hyperbolic Color Code Circuits
Modular hyperbolic color codes are associated with a 2r.2s.2thyperbolic tiling as explained in Section S2. We focus
on the quadrilateral module partition (K=2) in the explanation here, though the same circuit techniques apply to the
other partitions. We can classify the stabilizers into two categories:bulkandboundarystabilizers. Bulk stabilizers are
supported entirely within a single module and are all weight-6 in a quadrilateral module. Weight-6 bulk stabilizers are
measured using standard Bell-flagged color code circuits introduced for Euclidean planar color codes [11]. Boundary
stabilizers have support on data qubits in more than one module. Most boundary stabilizers are also weight-6, but
they also include thecorner stabilizersof weight 2r, 2sor 2t, inherited from the faces of the underlying 2r.2s.2t
hyperbolic tiling. We measure corner stabilizers with a generalization of Bell-flagged circuits that we callmodular

[PDF page 40]
21
cat-flagged circuits. Flag-based circuits for (non-fine-grained, non-modular) hyperbolic color codes were also studied
in Ref. [12].
The idea for modular cat-flagged syndrome extraction is that for a stabilizer allocatedmancillas across the modules
it touches, we unitarily prepare anm-qubit cat state on the ancillas using the inter-module couplers. Each ancilla then
couples to the data qubits associated with the stabilizer within its own module using nearest-neighbor gates on the
square grid. Finally we apply the inverse of the cat-state preparation circuit followed by single-qubit measurements,
which measures the stabilizer on a single ancilla qubit. The remainingm−1 ancillas give the flag measurements
that detect hook errors. One syndrome extraction round consists of anXsubround followed by aZsubround. Each
subround carries out a cat-state preparation, a data–ancilla coupling stage, and cat-state measurement.
The number of ancillas associated with a stabilizermis fixed by the boundary geometry rather than the stabilizer
weight, and varies even among stabilizers of equal weight, as Figure S14 shows. Figure S15, for example, shows a
weight-6 boundary stabilizer on a skewed boundary present in Figure S14, allocatedm= 3 ancillas (instead of 2)
to enforce nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates. The corner stabilizers are the most demanding case in the syndrome
extraction, since they require preparation of larger cat states. Figure M3 shows a weight-8 corner stabilizer shared
among 4 modules, extracted with a 4-qubit cat state. Figure S16 shows another weight-8 stabilizer that instead spans
8 modules with a single ancilla in each.
The cost of anm-qubit cat state depends on the inter-module connectivity available. Prepared along a linear chain
of seams it takes depth⌈m/2⌉. Adding further inter-module connectivity as shown in Figure S17 reduces this to
log-depth⌈log 2 m⌉, trading extra inter-module connectivity for a shallower circuit.
FIG. S14. Long-range stabilizers of four boundary-connected planar modules of an 8.8.8 modular hyperbolic color code. The
four modules are identical and related to their neighbors by a 90 ◦ rotation. Data qubits (black) and ancilla qubits (white) sit
on the square grid within each module. Dashed lines show the inter-module links. Stabilizers spanning across modules may get
skewed relative to the square-grid layout, and since ancillas are placed on the square grid the number available varies between
stabilizers, even among those of equal weight. The cat-flagged circuits accommodate this. A stabilizer allocatedmancillas is
extracted using anm-qubit cat state.
FIG. S15. A skewed weight-6 boundary stabilizer and theZ-basis cat-state preparation circuit used to measure theXstabilizer,
using a 3-qubit cat state prepared in CNOT-depth 2.

[PDF page 41]
22

RZ MZ RX MX

FIG. S16. A weight-8 stabilizer spanning 8 modules, with 1 ancilla per module, and the cat-flagged circuits used to measure its
XandZstabilizers, using an 8-qubit cat state. The cat state is prepared and measured along a linear chain in CNOT-depth
4, and syndrome CNOTs in depth 3.
FIG. S17. Log-depth preparation of the 8-qubitZ-basis cat state. Using additional inter-module connectivity, the cat state is
prepared in CNOT-depth 3 rather than depth 4. The measurement circuit is the inverse of the preparation circuit above.
The data–ancilla coupling gates within a block ofX- (orZ-) extraction all commute, and any edge coloring of the
bipartite data–ancilla interaction graph yields a valid schedule, of depth at most the maximum vertex degree of that
graph [13]. Since each data qubit couples to three ancillas, three time steps suffice. Additionally, on an orientable
surface we can define a globally consistent direction, and use the translationally-invariant up–middle–down plaquette
schedule of the planar color code rather than an unstructured circuit found by edge coloring. The up-middle-down
circuit shown in Figure S18 is applied identically to every face and every module.
The detectors of cat-flagged circuits are of two types, as shown in Figure S19. Aplaquette detectorcompares a
stabilizer’s syndrome measurement in one round with its measurement in the previous round. In the absence of errors,
this parity is deterministic, so the detector spans two consecutive rounds. In anXsubround these are theX-plaquette
detecting regions (Figure S19a), sensitive toZerrors, and in aZsubround they are theZ-plaquette detecting regions
(Figure S19b), sensitive toXerrors. Aflag detectorarises from each of them−1 ancillas that are not used to read
out the plaquette stabilizer syndrome. The cat-state measurement collapses the syndrome onto a single ancilla, and
the remainingm−1 ancillas are measured in the opposite basis. Each such measurement has a deterministic outcome
in the absence of errors, yieldingm−1 flag detectors per round (Figure S19c). These flag detectors fire on hook
errors that would otherwise propagate from a single fault on one ancilla qubit onto multiple data qubits and reduce
the effective distance.

same up–middle–down ordering is applied identically to every plaquette. The three panels, left to right, show its successive
time steps. The schedule shown extracts theZstabilizers; theXstabilizers are extracted by the same schedule with the CNOT
data–ancilla directions reversed.

FIG. S19. Detecting regions of the cat-flagged syndrome extraction circuit for a weight-8 stabilizer measured withm= 4 ancillas,

B. Hyperbolic Surface Code Circuits
The modular hyperbolic surface codes use cat-flagged syndrome extraction that is conceptually similar to that of the
modular hyperbolic color codes. As was done for the modular hyperbolic color code circuits, we unitarily prepare an
m-qubit cat state on themancilla qubits of each stabilizer that is supported on module boundaries (e.g. each stabilizer
that spans more than one module, or that spans both sides of a module that wraps around a handle of the surface).
Once the cat states have been prepared, each ancilla couples to the data qubits of its stabilizer within its own module

[PDF page 44]
25
using nearest-neighbor gates on the square grid. Finally we apply the inverse of the unitary cat state preparation
circuit followed by single-qubit measurements to measure the stabilizer along withm−1 flag measurements.
a b
FIG. S20. AdjacentXandZstabilizers in modular hyperbolic surface codes.a, The stabilizer structure of a modular hyperbolic
surface code derived from a{4,6}regular tiling withL= 5 fine-graining. Here we show the structure on the infinite hyperbolic
plane (rather than a fundamental polygon corresponding to a specific closed surface).b, Examples ofXandZstabilizers
that share support on data qubits in the code. If anXstabilizeriintersects with aZstabilizerj, the two stabilizers always
have exactly two qubits in common. Let stabilizeribe supported on ann i-gon plaquette containing anm i-qubit cat state.
Similarly stabilizerjis supported on ann j-gon plaquette containing anm j-qubit cat state. In general note thatn i ̸=n j and
mi ̸=m j, and furthermore note thatm k = 1 corresponds to the trivial case of a single-qubit|+⟩or|0⟩ancilla qubit for anX
orZstabilizer respectively.
One important difference with the hyperbolic surface code circuits is that we interleave theXandZsyndrome
extraction, unlike the hyperbolic color codes for whichXandZare measured sequentially. In other words, we extract

the CNOT gates used to extract adjacentXandZstabilizers do not commute.
If anXandZstabilizer have data qubits in common, they always share exactly two data qubits, as shown in
Figure S20. We can determine if a particular interleaved extraction schedule (CNOT ordering) is valid using the

CNOT gate CNOT(aX , aZ) coupling the ancillas of the two plaquettes. We can determine if a given schedule is correct
by starting with a schedule that is not interleaved and which we therefore know to be correct (extractXstabilizers
followed byZstabilizers), and then commute CNOT gates past each other until the schedule’s gate ordering is reached.

i. Lifted schedules
We now show how we find a valid CNOT gate ordering for a low-depth syndrome extraction circuit for the entire
modular hyperbolic code (i.e. such that the gate ordering for every pair of adjacentXandZstabilizers satisfies
Equation (S26)). We simplify the scheduling problem by choosing to use a translationally invariant schedule within
each face of the base tiling, so that the schedule is fully determined by its values at the four corners of each face
of the base tiling. Translational invariance is not a requirement, but it significantly simplifies the overall constraint
satisfaction problem (by reducing its size by a factor ofL 2) and leads to a more structured circuit.
Recall that the semi-hyperbolic surface code is constructed by subdividing each face of the base quadrilateral tiling
T □ into anL×Lgrid of fine squares, and then taking the dual. The fine tiling within each base face is therefore

[PDF page 46]
27
a regular square lattice, and we choose a schedule within each base face that is translationally invariant on this
fine-grained lattice (every face in the fine-grained lattice is assigned the same time steps, regardless of its basis).
Not every permutation of{0,1,2,3}assigned to the 4 corners of a square yields a valid translationally-invariant
schedule that satisfies Equation (S26) for pairs of adjacentXandZstabilizers. Only those permutations in the
dihedral orbit of the base permutation [0,1,3,2] underD 4 do (Figure S22). This gives 8 valid permutations per face:
the 4 rotations and 4 reflections of [0,1,3,2].
Assigning one of these 8 valid permutations to a face of the base tiling uniquely determines a translationally-
invariant fine schedule within that face (Figure S23b). We call this thelifted schedule: the coarse assignment at the
4 corners of each base face is “lifted” to the fullL×Lfine grid by translational invariance. The scheduling problem
thus reduces to a constraint satisfaction problem (CSP) on the base tiling, with one variable per face and a domain
of 8 valid permutations.
Optionally, the domain can be extended by allowing aglobal shift: adding a constant offsetδto all 4 timesteps of
a face, giving permutations of{δ, δ+1, δ+3, δ+2}. This enlarges the domain from 8 to 8(1 +δ max) permutations per
face, increasing the likelihood of finding a satisfying assignment at the cost of additional extraction layers. For the
circuits used in this work, we initially tryδ= 0. If that does not yield a solution we tryδ= 1 and finally fall back to
the boundary-first depth-6 schedule explained in Section S4 B iv if that also fails.
ii. Compatibility constraints between adjacent faces
When two base facesAandBshare an edge with verticesrands, as in Figure S23a, the lifted schedules withinA
andBmust be compatible. Lett F
x denote the time step assigned to vertexxin faceF(see Figure S23a). Two types
of constraints arise:
Commutativity constraint (all adjacent faces).The relative ordering of the timesteps at the shared vertices must
be consistent across the two faces (Figure S23c). Concretely, for two base facesAandBsharing verticesrandswe
require:

This ensures that the lifted translationally-invariant schedule satisfies the commutativity condition Equation (S26)
for all pairs of adjacentXandZstabilizers that lie on the border of the two coarse base facesAandB.
Timestep collision constraint (same-module faces).When two base faces additionally belong to the same module,
there exist fine stabilizers along the shared boundary whose support spans both faces’ territories (Figure S23d). Each
such stabilizer has a single ancilla that is coupled to data qubits in both faces, and this ancilla can participate in at
most one CNOT per timestep. This requires that the four timesteps at the shared vertices are all distinct:

iii. Constraint satisfaction via backtracking
We cast the scheduling problem as a binary CSP and solve it with a backtracking search enhanced by arc consistency.
Each face of the base tiling is a variable with a domain of 8 valid permutations (or more with shifts). Each shared edge
imposes the commutativity constraint (S31), and each shared edge between same-module faces additionally imposes
the collision constraint (S32). The solver uses the AC-3 algorithm to maintain arc consistency, pruning the domain
of each variable after every assignment. Variables are selected using the minimum remaining values (MRV) heuristic,
and values are ordered by the least constraining value (LCV) heuristic.
iv. Boundary-first fallback
For some base tilings and module partitions, no assignment satisfying both the commutativity constraint (S31)
and the collision constraint (S32) exists. In this case we relax the commutativity constraint on cross-module edges
(edges shared by faces in different modules), while retaining it on intra-module edges. The resulting schedule may
have inconsistent gate orderings across module boundaries, which we handle by using aboundary-firstextraction
strategy: the boundary CNOTs used for extraction of inter-moduleXstabilizers are implemented in earlier time
steps, scheduled via a bipartite edge coloring [13] to avoid time step collisions, before the remaining CNOTs proceed
with the standard lifted schedule. By measuring the inter-moduleXstabilizers first, this ensures that theXandZ

lifted schedule this leads to a total extraction circuit depth of six.

alternating) rounds with alternating forward-backward (fb) rounds for multi-rate hyperbolic modular surface codes at side
lengthL= 5 (r= 5 rounds). Distances are computed using a search for graphlike errors on the detector error model (using
the stim methodstim.Circuit.shortest graphlike error[14]). For Square modules, non-alternating rounds cap the circuit
distance tod circ = 6 for all tilings; round alternation alone is insufficient to raise the distance of the ZZ schedule on{4,6},

Rectangular modules on{4,6}and{4,10}codes, zero-shift collision-free scheduling is infeasible: the standard ZZ schedule
resolves collisions by introducing a shift offset (δ= 1), whereas the NZ schedule disablesδ= 1 and instead uses the boundary-

hyperbolic codes with up to 10 4 square modules or 10 4/2 rectangular modules), we find that the scheduler finds a
schedule in less than a minute. For base tilings with fewer than 10 3 faces, the scheduler finds a schedule in less than
a second.
vi. Hook errors, NZ and alternating schedules
In stabilizer measurement circuits for the Euclidean surface code, anXerror on anXstabilizer ancilla half-
way through the cycle propagates onto two data qubits (and likewise forZerrors in the middle of aZstabilizer
measurement). These types of errors are commonly known as hook errors [15]. For the rotated surface code, it is
known that some gate orderings can lead to hook errors that reduce the circuit distance below the code distance [16].
One can avoid distance-reducing hook errors in the Euclidean rotated surface code by choosing a schedule such
that the hook errors propagate orthogonal to the relevant logical operator. However, even a bad gate ordering, with

[PDF page 50]
31
FIG. S27. Comparison between standard and alternating forward-backward (fb) schedules for ZZ and NZ across all four tilings
for square modules (side lengthL= 5,r= 30 syndrome measurement rounds, averaged overXandZbases).
FIG. S28. Comparison between standard (non-alternating) and alternating forward–backward (fb) schedules for ZZ and NZ
across all four tilings for rectangular modules (ms= 2, side lengthL= 5,r= 30 syndrome measurement rounds, averaged
overXandZbases).
distance-reducing hook errors, can mitigate against some hook errors using analternating schedule[17]. If one starts
with a circuit with bad hook errors, such that the circuit distance is⌈d/2⌉for a distancedrotated surface code,
the circuit distance can be improved tod−1 by alternating the order of CNOT gates in consecutive cycles (i.e. use
a reversed ordering of the CNOT gates in odd cycles). This trick exploits the fact that problematic hook errors in
rotated surface code circuits correspond to diagonal errors in the spacetime decoding graph (the problematic errors
trigger a pair of detectors that are separated in time by one cycle) [17]. Furthermore, the shortest logical error in
such a circuit with hook errors (and without an alternating schedule) consists of a path of these diagonal errors that
spans⌈d/2⌉cycles.
For our modular hyperbolic surface codes, we would expect any schedule for our qubit layout (single bare ancilla

FIG. S29. Hook errors in a ZZ schedule of a modular hyperbolic surface code. The twoZerrors ina, both occurring between
time steps 1 and 2 of roundrof the syndrome extraction circuit, are equivalent to the threeZerrors inb, all occurring
immediately after time step 3 (data qubit errors occurring between roundrand roundr+ 1). Notice that the leftZerror in
aflips stabilizerαin roundr+ 1 and flips stabilizerβin roundr. This error is therefore diagonal in spacetime, however the
rightZerror inaalsoflips stabilizerβin roundr, canceling with the leftZerror’s syndrome. Therefore the combined effect
of the twoZerrors is a pair of stabilizersαandγoccurring in the same roundr+ 1, and equivalent to the three data qubit
errors occurring between roundsrandr+ 1 inb.

module partitions, averaged overXandZbases. We compare withd= 9 andd= 11 Euclidean rotated surface codes using a

FIG. S31. Comparison of alternating ZZ and NZ schedules, ford= 10,L= 5 modular hyperbolic surface codes with rectangular
module partitions.
per square plaquette in the bulk) to have distance-reducing hook errors. To understand why, we first note that any
homologically non-trivial cycle on the hyperbolic code supports both anXandaZlogical operator. Suppose a
low-weight logicalXoperator crosses a module of a modular hyperbolic surface code horizontally. Naively we might
try to avoid hooks just by using a standard “NZ” rotated surface code schedule (with a different schedule forX
andZplaquettes, see Figure S26), in the hope of aligning problematicXhook errors orthogonal to the minimum
weight logicalXoperator while keepingZhook errors parallel. However, there isalsoa low-weight logicalZoperator
crossing the module horizontally, so this approach will not work. Furthermore, for any tiling with a symmetry group
that includes the internal symmetries of the square module (e.g. those derived from{4, s}regular tilings), a minimum
weight logical operator that passes horizontally through the module can be mapped to another minimum weight logical
operator passing vertically across the module via an automorphism (a rotation centered on the module). Therefore we
would not expect a standard “NZ” schedule to avoid distance-reducing hook errors in our modular hyperbolic codes,
since it is impossible to align hook errors orthogonal to logical operators.
We confirmed the presence of hook errors numerically by finding the shortest graphlike error for some of the circuits
of interest, see Table S2. The shortest graphlike error gives an upper bound on the full circuit distance and is efficient
to compute (we used the stim methodstim.Circuit.shortest graphlike error[14]). We denote by “ZZ” the
standard translationally-invariant schedule for the modular hyperbolic surface codes we have introduced so far, where
we give every plaquette within a module the same CNOT ordering regardless of its basis (each “Z” denotes the shape
drawn by the CNOT order in each of theXandZbasis plaquettes). As shown in Table S2, using the ZZ schedule
ford= 10,L= 5 regular{4, s}modular hyperbolic surface codes, the circuit distance drops to 5, 6 or 7, well below
the full code distance of 10.
We mitigate against this distance reduction by making two further modifications to the circuit. First, we use an
alternating schedule by reversing the order of the CNOT gates that couple data qubits to ancilla qubits in the odd
rounds. As shown in Table S2, for the square module partition this boosts the distance from 6 to 8 for the{4,8}
code, however the distance remains 6 for the other three codes. For the rectangular module partition, the alternating
schedule increases the distance by one (up to 6 or 8, depending on the tiling). The reason that the alternating schedule
does not mitigate hook errors in the ZZ schedule as well as it does for the NZ schedule in the Euclidean surface code
is shown in Figure S29. Specifically, Figure S29ashows a pair ofZerrors that trigger only two detectors, both in the

[PDF page 52]
33
samecycle, and which are equivalent to threeZerrors on data qubits lying parallel with a logical operator. Since
chains of hook errors of this form are not diagonal in the spacetime detector error model (the chain does not traverse
time), the alternating schedule is unable to mitigate against it. Nevertheless, the alternating schedule does still lead
to a modest improvement in the logical error rate, see Figure S27 and Figure S28.
Despite the circuit distance dropping below the code distance even with the alternating schedule, the logical error
rate performance is still excellent, matching what we would expect for using a Euclidean surface code at a distance equal
to the hyperbolic code’s full code distance. This is shown in Figure S30, where we see that the ZZ alternating schedule
circuits outperformd= 9 Euclidean surface codes and almost matchd= 11 Euclidean surface code performance,
despite the circuit distance being 6 for the{4,6},{4,10}and{4,12}codes. Remarkably, our simulations of the
distance 20{4,10}modular hyperbolic surface code, shown in Figure 3 of the main text, show that its logical error
rate outperforms a distance-21 Euclidean surface code, despite the inevitable presence of hook errors that reduce the
circuit distance below the code distance of 20 (we used ZZ alternating multi-rate schedules for these simulations).
While the ZZ alternating schedule circuits already have good performance, we also implemented an “NZ” alternating
schedule, in order to further mitigate against hook errors, and in the hope that this might additionally translate into
improved logical error rates. Our NZ schedule adapts the standard rotated surface code extraction pattern [16] to
modular hyperbolic surface codes by assigning distinct CNOT interaction orders to theXandZplaquettes within one
base face of the original hyperbolic tiling, see Figure S26. In cyclic order around each fine-grained square plaquette,
Xstabilizers follow an “N”-shaped schedule (traversing vertically first, i.e. time stepsP X := [3,1,0,2] for corners
[NW,NE,SE,SW]), whileZstabilizers follow a “Z”-shaped schedule (traversing horizontally first, e.g.P Z := [3,2,0,1]).
This NZ circuit does not have the problematic, purely “spatial”, pair of hook errors shown in Figure S29, allowing
the alternating schedule to be more effective at hook error mitigation in the bulk of a base face.
To lift this schedule to the full, fine-grained modular hyperbolic code, each face of the base tiling is assigned a
jointX/Zschedule pair (g·P X , g·P Z), obtained by applying the same dihedral symmetryg∈D 4 (one of the 4
rotations or reflections) simultaneously to both seed schedules (each a permutation of{0,1,2,3}). This preserves the
relative orientation betweenXandZschedules across the fine grid within each base face. The global schedule is
found using the same backtracking CSP solver described in Section S4 B: each base face represents a variable with a
domain of the 8 jointD 4 permutations, with edge commutativity constraints adapted to the NZ schedule to ensure
Equation (S26) holds forXandZstabilizers spanning adjacent base modules. For adjacent base faces within a
module, rather than using only the time step collision constraint (Equation (S32)), we instead enforce that different
base faces within the same module have translationally invariant schedules (i.e. allXplaquettes within a module have
the same translationally invariant schedule, and similarly forZ). Since we wanted to enforce translational invariance
within a module, we fixed the shiftδ= 0 (rather than falling back toδ= 1 as we did for the ZZ schedule). However
when theδ= 0 NZ scheduling fails, we do still use the boundary first fallback.
The circuit distances of the NZ schedule, with and without using an alternating schedule, are shown in Table S2.
Across the codes benchmarked, the NZ alternating schedule has the best circuit distance among the different schedules,
as expected, typically achieving a circuit distance of 8 (for a distance 10 code). This corresponds to losing a distance
of 1 across each of the two modules that wraps around a handle of the hyperbolic surface. When the boundary fallback
is used, the distance drops to 7. This is expected, since the boundary fallback un-interleaves the module boundary
stabilizer measurement CNOTs in one basis, so a hook error that would have been diagonal in spacetime becomes
purely spacelike (and as such the alternating schedule cannot mitigate against it). Regardless, as shown in Figure S30
and Figure S31, we find that the NZ alternating schedule has essentially the same logical error rate performance as
the ZZ alternating schedule, despite the different circuit distances. This further confirms that, while hook errors are
present in these modular hyperbolic surface code circuits, they are so improbable that they have no noticeable impact
on logical error rates at practical physical error rates.
vii. Multi-rate measurement schedules
Rather than measuring all stabilizers in every cycle, we measure some higher-weight stabilizers less frequently.
Concretely, any stabilizer in the code associated with a vertex of the original base hyperbolic tiling is measured once
every three cycles. As an example, consider a square module partition of a{4, s}regular hyperbolic surface code. In
the modular hyperbolic code, each degreesvertex in the base tiling lifts to a weight-sstabilizer in the semi-hyperbolic
code, which is shared by thessquare modules at their corners (see Figure S20afor the stabilizer structure of a{4, s},
L= 5 modular hyperbolic code, as an example). Preparing the cat state with the linear encoding circuit we use
requires⌈ s
2 ⌉layers of inter-module gates. We will refer to these high-weight stabilizers as “corner” stabilizers. Since
the corner stabilizers require both high-weight cat statesandthe use of inter-module couplers, which are expected
to be noisier and slower, they are therefore much slower to measure than the weight-four stabilizers fully supported
within a module. If we were to measure all stabilizers every cycle, the data qubits would be idling while the corner

FIG. S32. Timeline of a multi-rate syndrome extraction circuit for modular hyperbolic codes. Full-rate stabilizers (including
intra-module bulk stabilizers and inter-module edge stabilizers extracted via two-qubit Bell pairs) are extracted and measured in
every cycle (t= 0,1,2, . . .), with detectorsD t comparing syndromes between consecutive cycles (Dt =S t ⊕S t−1). Subsampled
corner stabilizers, which have higher weight and require multi-qubit cat states with preparation and measurement depth
scaling with the cat size across multiple module seams, are executed across a 3-cycle sequence (tmod 3∈ {0,1,2}): Phase 1
(tmod 3 = 0) prepares the multi-qubit cat state across seams while data qubits remain idle; Phase 2 (tmod 3 = 1) applies
local data-ancilla CNOT gates, synchronized with the full-rate schedules; Phase 3 (tmod 3 = 2) unencodes the cat state and
measures the root ancilla (syndromeS t) alongside leaf flag ancillas. Subsampled detectors compare syndromes across ∆t= 3
cycles (Dt =S t ⊕S t−3, e.g.D 5 =S 5 ⊕S 2).
stabilizers’ cat states are being prepared.
Instead, we measure the higher-weight, non-local stabilizers less frequently [18, 19]. Concretely, in ourmulti-rate
circuit, the corner stabilizers are measured once every three cycles, while all remaining stabilizers are measured every
cycle. A timeline of a multi-rate syndrome extraction circuit is shown in Figure S32. The “full-rate” stabilizers
(all stabilizers aside from corner stabilizers) are measured in full every cycle. The “subsampled” stabilizers (corner
stabilizers) are measured across three cycles. In a cycletfor whichtmod 3 = 0, the ancillas of the subsampled
stabilizers are reset and inter-module gates are used to unitarily prepare a cat state on the shared ancilla qubits. In
cycles wheretmod 3 = 1, local CNOT (or CZ) gates within a module are used to couple data and ancilla qubits,
synchronized for all stabilizers (subsampled and full-rate). Finally, for cyclestmod 3 = 2, the inverse of the unitary
cat-state encoding circuit is applied to the ancilla qubits of the subsampled stabilizers, followed by measurement of
all the ancillas. One of these ancillas is used to measure the stabilizer, while the remainings−1 ancillas are flag
qubits. We still use the alternating (forward-backward) schedule for the multi-rate circuits by reversing the order of
the data-ancilla CNOT (or CZ) gates in cycles for whichtmod 2 = 1.
In Figure S33 we show simulations that compare the multi-rate and uniform rate syndrome extraction circuits for
small regular{4, s}modular hyperbolic surface codes withL= 5 and fors∈ {6,8,10,12}. We find that the multi-rate
circuits, for which corner stabilizers are measured every three cycles, have better (lower) logical error rates than the
uniform circuits for which every stabilizer is measured each cycle. The improvement increases with the stabilizer
weights. This improvement can be explained by the fact that the uniform schedule requires the data qubits to idle
while the corner stabilizers’ cat states are encoded and unencoded.
S5. SYMMETRICAL CANONICAL LOGICAL BASIS FOR HYPERBOLIC CODES
The logical operators for hyperbolic color and surface codes are associated with the topology of the underlying
closed hyperbolic tiling. In particular, they are related to the homologically non-trivial cycles of the surface.
A closedorientablesurface Σ g of genusghas 2gindependent homologically non-trivial cycles and these cycles can be
grouped intogpairs, calledsymplectic pairs, in order to define acanonical homology basisB={(α 1, β1), . . . ,(αg, βg)}.
We define theintersection number⟨α, β⟩ ∈F 2 of two non-trivial cyclesα,βas their number of geometric crossings
modulo 2 and we say that the two cyclescrosswhen this number is 1 (when they geometrically cross each other an
odd number of times). In a canonical homology basis, symplectic pairs cross an odd number of times only with their
partner cycle and cross an even number of times with any cycle from another symplectic pair. Figure S34 illustrates
an example for an arbitrary genus-3 surface.
On a tiled surface, a cycle is a closed loop of edges. The cycle is homologically trivial if the edges of the cycle
form the boundary of a set of tiling faces. Otherwise, it is homologically non-trivial (it wraps around a handle of
the surface). Since stabilizers are defined on the faces of the underlying hyperbolic tiling for hyperbolic color and
surface codes, homologically trivial cycles of the hyperbolic tiling correspond to the stabilizers of the code, while

constructed from a non-trivial cycle differs between color and surface codes, as we explain in the rest of this section.
FIG. S34. Canonical homology basis on an orientable genus-3 surface Σ 3. Each handleisupports a symplectic pair of cycles
(αi, βi). Partner cycles in each pair cross each other an odd number of times and cross cycles of other symplectic pairs an even
number of times.
A. Color Code Logical Basis
For hyperbolic color codes, we associate the logical operators with the homologically non-trivial cycles on the primal
three-colorable tilingT(see Figure S35), using the colored structure of the edges. Every edge of the color code tiling
separates two faces of the same color, and we assign it that color through the mapc:E→ {R, G, B}, so that red
faces are separated by red edges, and likewise for green and blue. A homologically non-trivial cycleαconsists of
edges of all three colors, whereas a cycle whose edges use only two colors bounds a set of faces and is homologically
trivial [1, 2].
Following Ref. [1], a logical Pauli operator of the color code is obtained from a non-trivial cycleαby restricting its
support to a single color. For each colorC∈ {R, G, B}, themonochromatic substring
αC :={e∈α|c(e) =C}(S33)
is the set of color-Cedges ofα, and we writeV(α C)⊆Vfor the set of vertices incident to an edge inα C. The

This restriction to color-Cedges is equivalent to the shrunk-lattice construction of Ref. [1]. There, a color-Cstring
is defined on the shrunk lattice, obtained by contracting every color-Cface ofTto a single vertex and keeping only
the color-Cedges. We define logical operators on monochromatic substrings throughout, and it underlies the weighted
shortest-cycle search we use to compute color-code distances in Section S10 A.
Monochromatic substring operators ofXorZtype supported on the same non-trivial cycle are not all independent.

Starting from a canonical homology basisB={(α 1, β1), . . . ,(αg, βg)}ofgsymplectic pairs and defining the Pauli
operators on their monochromatic substrings using two colors yields a symplectic basis for thek= 4glogical qubits of
the hyperbolic color code. Furthermore, fine-graining a hyperbolic tiling with a Euclidean tiling does not change the
topology of the surface, therefore, we can always search for a canonical homology basis on the primal hyperbolic tiling
and then lift its non-trivial cycles to the fine-grained semi-hyperbolic tiling (we describe the lifting procedure in detail
in Section S9). Fine-graining does, however, increase the length of the non-trivial cycles, and hence the number of
qubits in the support of the logical operators, boosting the code distance. Figure S36 illustrates this for a symplectic
pair (α, β) of the 8.8.8-32 semi-hyperbolic color code: the same pair of non-trivial cycles is shown for fine-graining
parametersL= 1,L= 2 andL= 3. AsLincreases, each cycle is lifted to a longer path on the fine-grained tiling, so
that the logical operators in its support grow in weight while their homology class is preserved.
In our work we require an additional structure from the canonical logical basis. We construct it using the symmetries
of the tiling, particularly the orientation-preserving automorphisms Aut+(T) =G + =⟨ρ, σ⟩that correspond to global
rotations and translations of the tiling. We restrict to the orientation-preserving symmetries since we can realize
these symmetries as fault-tolerant automorphism circuits with minimal additional seam connectivity (described in
Section S7). The module partition we focus on is quadrilateral (K= 2) modules for which the modules are in
correspondence with group elements ofG +. Our aim is therefore asymmetricbasis, in which the homologically
non-trivial cycles are related to one another by the action ofG +, so that the corresponding logical operators can be
mapped onto one another by these automorphism circuits. We build the basis fromseed cycles. First we enumerate
a pool of short seed-cycle candidates: we perform a graph search from every vertex of the tiling, find and classify
each non-trivial cycle by its homology class, and keep the shortest representative of each class. We order the cycles in
the candidate pool by their length, starting from the systole which is the shortest non-trivial cycle of the tiling. We
then take the orbit of a seed cycle underG +. Rotating the tiling maps the seed to other non-trivial cycles of equal
length, sweeping it across many homology classes and giving a pool of equal-length candidates from which we search
forgcycles that pair up into a symplectic basis. We try the systole first, then proceed through successive seed cycles
in order of increasing weight, until the orbits together contain a complete basis. We give a complete account of how
the symmetric canonical homology basis is constructed in Section S9, explaining non-trivial cycle enumeration, the
generation of seed-cycle orbits under the orientation-preserving groupG + =⟨ρ, σ⟩, and the symplectic-pair search.
Since the modules of the quadrilateral partition correspond to elements ofG +, the symmetric structure of the
canonical logical basis has a natural description on the module partition, which we illustrate in Figure S37 and
Figure S38 for the 8.8.8-32 color code. Recall from Section S3 A ii that each quadrilateral module is formed by pairing
adjacent triangles of the base triangular tiling across an edge of one chosen color (here the red edges). Removing
the dual edges of that color leaves a reduced dual graph whose remaining (blue and green) dual edges lie along the

[PDF page 56]
37
module boundaries, so a non-trivial cycle can be represented by its dual cycle running along these boundaries. Lifting
a primal cycle to the fine-grained tiling, it follows the module boundaries along the path traced by this dual cycle,
and the corresponding logical operator can be supported on either side of the boundary. Furthermore, the non-trivial
dual cycles can be labeled by group elements: associating a chosen seed cycle with the identity elemente, its orbit
underG + produces the remaining cycles in its orbit, each labeled by the group elementg∈G + that maps the seed
onto it. The group elements corresponding to the non-trivial cycles that form a canonical basis are shown as colored
nodes in Figure S38 which makes explicit how every cycle in the basis is obtained from the seed by a global rotation of
the tiling. For the 8.8.8-32 color code (genusg= 3) the orbit of asingleseed cycle underG + produces six non-trivial
cycles that form all three symplectic pairs, so this one seed suffices to construct the full logical basis. The dashed
lines in Figure S38a show the partner cycles of each symplectic pair and Figure S38b illustrates the right Cayley
distance between them. If the ribbon extractor is attached to the seed cycleγ, the cyclexγis brought under it by
the automorphismx −1, so switching from measuringxγto measuringyγrequires the automorphismy −1x. Its word
length|x −1y|in the generators{ρ ±1, σ±1}is the distance betweenxandyin the right Cayley graph ofG +. This
is the minimum number of elementary movesρ, ρ −1, σ, σ−1 needed to bring a cycle under the extractor when it is
measuring the partner cycle, and determines the depth of the automorphism circuits required to switch between the
cycles within a symplectic pair. Note that this in general differs from the left Cayley distance|yx −1|, which is the
length of the automorphismyx −1 mappingxγontoyγ. We discuss the general case of an extractor attached to a
cyclehγin Section S8 (see Equation (S47)).
The dual cycle described above runsalongthe module boundaries, obtained by removing the red edges and searching
the reduced blue–green dual graph. An alternative is to search on the red edges instead, giving a dual cycle that
passes through theinteriorof the modules in its support, as shown in Figure S39. The modules are still defined by
pairing triangles across red edges, but now each module crossed by the cycle is split into its two constituent triangular
modules along that red edge. This exposes an internal module boundary through which the logical operator can be
accessed, which is convenient for logical measurement with the ribbon extractor of Section S6, since it separates the
seam used for logical readout from the seams used for memory and automorphisms. Only the modules in the support
of the seed cycle are split in this way, while all other modules remain quadrilateral.
A single seed orbit does not always yield a complete basis. When it does not, we may accept a partial basis, in
which fewer thangsymplectic pairs, and hence fewer than the fullk= 4glogical qubits, are covered by the orbit of
a single cycle. This may sometimes be a favorable trade-off. The covered logicals are all related to one seed cycle
by automorphisms, and can be measured with a single ribbon extractor (Section S6), with the remaining logicals
handled separately. For the 8.8.8-400 code, for example, one seed cycle covers 100 of the 104 logical qubits, so a single
extractor suffices for 100 logical qubits. To search for a complete basis, we can enlarge the candidate pool by taking
the combined orbits of pairs of seed cycles, then triplets, and so on. Table S3 reports how many seed cycles are used
for a range of 8.8.8 color codes and how many logicals their orbits generate.
To summarize, the canonical basis of symplectic pairs is built from the orbits of seed cycles, and this structure
significantly simplifies logical measurement. Each seed cycle and its orbit underG + is handled by a single ribbon
extractor (Section S6), which measures the logical operators on the seed cycle, while the automorphism circuits
(Section S7) map the images in the orbit back onto it. The logical operators are associated with non-trivial cycles,
the smallest of which is the systole, the shortest non-trivial cycle of the tiling. Since the systole scales asO(log|G|),
this upper bounds the weight of the shortest logical operators toO(log|G|) base tiles (see Section S10). After fine-
graining, these tiles are grouped into modules, so the shortest logical operators are supported on onlyO(logM) of
theMmodules, with weightO(LlogM) at fine-graining parameterL. Each such operator can moreover be made
to lie on the module boundaries (Figure S37) or cut through the middle of the module which is then split into two
triangular modules (Figure S39). Logical measurement then requires only minimal additional seam connectivity, never
addressing qubits in the bulk, and the modules themselves remain planar.
