B. Surface Code Logical Basis
Unlike color codes, hyperbolic surface codes are not self-dual, and theirX- andZ-type operators are supported
on different structures. In the two-colorable representation, the stabilizers are supported on the faces of the tiling,
with the two face colors carrying the two stabilizer types, e.g. red faces supportX-stabilizers and blue faces support
Z-stabilizers. To describe the logical operators it is convenient to pass to the equivalent cell complex representation,
obtained by collapsing every red face to a vertex and associating data qubits with the edges connecting the new
vertices, as illustrated in Figure S40. In the resulting cell complexC, the 2-cells (faces) supportZ-stabilizers, the
1-cells (edges) support data qubits, and the 0-cells (vertices) supportX-stabilizers. This is captured by the chain
complex
C2
∂2
− − →C1
∂1
− − →C0,(S36)
whereH Z =∂ T
2 andH X =∂ 1.
OnC, homologically trivial cycles are products ofZ-stabilizers while homologically non-trivial cycles give logical Z
operators. On the dual cell complexC ∗, trivial cycles are products ofX-stabilizers while non-trivial cycles give logical
Xoperators. A logical Zon a cycle and a logical Xon a cocycle anticommute precisely when the cycle and cocycle
cross, i.e. when their intersection number is 1. To build the canonical basis we takegsymplectic pairs of non-trivial
cycles (α 1, β1), . . . ,(αg, βg) onC. Eachα i, βi carries a logical Z, giving 2gmutually commuting Zoperators. The
anticommuting Xpartners are the dual cocycles:α ∗
i crossesβ i andβ ∗
i crossesα i, so (Z βi , X α∗
i ) and (Z αi , X β∗
i ) form
the 2ganticommuting logical qubit pairs, givingk= 2glogical qubits on a genus-gsurface.
Figure S40 shows a semi-hyperbolic surface code together with its cell complex description. For the canonical basis,
however, we work with the base quadrilateral tilingT □: we search for a canonical homology basis there, related by
automorphisms as for the color codes, and then interpret theX- andZ-type logical operators by relating the cycles
to the cell complex tiling. The combinatorial problem we solve is therefore the same for hyperbolic color and surface
codes, and is further explained in Section S9.
FIG. S40. Cell complex description of a semi-hyperbolic surface code.(a)The two-colorable tiling (T □
L )∗ with red and blue
faces.(b)Cell complexCoverlaid on the two-colorable (T □
L )∗ tiling. Red faces are shrunk to vertices and new edges connect
the new vertices. Qubits are now associated with edges of the tiling.(c)Cell complex tilingT cell:Z-stabilizers are associated
with faces, data qubits with edges andX-stabilizers are associated with vertices. Examples of weight-4 and weight-6 stabilizer
generators are shown. Homologically trivial cycles ofCare products ofZ-stabilizers and non-trivial cycles give logical Z
operators, while on the dual cell complexC ∗ trivial cycles are products ofX-stabilizers and non-trivial cycles give logical X
operators.
S6. RIBBON EXTRACTOR FOR LOGICAL MEASUREMENT
We utilize a technique called code surgery, which is a generalization of surface code lattice surgery [20], to access
the logical information stored in our modular hyperbolic memory. This access is provided in the form of fault-tolerant
measurements of logical Pauli operators, which can be utilized to implement different computational primitives such
as teleportation. The high-level idea can be summarized as follows: given a logical operator we intend to measure,
we initialize a set of ancilla data qubits and define a new stabilizer code called thedeformed code(supported on the
original data qubits and ancilla data qubits). In the deformed code, the operator we intend to measure (which is
of Hamming weight at least the code distanced) will become a product of new stabilizer checks (which are often

[PDF page 62]
43
of constant Hamming weight). Therefore, by measuring the constant-weight checks of the deformed code, we also
measure the high-weight logical operator of the original code. Fault tolerance of this procedure is then guaranteed
by guaranteeing the distance of the deformed code is not reduced and by repeating the measurement of the deformed
code for sufficiently many rounds (typicallydrounds).
The space- and time-costs of surgery procedures are determined by the ancilla systems and the associated deformed
codes. Construction of these systems and codes has been the focus of a line of recent works [21–27], for which we refer
readers to section 3 of Ref. [26] as a brief review. For our semi-hyperbolic codes, we construct surgery gadgets called
ribbon extractorsusing techniques from [21, 22, 26]. In this section, we detail the construction of these extractors,
the deformed codes we use for measurements, and their architectural utilities. Our presentation will be specific to
ribbon extractors on hyperbolic color codes with 8.8.8 tiling. The same methods can be applied to hyperbolic color
codes with other tilings or hyperbolic surface codes.
A. Construction of the Extractor Systems
FIG. S41. Choosing a seed loop that contains a logical representative in the semi-hyperbolic color code.
We begin by finding a non-trivial cycle in the base code. This cycle and its associated logical operators will be
supported on the boundary qubits of a collection of modules. One such cycle of interest is given in Figure S41; we
will refer to such a cycle (given in cyan) as a seed loop. The highlighted set of edges represent a set of qubits where a
given logical operator is supported, such qubits all belong to stabilizer checks that are at the boundaries of modules

[PDF page 63]
44
forming the code. Note, there is a freedom in choosing a logical representative residing along a seed loop, namely one
can choose equivalent logical representatives to be on either, or both sides, of the seed loop. Logical representatives of
the same Pauli type and color (as will be described below) will be logically equivalent. See Figure S42 for an example
of a set of connected edges on either side of the seed loop that can harbor logical operators.
Along each seed loop there exist four different independent logical Pauli operators, two of bothXandZtype,
all of which commute. These logical operators can be chosen to live along the edges of a given color, that is they
form a continuous set of edges connecting faces of the same color. As a result, we can label the resulting logical
operators as X B, X R, Z B, Z R (where theBandRlabels refer to the blue and red logicals, we forgo the superscript
label from Section S5 A as we are focusing on a single loop). Note, that while there is also a green logical, it would
be equivalent to the product of the logicals of the other two colors X G ≃ X BX R.
We will denote the stabilizers that straddle both sides of the seed loop, and as such share data qubits between
more than one module, asboundary stabilizers. Note that all the weight-6 boundary stabilizers have half of their data
qubit support on one module, and half on another module on either side of the boundary seed loop. For the weight-8
cornerstabilizers, the stabilizer can either have a 4-4 or 2-6 split on either side of the seed loop.
Ancilla Qubits
Ancilla Checks
Extractor Modules
FIG. S42. Extractor connectivity for measuring logical operators along a seed loop. Solid lines represent in-module connectivity
while dashed lines represent inter-module connectivity. Surgery checks on the first row of surgery gadget can couple to the
qubits of the corresponding color in the base code via inter-module connection to the associated base code ancilla measurement
qubits. Each shaded extractor module box corresponds to an individual module which are connected in a 1D-chain manner to
one another along with the base code, thus forming a ribbon extractor.
We will now describe the connectivity of the extractor gadget (see Figure S42) that will be used to implement
logical measurements via code surgery. We will detail the exact stabilizers to be measured for a given desired logical
in the subsequent section.
The ribbon extractor consists of layers of ancilla check qubits and ancilla data qubits. Note that a surgery check,
which is a stabilizer we use to perform surgery measurement, can be implemented by one or more ancilla check qubits.
For readability, in the rest of this section we always differentiate between ‘checks’ and ‘check qubits’. There will be a
total of 2ℓ+ 1 layers, for an integerℓto be specified later. The first layer of the extractor is constructed as follows.
•For every boundary stabilizer, we will associate a surgery check that can access a subset of qubits belonging
to the corresponding stabilizer through the associated ancilla check qubits (white circles). In Figure S42, these
surgery check qubits are colored according to the colors of the boundary stabilizers. For reasons which we will
explain later, the surgery checks associated to the blue boundary stabilizers are implemented using two ancilla
check qubits, as well as the surgery checks associated to the corner stabilizers. We will refer to these surgery
checks as ‘wide’. The remaining ‘standard’ surgery checks are each implemented with one ancilla check qubit.
•Each surgery check acts on two ancilla data qubits on the first layer. Surgery checks corresponding to neighboring
boundary stabilizers share one such qubit.

[PDF page 64]
45
In particular, the first layer of the ribbon extractor is a closed loop of alternating surgery checks (which are further
connected to check qubits in the code) and ancilla data qubits.
The second layer of the extractor is constructed as follows.
•For each standard surgery check from the first layer, we add one ancilla data qubit of support. For each wide
surgery check from the first layer, we add two ancilla data qubits of support.
•For each ancilla data qubit from the first layer, we add one ancilla check qubit (colored orange in Figure S42).
This orange check will act on the data qubit from the first layer, as well as two adjacent ancilla qubits in the
second layer.
•For each wide surgery check from the first layer, we add one orange check qubit acting on the two corresponding
ancilla data qubits in the second layer.
Similar to the first layer, the second layer is also a closed loop of alternating ancilla checks and data qubits, connected
to the first layer in an almost 1-to-1 fashion.
Once we construct the first two layers, we repeat them forℓ−1 times such that the (2i+ 1)-th layer is connected
to the 2i-th layer in an almost 1-to-1 fashion. Finally, we add one final copy of the first layer as the (2ℓ+ 1)-th layer,
and connect it to the 2ℓ-th layer. This completes the construction of the ribbon extractor [28].
We can calculate the space-cost of ribbon extractors (total number of ancilla data and check qubits) from the
parameters of corresponding seed loops. Suppose the chosen seed loop traverses the boundaries ofttriangles. A
boundary of a triangle hasL+ 1 adjacent plaquettes, and a seed loop traversestLplaquettes,tof which are corner
plaquettes. We will choose the seed loops such that the corner plaquettes are always green or red. As a result, out of
thetLplaquettes traversedtof them are blue, and none of them are corners. On the first layer of a ribbon extractor,
we therefore havetL+ 2tancilla check qubits (accounting for the blue and corner wide checks) andtLancilla data
qubits. On the second layer, we havetL+ 2tancilla data qubits andtL+ 2tancilla (orange) check qubits, because
each wide check from the first layer needs an orange check qubit acting on its two adjacent data qubits in the second
layer. In total, on the first two layers, we needt(2L+ 4) ancilla check qubits andt(2L+ 2) ancilla data qubits. We
repeat these two layers forℓ−1 times, and finish with another copy of the first layer. As a result, the total number
of ancilla qubits in a ribbon extractor is
Space cost of ribbon extractor = [t(2L+ 4) +t(2L+ 2)]·ℓ+t(2L+ 2) =t((4L+ 6)ℓ+ 2L+ 2).(S37)
As we will discuss in Section S6 C, for a code of distancedwe will chooseℓ=⌈d/2⌉ −1 to ensure that the surgery
procedure has phenomenological fault distanced. With this choice, the above equation simplifies to
Space cost of ribbon extractor =
(
t((2L+ 3)(d−1)−1) fordeven,
t((2L+ 3)d−1) fordodd. (S38)
We see that the leading factor is 2tLd. The example code we used for Figure S42 has parameters [[6400,104,30]], with
L= 4,t= 10 and total qubit count 14400. Putting these numbers in, we see that the qubit count of a ribbon extractor
is up to 3180, which is roughly 22.1% of that of the code. ChoosingL= 3 instead of 4, we get a [[3600,104,22]] code,
with total qubit count 8400 and the same seed loop wheret= 10. The associated ribbon extractor uses up to 1880
qubits, which is roughly 22.4% of that of the code. It should be noted that the choice of wide checks is motivated
for tilings ofL >3 and as such may be suboptimal forL= 3, therefore the associated overhead could potentially be
reduced.
B. Logical Measurement via Surgery
Given the ribbon extractor, to measure a logical operator P C, we will define a new stabilizer group supported on the
code and extractor. We first describe this stabilizer group for measuring anXlogical operator of any color X C, the
case forZlogical operators will follow by symmetry. We will return to measuring the product of anXandZlogical
later.

FIG. S43. Measurement of a blue logicalXoperator, X B. (a) Surgery checks that are red or green have support on the
corresponding data qubits on one side of the seed loop. Blue surgery checks only retain support in the ancilla system.
(b)Zboundary stabilizers are modified to gain support in the surgery ancilla qubits in order to commute with the new surgery
checks.
Prepare all ancilla data qubits in the surgery gadget in|0⟩. To measure the X C1 (colorC 1):
•Choose one side of the seed loop. On the first layer of the ribbon extractor, for every surgery check of the
other two colorsC 2, C3, the surgery check will haveXsupport on the qubits on the chosen side of the seed loop
belonging to the associated boundary stabilizer. It will also haveXsupport on the (three to four) neighboring
ancilla data qubits in the extractor modules. For the surgery check of colorC 1, only measure theXsupport on
the extractor module (no support on the data qubits of the original code), see Figure S43(a) for an example of
the support for measuring X B.
•As the surgery checks on the first layer haveXsupport on the data qubits of the code, there will beZstabi-

[PDF page 66]
47
lizers, namely the boundaryZstabilizers, whose original support will anti-commute with these measurements.
To restore commutativity, they will have to gain support on the ancilla data qubits on the first layer, see
Figure S43(b). They will gain support on a given ancilla data qubit in the first layer if their corresponding
neighboringXsurgery check had been extended into the data qubits of the code.
•For subsequent odd layers, the surgery checks (colored red, green or blue) act on adjacent data qubits byX.
Note that the regular surgery checks have weight 4, while the wide surgery checks have weight 6.
•On the even layers of the ribbon extractor, for each orange ancilla check qubit, we define aZstabilizer that
acts on its adjacent data qubits. TheseZchecks have weight 2 or 4, and they commute with the surgery checks
defined above.
We now prove the following crucial claim.
Claim 1.The product of all surgery checks (from all odd layers) equals theXlogical operator of colorC 1 on the base
code (X C1).
Proof.We begin with a few definitions: we say an edge is adjacent to a given face if it is one of the boundary edges
of the face, while an edge is incident to a face if it touches one and only one of the vertices of the face. We have
chosen a path for the logical operators which is a continuous set of edges, alternating in color (where the color of an
edge is defined in the standard way for color codes as an edge incident to two faces of that color). Each edge can be
characterized in one of the following two ways:
1. An edge is adjacent to a single boundary face and is not incident to any other boundary face.
2. An edge is adjacent to a single boundary face and is incident to one or two other boundary faces.
Without loss of generality, let us assume we want to measure a blue (B) logicalXoperator. We want to argue that
by multiplying the surgery checks, of which the red (R) and green (G) colored surgery checks on layer one act on the
base code non-trivially, the resulting support will be the support of all the blue edges along the logical path.
Let us first show that any qubit belonging to a blue edge will have support. We will work this out case by case
among the edges above.
1. If a blue edge is only adjacent to a single boundary face, that face must be of colorRorGand as such the
corresponding surgery check on layer one will have support on this edge. Moreover, since it is not incident to
any other boundary face, this support cannot be canceled out by another surgery check.
2. Similarly to the case above, the support of the edge will have to be measured by theRorGsurgery check to
which it is adjacent. Any other face to which this edge is incident must be a blue face (since this is a blue edge).
Since the blue surgery checks do not act on the base code, the support on these blue edges cannot be canceled
out.
Next, we must show that any qubit along the logical boundary path that does not belong to aBedge, that is the
endpoint of anRandGedge, must not have support in the overall measurement. There are two cases for such qubits:
1. The only boundary stabilizer to which they belong is blue, as such the measurement has no support on such
qubits.
2. The qubit belongs to two boundary stabilizers. Given the qubit lies at the end point of red and green edges,
the corresponding boundary stabilizers to which it belongs must be red and green, and the two corresponding
surgery checks must cancel out its support.
Therefore we have established that on the data qubits in the base code, the product of all the surgery checks is
supported exactly on the blueXlogical operator. On the ancilla data qubits in the ribbon extractor, we note that
every ancilla data qubit is acted on by exactly two surgery checks, whose Pauli action cancels on the qubit. We
conclude that the product of all the surgery checks equals the blueXlogical operator.
Corollary 1.Measurement of theZlogical of colorC 1 is identical in construction to that of theXlogical except
that the support of the surgery checks on the code qubits is ofZ-type (however, it remains ofX-type on the ancilla
qubits). As a result, only theXboundary stabilizers are now deformed to gainZsupport on the ancilla qubits.
Corollary 2.Measurement of theYlogical of colorC 1 is identical in construction to that of theXlogical except
that the support of the surgery checks on the code qubits is ofY-type (however, it remains ofX-type on the ancilla
qubits). As a result, both theXandZboundary stabilizers are now deformed to gainZsupport on the ancilla qubits.

FIG. S44. Measurement of X BZ R. Green surgery checks gain support ofXandZtype on the data qubits on respective sides
of the seed loop, while red (blue) surgery checks only gainX(Z) support. All surgery checks only haveXsupport on the

In order to measure the product of two logical operators of different types, we combine the technique of measuring
each logicalXandZ. Namely, the surgery checks will gainXsupport on one side of the seed loop andZsupport on
the other (depending on the color), see Figure S44 for an example. The original boundary stabilizers would have to
be deformed in a similar manner to guarantee commutativity.
Corollary 3.In order to measure the product operator X C1 Z C2, that is the product of logicalXof colorC 1 and
logicalZof colorC 2, all surgery checks on layer one of colorsC 2 andC 3 will haveXsupport on the data qubits on
one side of the seed loop, while surgery checks on layer one of colorC 1 andC 3 will haveZsupport on the data qubits
on the other side of the seed loop. All surgery checks will have their associatedXsupport on the ancilla data qubits
in the extractor. The originalXandZboundary stabilizers of the code will gainZsupport on the ancilla qubits
following the same procedure as the measurement of X C1 and Z C2 individually.
C. Fault Tolerance
Given the extractor system and the deformed codes, we now show that the surgery protocol is fault-tolerant.
Specifically, we will show that the deformed codes have the same distancedas the original code. Following standard
arguments [22, 23], this implies that the surgery protocol has phenomenological fault distanced.
In order to prove that the deformed codes have sufficient distances, one must show that the original logical operators
of the code cannot be weight-reduced by multiplying by the new stabilizers of the deformed code or any new gauge
logical operators. We will begin by arguing that no new gauge logical qubits are introduced by the ribbon extractor
before arguing that the new stabilizers of the code cannot reduce the weight of a logical operator to be below that of
the distance of the original code.
Claim 2.The ribbon extractor measures a target logical operatorPin the base code without introducing any other
new logical qubits.
Proof.From our previous calculations around Equation (S37), we can count the number of new data qubits and
stabilizers introduced by the ribbon extractor. There areℓ+ 1 odd layers, each withtLnew data qubits andtLnew
stabilizers, andℓeven layers, each witht(L+ 2) new data qubits andt(L+ 2) new stabilizers. It is straightforward
to verify the following claim of independence: there is no product of deformed code stabilizers, with a non-trivial
combination of extractor stabilizers, that results in identity. Therefore, the number of logical degrees of freedom in
the overall system cannot be more thank.

[PDF page 68]
49
However, this is not quite the end of the story. As when we take the product of all stabilizers whose support is of
Xtype in the ribbon extractor, the resulting support is the logical operatorPfrom the base code to be measured,
therefore we explicitly have eliminated one of the logical degrees of freedom of the original code! Equivalently, the
rank of stabilizers should have increased by one more than the number of new stabilizers. We can observe this directly.
In the original code, theZstabilizers are redundant in the sense that the product of all blue and red/green stabilizers
give identity. However, in the deformed code, the product of all blue and red/green (original and deformed) stabilizers
equals the operator that is allZon the data qubits of the first layer of the ribbon extractor [29]. Therefore, this
degree of redundancy is removed as we deform the code and we conclude that the deformed code hask−1 logical
degrees of freedom.
Claim 3.Given a thickening of at leastd/2layers, the distance of the deformed code through surgery measurement
remains distanced.
Proof.Suppose we are given some logical operatorQof weightwin the base code, we would like to show that the
weight of this operator cannot be reduced to be less than the distancedby multiplying by the new surgery stabilizers
of the code.
Let us first consider the case when the ancilla system is added to measure a blue (B) logicalX. Given none of the
newly introducedZstabilizers in the ribbon extractor are supported on the base code, they cannot contribute to a
distance reduction of theZlogical operator. Moreover, multiplying by any deformedZstabilizer will only end up
increasing the support of the logicalZoperator, as such any logicalZcannot see their distance reduced by a ribbon
extractor built for measuring a logicalXoperator.
We will consider the set of neighboringR l, G c, R r surgery stabilizers whereG c is the corner stabilizer, andR l, R r
are the left- and right-neighboring surgery stabilizers ofG c (associated with the neighboring boundary stabilizers).
Without loss of generality our arguments will also apply forGRGfaces. We will consider different scenarios of
multiplying by the new surgery stabilizers and how support of the logical operator is deformed on the base code plus
the first two layers of the ancilla graph.
Consider multiplying by only theG c surgery stabilizers, that is the one associated with a corner stabilizer of the
original code. The surgery stabilizer can have support of weight-2, 4, or 6 on the base code. Note the support ofG c
will be weight-4 on the ancilla code.
•Weight-2 support on base code: the weight ofQG c must increase by at least 2 when compared toQ, as the
support on the base code could be canceled but 4 qubits of support is gained in the ancilla system.
•Weight-4 support on base code: the weight ofQG c must be at least as large asQ.
•Weight-6 support on base code: If the overlap betweenG c andQis at most 5, then the weight ofQG c must be
at least as large asQ. If the overlap is 6, then indeed the weight ofQG c is decreased by 2, however in such a case
since the overlap ofQwithG c is 6, it means that the overlap ofQwith the associated originalG Xstabilizer
of the code is 6, as such by multiplying by that stabilizer we can find an equivalent logical operator of weight
w−4, thus in such a scenario the weightwmust be lower bounded byd+ 4. Therefore reducing the weight of
QGc by 2 still bounds the weight of such an operator to be at least of weightd+ 2.
Therefore, multiplyingQby the surgery stabilizerG c associated with a corner stabilizer either cannot reduce the
weight ofQor bounds the product ofQG c to be of weight at leastd+ 2.
Now we will consider the multiplication by a singleRsurgery stabilizer, without loss of generality assumeR l. Since
Rl has weight-3 support on the base code and weight-3 in the ancilla code, if the overlap ofQandR l is at most 2,
then the weight ofQR l will increase by 2. If their overlap is three, then the weight ofQR l remainsw, however in such
a scenarioQmust have at least weight-4 overlap with the associatedRboundary stabilizer of the base code, as such
the weight ofQwould be at leastd+ 2 in such a scenario (as multiplying by that boundary stabilizer could reduce
the weight of the chosen representativeQby 2). Therefore,QR l will be of weight at leastword+ 2.
Now let us consider multiplyingQbyR lGc. Again there are three scenarios to consider based on the support of
Gc on the base code being either 2, 4, or 6. Note the support ofR lGc will be weight-5 on the ancilla code.
•R lGc weight-3 support on base code: the weight ofQR lGc must increase by at least 2 when compared toQ, as
the support on the base code could be canceled but 5 qubits of support is gained in the ancilla system.
•R lGc weight-5 support on base code: the weight ofQR lGc must be at least as large asQ.
•R lGc weight-7 support on base code: If the overlap betweenR lGc andQis at most 6, then the weight ofQR lGc
must be at least as large asQ. If the overlap is 7, then indeed the weight ofQR lGc is decreased by 2, however
in such a case since the overlap ofQwithG c is at least 5, it means that the overlap ofQwith the associated

[PDF page 69]
50
originalG Xstabilizer of the code is 6, as such by multiplying by that stabilizer we can find an equivalent
logical operator of weightw−4, thus in such a scenario the weightwmust be lower bounded byd+4. Therefore
reducing the weight ofQR lGc by 2 still bounds the weight of such an operator to be at least of weightd+ 2.
Finally, let us consider multiplyingQbyR lGcRr. Again there are three scenarios to consider based on the support
ofG c on the base code. Note the support ofR lGcRr will be weight-6 on the ancilla code.
•R lGcRr weight-4 support on base code: the weight ofQR lGcRr must increase by at least 2 when compared to
Q.
•R lGcRr weight-6 support on base code: the weight ofQR lGcRr must be at least as large asQ.
•R lGcRr weight-8 support on base code: If the overlap betweenR lGcRr andQis at most 7, then the weight of
QRlGcRr must be at least as large asQ. If the overlap is 8, then indeed the weight ofQR lGcRr is decreased
by 2, however in such a case the weight-8 overlap is a result of 4 edges of the same color whose overall endpoint
would be faces of the same color that could be connected via two other edges of the same color in the base
code. As a result, in this case we can lower bound the weightwofQbyd+ 4. Therefore reducing the weight
ofQR lGcRr by 2 still bounds the weight of such an operator to be at least of weightd+ 2.
Therefore we can conclude that multiplyingQby any combination ofR l, G c, R r is lower bounded by the weightw
ofQord+ 2, and the case ofd+ 2 is due to the existence of a local deformation of the representativeQthat bounds
the weight away from the distancedof the code. These bounds will apply for any sequence ofR lGcRr orG lRcGr
ancilla checks between blue ancilla checks which we have chosen to be weight-4 and are only supported on the ancilla
code, therefore they cannot result in a reduction of weight. This concludes that the first layer of checks cannot reduce
the weight of any other logical operatorQwhen measuring a blue logicalX.
We now consider the surgery checks associated with measuring a red (R) logicalX. Here, theBandGsurgery
checks will be extended into the base code following the procedure previously laid out. We would again like to consider
another logical operatorQin the base code and show that by multiplying by the new surgery checks we cannot reduce
the weight ofQto be below the distance of the code.
Consider first neighboringB b andG b surgery checks that do not correspond to those associated with a corner
stabilizer (we label them with the subscriptbfor bulk). SinceG b has weight-3 support on the base code and weight-3
in the ancilla code, if the overlap ofQandG b is at most 2, then the weight ofQG b will increase by 2. If their overlap
is three, then the weight ofQG b remainsw. The exact same argument holds true for multiplying by onlyB b, however
QBb would have an additional qubit of support since it has weight-4 in the ancilla, thusQB b will be of weight at least
w+ 1. Finally, sinceB bGb has weight-5 support in the ancilla and only weight-4 support on the base code,QB bGb
will always be at least of weightw+ 1.
Finally, as argued previously, multiplying by only a surgery checkG c associated with a corner stabilizer either
cannot reduce the weight ofQor bounds the product ofQG c to be of weight at leastd+ 2 (due to the possibility of
local weight reduction from the associated corner stabilizer).
Thus, considering the potential multiplication of different combinations ofB b, G b pairs andG c surgery checks,
individually none of them can cause the distance to be reduced below the distanced. The final piece is potentially
multiplying by red surgery checks that only have support on the ancilla system. Multiplying by red surgery checks
can only reduce the weight if the red surgery check is weight-3 and both neighboring blue and green surgery checks
have also multiplied the logical operatorQ(they have to be blue and green because the only weight-3 red surgery
checks are those associated with non-corner stabilizers). However, as we have previously seen, ifQis multiplied by
anyB b surgery check then the weight of such an operator is guaranteed to have already increased by 1, thus the
potential decrease in weight from multiplying by red surgery check is harmless.
As a final part to prove the distance preservation, we must argue that the weight obtained on the second layer of
ancilla checks cannot be reduced without gaining some additional support in the ancilla. Indeed, any support of a
representative on the second layer of ancilla qubits can be multiplied with the associated surgery check ofX-type
on the third layer and move its support up to the fourth layer. This process can be repeated up until the point
of multiplying by the top layer of surgeryXstabilizers, however support will be also gained at the endpoints of
chains of surgery checks, and that support will be as large as the number ofXstabilizer layers in the ancilla system.
Therefore, to guarantee that the distance of a representative is lower bounded byd, we need the height of the number
ofXstabilizers in the ancilla system to bed/2 since the support ofd/2 is gained at both endpoints.
We have argued that the ribbon extractor for measuring a blue or red logicalXoperator cannot reduce the
distance of the other logical operators in the base code. By symmetry between green and red colors, measuring
a green logicalXwill also not cause a distance reduction. In addition, due to the symmetry of the construction,
measurement of a logicalZoperator using the ribbon extractor will also not result in a distance reduction. Therefore,
the only remaining case to be considered would be the measurement of a product of logicalXandZoperators of

FIG. S45. Reduction of support of a blueXstring on the base code. (a) The weight-8Xstring (faded and dashed) on the

different color. However, as presented in Corollary 3, the construction of the ribbon extractor for the measurement
of the product operator X C1 Z C2 will have surgery checks whose support on the base code will mirror that of the
individual logicalXandZconstructions, only jointly. This implies by the same arguments previously outlined, that
the remaining logicalXandZrepresentatives of the base code cannot have their distance reduced since if such a
reduction existed it would imply existence in the ribbon extractors for the individual measurements of logicalX
orZ.
D. Teleporting Logical Information to an External Code
In the previous subsections we have described measuring logical Pauli operators of the modular hyperbolic mem-
ory. These forms of measurement can be used in a model of measurement-based logical computation, however the
computation would have to be facilitated with the introduction of resource states to complete universal computation.

[PDF page 71]
52
This could be achieved by preparing ancillary resource states in external codes, via techniques such as magic state
cultivation or distillation. In addition, it may be advantageous to have an external compute region, which would be
a set of logical qubits encoded in some other code that is specifically designed for the purpose of facilitating com-
putation. Regardless of which of the above models are used to complete the universal set of operations required to
run a quantum algorithm, we require a means to do joint logical Pauli measurements between logical qubits in the
hyperbolic code and some external code. We can re-purpose the ribbon extractor for this task, using the top boundary
of the ribbon extractor to couple to a logical qubit of the external code, thereby using the extractor to jointly measure
the product of logical Paulis between the hyperbolic memory, P m, and the compute code P c.
We provide an example of such a design in Fig. S46, where the bottom boundary of the ribbon extractor is coupled
to the hyperbolic memory as previously discussed. However, we now couple the top boundary to another code block,
in the presented example it is a surface code. The connectivity at the top boundary would then be used to extend
the surgery checks onto the support of a logical operator (in the example it would be X c) of the surface code given
by connected modules. Therefore, the product of all of the surgery checks would result in the measurement of P mX c.
FIG. S46. Ribbon extractor to measure the product P mX c of a logical Pauli operator P m of the hyperbolic code and the
logical X c of the surface code. The ribbon extractor is formed from connected modules (cyan) whose bottom boundary is
connected to the hyperbolic code, while the top boundary is connected to a surface code that itself is formed from a connected
set of modules (orange). The ribbon extractor has a total of 2ℓ+ 1 layers, with one extra layer at top boundary used to connect
to the surface code.
S7. AUTOMORPHISM CIRCUITS
Code automorphisms can be used to implement certain logical Clifford gates in modular hyperbolic codes. A code
automorphism is a permutation of the physical qubits that maps the stabilizer group onto itself.
For modular hyperbolic color codes, the global symmetries of the base tiling correspond to code automorphisms

FIG. S47. Quadrilateral (K=2) modules of the 8.8.8-32 color code, labeled by the orientation-preserving subgroupG + =
△+(4,4,4)/Γ.aThe base triangular tilingT △, with each triangle labeled by a word in the reflectionsa,b,cthat reaches it
from the identity trianglee(purple), and each edge colored by its mirror (ared,bgreen,cblue). Boundaries labeled with the
same letterA–Lare identified.bThe 16 quadrilateral modules, formed by pairing adjacent triangles across their green edge
(bmirror) into left cosetsg⟨b⟩. Becausebreverses orientation, each module contains exactly one even-length wordg∈G +
(circled in green, in the middle of each module), which labels the module. Left multiplication by anyh∈G + mapsg7→hg
andgb7→(hg)b, permuting the 16 modules while preserving the internal coordinate system (compass, inset) of every module.
cThe same partition on the fine-grained semi-hyperbolic code, with data qubits (filled circles), ancilla qubits (open circles),
and inter-module seams (dashed lines).
(since the stabilizer generators are defined from the faces of the tiling, and the codes are self-dual CSS codes). The
symmetry group of the base tiling maps the set of generators of the stabilizer group to itself (rather than just mapping
the stabilizer group to itself) so is in general a subgroup of the full automorphism group. However, for all but the
smallest tilings, we find that the tiling’s symmetry group is isomorphic to the group of code automorphisms.
The modular hyperbolicsurface codesare not self dual, and recall that the basis of a stabilizer (XorZ) is
determined by the color of its corresponding face in the tiling (red or blue). A global isometry that is a symmetry
of the base tiling either preserves the face coloring, in which case it directly corresponds to a code automorphism, or
swaps the red and blue face colors, in which case it is a code automorphism up to a global application of a Hadamard
gate on each physical qubit. The specific subgroup of the automorphism group that we propose to implement does
preserve face colors (and therefore does not require a layer of Hadamards).
A. Automorphism Circuits for Color Codes
We first explain our automorphism circuits using the modular hyperbolic color codes. The hyperbolic surface codes
use a similar approach with quadrilateral groups instead of triangle groups. We use a quadrilateral module partition
of the modular hyperbolic color codes (Figure S47 and right panel of Figure S7), in which adjacent triangles ofT △
are paired across a chosen edge color (green, abmirror, in Figure S47b) into left cosetsg⟨b⟩. Because every generator
a, b, creverses orientation, each quadrilateral module{g, gb}contains one triangleg∈G + =△ +(r, s, t)/Γ of even

FIG. S48. Examples of circuits that implement a permutation of the modules that preserves their internal rotation and
orientation. We can use these circuits to implement automorphisms in modular hyperbolic codes.a, By adding additional
seams (inter-module couplers) on the east and west boundary of each module we can use SWAP gates to implement one
such permutation. The four layers of gates shown in the figure implement one step of the automorphism circuit (repeating
this stepLtimes completes the automorphism, and in this exampleL= 4).b, By adding additional seams on the north
and south boundaries we can implement a different automorphism. Here the three layers of gates implement one step of the
automorphism circuit, and again repeating itL= 4 times completes the automorphism.c, Using each of these two circuits
to implement one of the two generators of the automorphism group△ +(r, s, t)/Γ, we can implement the full automorphism
group using fixed connectivity. The connectivity required uses two seams on each side of the module. One seam is used for
automorphisms (connectivity defined by left multiplication) and the other for syndrome extraction (connectivity defined by
right multiplication). The overall connectivity of the modules then corresponds to the union of the edge sets in a Left Cayley
graph and Right Cayley graph. One node (module) of this vertex-transitive graph is shown inc, where each arrow represents
a seam of inter-module couplers leaving the edge and connecting the same pair of modules.
word length and one trianglegbof odd word length. Choosing the even-length triangleg∈G + as the reference
triangle of each module labels the modules by the elements ofG + and fixes a consistent internal 2D coordinate system
(g, x, y) across all modules (the compass in the inset of Figure S47). As explained in Section S1 G, an automorphism
Ah defined by left multiplication byh∈G + maps the reference trianglegof modulegto the reference triangle
hg∈G + of modulehg, and mapsgbto (hg)b. ApplyingA h therefore maps (g, x, y) to (hg, x, y), permuting modules
while preserving the internal rotation and orientation of every module.
In Figure S48 we show how such an automorphism can be implemented with physical circuits. Figure S48ashows
the local structure of a circuit on anL= 4 modular hyperbolic color code that uses an additional seam of inter-module
couplers on the east and west boundary of the module to implement onestepof one such permutation. Repeating
this circuit forL= 4 steps completes the permutation. Figure S48bshows a circuit that instead uses the north and
south boundaries of the modules to implement a permutation of the modules. Since each SWAP gate swaps a data

[PDF page 74]

FIG. S49. Circuits for implementing automorphisms in a modular hyperbolic surface code, using a rectangular partition of
anL= 5 modular hyperbolic surface code as an example. Left: One step of an “east-west” automorphism circuit that uses
additional seams on the east and west boundaries of the module to implement a permutation of the modules. Each step of the
circuit consists of three layers, and implementing the full automorphism requires repeating this step 2L= 10 times. Right: One
step of a “north-south” automorphism circuit that uses additional seams on the north and south boundaries of the module to

qubit and an ancilla qubit, we can use the standard identity
to implement each SWAP gate with two CNOT gates by initializing the ancilla in|0⟩. Furthermore, we can ensure
fault-tolerance by applying aZreset to each qubit in the|0⟩state between SWAPs, ensuring that a single fault does
not propagate to more than one data qubit [30].
To implement the automorphisms corresponding to the full orientation-preserving subgroup△ +(r, s, t)/Γ we need
only implement automorphismsA ρ andA σ corresponding to left multiplication by the generatorsρandσ. For a
standard quadrilateral partition of a modular hyperbolic color code, we add a new seam from the north boundary
of each modulegto the south boundary of moduleρg. We also add a new seam from the east boundary of each
modulegto the west boundary of moduleσg. This allows us to use walking circuits with the north/south seams
to implementA ρ and a walking circuit with the east/west seams to implementA σ. Note that the existing inter-
module couplers required for the code connectivity (syndrome extraction) instead use right multiplication (i.e. each
modulegis connected togρ,gρ −1,gσandgσ −1). Therefore, the overall connectivity of the modular architecture
required for syndrome extraction and automorphisms corresponds to that of a superposed left Cayley graph and right
Cayley graph, where each module is a node and left and right edges correspond to automorphism and memory seams,
respectively (see Figure S48c).
B. Automorphism Circuits for Surface Codes
In Figure S49 we show analogous circuits for modular hyperbolicsurfacecodes, capable of implementing automor-
phisms using either additional inter-module couplers on the east and west boundaries (left panel) or on the north
and south boundary (right panel). Let us assume that the quadrilateral tiling has the symmetries of an orientation-
preserving quadrilateral group□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ (see Section S1 B), i.e. the dual of the quadrilateral tiling is the Cayley
graph of□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ. Let us also suppose that we use the rectangular module partition, for which we choose a
module as the fundamental domain of□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ and label each module with an elementg∈□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ. It is
preferable to implement only two distinct automorphisms so that only one additional seam needs to be added to each
module boundary. For example, we can implement a generatorA ρ (whereρ∈□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ) using the east-west
automorphism circuit andA σ (whereσ∈□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ) using the north-south automorphism circuit. However note
that, unlike the triangle groups, the quadrilateral groups are not necessarily two-generated (in general they require
three generatorsρ,σandτ, see Section S1 B), and soρandσalone in general generate a subgroup of□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ.

[PDF page 75]

Step 1 Step 2 Step 3 Step 4
FIG. S50. An illustration of the stabilizer structure within a module for each step of an automorphism circuit, for anL= 4
modular hyperbolic color code with a quadrilateral module partition. Each step of the circuit (Figure S48b) moves the quantum
information up by one plaquette within the module. In this example we show each of theL= 4 steps of the automorphism circuit
that uses seams on the north and south boundaries. Some of the stabilizers can still be measured between consecutive steps of
the automorphism circuit, however some of the stabilizer generators becomedislocated. A stabilizer generator is dislocated if
the data qubits in its support are no longer adjacent to the necessary measure qubits using the fixed connectivity of the device
(and therefore cannot be measured). In each step of the automorphism circuit we have annotated the plaquettes supporting
dislocated stabilizer generators with a red square, whereas the plaquettes supporting stabilizers that can be measured using
device connectivity within the time step are marked with a green square. Each plaquette is given the color of the corresponding
pair ofXandZstabilizers it supports (i.e. the colors move with the quantum information) and plaquettes that support qubits
spanning two dislocated stabilizers are bicolored (for each dislocated stabilizer generator within the plaquette, we color that
portion of the plaquette with the stabilizer’s color). Data qubits are not explicitly drawn but are at the vertices of the hexagonal
grid.
However, in practice we find that many quadrilateral groups□ +(r, s, t, u)/Γ are in fact two-generated, and so just the
east-west and north-south circuits alone are enough to generate the full group.
C. Stabilizer Dislocation and Logical Error Rates
While the automorphism circuits preserve the code distance (since errors cannot propagate between data qubits), we
expect their logical error rates (LERs) to be higher than that of syndrome extraction alone (idling). One reason that
the LER can increase is that only a subset of the stabilizers can continue to be measured while the automorphism
is being implemented. Concretely, between two of theLconsecutive steps of the automorphism circuit, we can
continue to measure the stabilizers that were contained fully within a module (before the automorphism circuit was
implemented). Each of these stabilizer measurements can be compared to a prior measurement of the same stabilizer
to form a detector. However, some of the stabilizer generators becomedislocatedduring the automorphism circuit
(marked with red squares in Figure S50). These dislocated stabilizer generators can only be measured again after all
Lsteps of the automorphism circuit have been completed. While we have not yet simulated the full automorphism
circuits, we have simulated modified memory experiments that turn off the stabilizers on the boundary of each module
for a number of cycles, as a simple way to model the impact of stabilizer dislocation. As is shown in Figure S51,
turning off the boundary stabilizers for a few rounds only leads to a modest increase in logical error rate (<10×
increase when turning off boundary stabilizers for 5 rounds). We leave further optimization and full simulation of
the automorphisms to future work. In particular, we expect that the use of time-dynamic circuits [31–34] may lead
to reduced circuit depth relative to the SWAP-gate-based circuits we have presented, as was demonstrated with the
walking circuits for the Euclidean surface code in [31].

FIG. S52. We can use a non-uniform module partition that uses a mixture of quadrilateral and triangular modules in a modular
hyperbolic code. This can be useful for reducing the total qubit connectivity degree while implementing both automorphisms
and surgery. For example, we can choose a representative of a logical operator that cuts through the middle of quadrilateral
modules, and then we can split these modules into triangular modules. In this figure we show how the automorphism circuits
of Figure S48 can be generalized to handle such a non-uniform partition where some quadrilateral modules have been split into
a pair of triangular modules.
In order to reduce the overall boundary connectivity of the architecture when a surgery ancilla system is added, we
can split some of the quadrilateral modules each into two triangular modules. This allows us to use the boundary of the
triangular modules (corresponding to the interior of the quadrilateral module it was derived from) as an access point
for the ribbon extractor, without increasing the overall boundary connectivity of the architecture. The automorphism
circuit can be adapted to this non-uniform partition, as shown in Figure S52.
E. Module Frames and Complements of the Module Subgroup
The automorphism circuits described above rely on each module being labeled by an element of a subgroup ofG,
such that implementing an automorphism corresponds to left multiplication by an element of the same group. This
ensures that the automorphism corresponds to a permutation of the modules that leaves the internal coordinates

equivalently the eight triangles of one red octagonal face ofT(Section S3 A iii). Each triangle is labeled and colored by its slot
d∈D, and the word at the center of each module (circled in red) is its element of the complement Λ. Of the 12 complements
ofDfor this code we show Λ =⟨ρ⟩={e, ab, abab, ba} ∼= Z4, the cyclic subgroup generated by the rotationρ=ab, which is

(x, y) of each module fixed, which is a requirement of the walking circuits. This property is guaranteed to hold for
the quadrilateral (K= 2) module partition on orientable surfaces, where each module is labeled with an element of
the orientation-preserving subgroupG +. In general, however, it is not guaranteed that a module partitioning scheme
will admit a suitable labeling of each module by an element of a subgroup. In particular, in this section we will focus
our attention on the octagon (K= 8) module partition, where each module is aleft cosetof the order-eight dihedral
subgroupg⟨b, c⟩ ∼= D4 ≤G. If we were to attempt to implement automorphisms as left multiplication by elements
ofG +, for example, this would require implementing the automorphismg→σg. However,σ :=bc∈G + rotates
the identity octagon module around its center, which does not preserve its internal coordinate system and cannot be
implemented using our walking circuits.
In this section we will show that, even with octagon module partitions (or any partition into cosets), it can often still
be possible to implement automorphisms using walking circuits. As we will show, the requirement is that we can find
a subgroup Λ≤Gsuch that each module contains exactly one element of Λ, and which is used to define its internal
coordinate system. The implementable automorphisms are then left multiplication by elements of Λ. Equivalently,
for a module partition defined by left cosets of a subgroupD≤G(e.g.|D|= 2 for quadrilaterals, or|D|= 8 for
octagons) this corresponds to finding asubgroup complementΛ ofDinG, by definition satisfying:
Λ∩D={e},ΛD=G.(S39)
We will further show that we can find complements Λ for octagon partitions of several of the most promising modular

[PDF page 79]
60
hyperbolic color codes considered in this work.
We will explain this condition using octagon module partitions as an example, following Figure S53, but note that
the same condition holds for any partition into left cosets. Let us first assume that Λ⊆Gis just a subset of the
elements ofG, such that each modulegD(D=⟨b, c⟩ ∼= D4) contains exactly one triangle corresponding to an element
of Λ. We will refer to the triangle in a module corresponding to an element of Λ as the module’sframe. We further
label each triangle in a module by itsslotd∈D, where by definition the module’sframeis assigned the slote, and
the remaining slots are determined by right multiplication by the generatorsb, cofD=⟨b, c⟩. The frame alone (along
with the generator labels{a, b, c}of its three sides) is sufficient to define the module’s internal coordinate system.
The inset of Figure S53 shows an octagon module with each of its triangles labeled by its slotd∈D, and with the
compass signifying its internal coordinate system. Note that we normalize Λ such that it contains the identity element
e∈Λ corresponding to the frame of the identity module.
Notice that every triangleg∈Gfactorizes as
g=λd(λ∈Λ, d∈D),(S40)
whereλ∈Λ labels the module andd∈Dis the slot ofgin the module. As an example, see the example of the
octagon module partition of the 8.8.8-32 code in Figure S53, with triangles labeled by their group elementg∈Gin
Figure S53aand factorized into their frameλ∈Λ and slotd∈Din Figure S53b.
Now consider left multiplication by some group elementh∈G, corresponding to an automorphism. Our automor-
phism circuits require that this preserves the internal coordinate system of each module, which corresponds to mapping
module frames to module frames. Sincee∈Λ, requiring that the identity frame maps to a frame,e→he∈Λ, implies
that the allowable automorphisms must be a subset of Λ. Furthermore, if we wanteveryelementλ∈Λ to map frames
to frames as an automorphism, this requires that every elementλ∈Λ maps a frameλ ′ ∈Λ to a frameλλ ′ ∈Λ, which
is true if and only if Λ is closed under multiplication. Therefore a partition into left cosets ofDadmits a full group of
|Λ|=|G|/|D|frame-preserving automorphismsA λ : (λ′, x, y)→(λλ ′, x, y) if and only if Λ is a subgroup complement
ofDinG(Equation (S39)).
For quadrilateral modules (D=⟨b⟩) partitioning an orientable surface, we can always use Λ :=G +, which automat-
ically satisfies Equation (S39). Furthermore,G + =⟨ρ, σ⟩is two-generated, and therefore only requires one additional
seam of inter-module couplers at each of the four boundaries of the module (one for each generator and its inverse,
see Figure S48c). For octagon modules (D=⟨b, c⟩,|D|= 8) there is no guarantee that a complement Λ satisfying
Equation (S39) will exist for any given tiling, nor that such a complement would be two-generated. However we will
now show that two-generated complements often do exist, which we find by a combinatorial search.
Given a groupGand a module partitioning into left cosetsgD, a naive search for a complement Λ might attempt to
find the elements of Λ by brute force, picking one element from each cosetgDand checking for group closure. However,
such a brute-force search would clearly be intractable; after fixing the identity element in the identity module, there
remain|D| |Λ|−1 sets to try, amounting to 8 49 ≈10 44 combinations for the 8.8.8-400 code, for example. Instead, we
enumerate all complements efficiently by exploiting structure in the problem. Rather than searching for elements of
Λ directly, we instead search for consistentslotlabelings using an efficient generalized graph coloring algorithm, and
from that we extract Λ as the set of triangles assigned slote.
Our search makes use of the following observation. Take any triangleg, which has a unique factorizationg=λd
(whereλ∈Λ, d∈D) defining its slot labeld. If we multiplygon the right by any elementx∈Gwe obtain
gx=λdx=λλ ′d′, where hereλ ′d′ is the unique factorization ofdx. Sinceλλ ′ ∈Λ (from group closure of Λ) we
see that the slotd ′ ofgxdependsonlyonxand the slotdofg, and is completely independent of which moduleg
is in. As a consequence, for any valid complement Λ, every elementx∈Gdefines a permutation of the slot labels
πx. Any complement Λ is therefore fully defined by how the three generatorsa,bandcofGpermute the slot labels.
Since each generator is self-inverse (a reflection), we know thatπ x :x∈ {a, b, c}is aninvolutionof the slot labels
D. Additionally, for the octagon modules whereD=⟨b, c⟩, we already know howbandcpermute the slot labels
by considering their action on the identity module⟨b, c⟩(they are fixed). Therefore enumerating complements of
Dcorresponds to enumerating possible involutionsπ a of the slot labelsDdefined by generatora. There are 764
possible involutions of the 8 elements ofDto check when enumerating octagon module complements, and this is
fixed irrespective of the size of the tiling, unlike the brute-force approach. We can check whether each of these 764
involutions defines a valid complement Λ using a breadth-first search (BFS) on the tilingT △. For each candidate
πa, we start the BFS by labeling the identity triangle with the identity slot labeleand then propagate the slot
labels acrossT △ by applying the involutionπ x each time a mirrorx∈ {a, b, c}is crossed, whereπ a is the candidate
involution andπ b andπ c are fixed by the known action ofbandcon slot labels. Each time a mirror is crossed that
closes a cycle (reaching a triangle that already has a slot label) we check for consistency of the slot label, and break
if an inconsistent slot label is observed.
We do not require that Λ be anormalsubgroup Λ⊴G, but if Λisnormal then the slot labeling takes a particularly
simple form. To see why, take two elementsg 1, g2 ∈Gwhich factorize asg 1 =λ 1d1 andg 2 =λ 2d2 (whereλ 1, λ2 ∈Λ

We enumerated the complements of the octagon module subgroup for the most promising 8.8.8 color code tilings

the cycles in this same homology basis using the octagon modules. One could simply construct 4 ribbon extractors,
one to measure each of these seed cycles, however we note that each module in the ribbon extractor has only one
outgoing seam of inter-module couplers at its boundary, and additional inter-module couplers could be used to couple
the same ribbon extractor to all four seed cycles (using additional seams on both sides of the cylindrical ribbon).
S8. COMPILATION AND RESOURCE ESTIMATE OF A RIPPLE-CARRY ADDER
In order to estimate the time overhead of implementing quantum algorithms using our modular hyperbolic memory
architecture, we compile a quantum arithmetic circuit to automorphism moves, ribbon extractor measurements and
compute region operations. We use the ripple-carry adder of Ref. [35], which we refer to as the CDKM adder.
A. The CDKM Addition Circuit
The CDKM adder is a ripple-carry quantum addition circuit introduced in Ref. [35]. It adds twon-bit numbersa
andbin place: the lownbits of the sum are written to thebregister, and the carry-outc n (the high bit of the sum)
is XORed into an extra qubitz. On computational basis states it acts as
|a⟩ |b⟩ |c0=0⟩ |z⟩ 7→ |a⟩ |(a+b) mod 2 n⟩ |0⟩ |z⊕c n⟩, c n =

(a+b)/2 n
,(S42)
wherec 0 is the single ancilla (the carry-in), initialised in and returned to|0⟩. Withzinitialised to|0⟩, thebregister
together withzholds the full (n+1)-bit suma+b. An example of a CDKM adder, forn= 3, is shown in Figure S55.
The adder is composed primarily of two types of gates (or “blocks”): the MAJ (majority) and UMA (unmajority-
and-add) gates, each consisting of one Toffoli and two CNOTs (see Figure S55), and acting on basis states as
MAJ :|c, b, a⟩ 7→ |c⊕a, b⊕a,maj(a, b, c)⟩,(S43)
UMA :|c⊕a, b⊕a,maj(a, b, c)⟩ 7→ |c, a⊕b⊕c, a⟩,(S44)
where maj(a, b, c) =ab⊕bc⊕cais the majority function.

FIG. S56. Standard circuits for teleporting a qubit into and out of memory using Pauli measurements. Measurements which
touch the memory, and therefore use the ribbon extractor, are shaded in grey. The dashed boxes signify Pauli feedback that
can be tracked by the classical control system in the accumulated Pauli frame.a, Aloadoperation, teleporting a qubitqfrom
memory into the compute region. A joint measurement X qX c followed by a single measurementZ q.b, Astoreoperation, which
teleports a qubit from the compute region back into the memory. We measure X qX c followed by a single-qubit measurement
MZc in the compute region (e.g. a transversal measurement of a surface code patch).
B. Compilation Overview
We implement the adder by combining the modular hyperbolic memory with a small compute region. The ribbon
extractor and automorphisms are used to teleport logical qubits between the memory and compute region, and all
logical gates (CNOTs and Toffoli gates) are applied directly in the compute region.
To implement the teleportation we use the standard circuit identities shown in Figure S56, for teleporting a memory
qubitqto or from a compute qubitc. Aloadoperation requires two ribbon measurements, a joint X qX c measurement
followed by a single-qubit Z q measurement. Thestoreoperation requires only a single X qX c measurement followed
by a single-qubit Z c measurement in the compute region (which can just be a transversalZmeasurement of a
surface code, for example). The teleportation (load and store combined) therefore coststhreeribbon measurements.
Additionally, automorphisms are needed to move the X q and Z q logical operators into the extractor’s slot, which
corresponds to moving between the two cycles in a symplectic pair. For simplicity we will leave thec 0 andzqubits
in the compute region, and only store theaandbregisters in the hyperbolic memory.
We will assume that each ribbon extractor measurement takesαdcycles to achieve the desired logical fidelity, where
dis the distance of the hyperbolic memory and where we expectα≈1. Each basic automorphism move (implementing
one of the generators{ρ, ρ −1, σ, σ−1}) is a walking circuit described in Section S7, which we assume takesκLcycles.
We will focus on the quadrilateral module partition of the hyperbolic color codes, for which we estimate thatκ≈2
is achievable with some additional circuit optimizations inspired by Ref. [31]. We will letN R denote the number of
ribbon measurements used for the adder, and letN A denote the number of automorphisms. The time attributed to
ribbon measurements and automorphisms is then
T=αdN R +κLN A.(S45)
For each MAJi or UMAi block in the adder, at least one of the three qubits it acts on (one of its operands) is already

[PDF page 83]
64
located in the compute region at the end of the prior operation (or initialized there, in the case ofc 0). Therefore we
only need to load at mosttwoof the three operands before each block, and to store at most two operands to make
room for the operands loaded for the next block. Furthermore, we allocateC≥3 qubits in the compute region for
memory qubits (Coperand slots). In the middle of the adder (between MAJ n−1 and UMA n−1) we can therefore
retainCqubits in the compute region, since the lastCoperands used by MAJ i blocks are the firstCneeded by
the UMA i blocks. This saves one store and one load for each of theCoperand slots, while the remaining 2n−C
operand qubits each require the full two loads and two stores throughout the adder. Since each combined load and
store requires three ribbon measurements, this leads to
NR = 3(4n−C) (S46)
ribbon measurements needed for ann-bit adder. We will chooseC= 5, and hence haveN R = 12n−15. Note that
this corresponds to Belady’s cache replacement policy [36] (when a slot is needed, storing the operand whose next use
is furthest in the future), which minimizes the number of loads, and hence stores (each loaded qubit is stored exactly
once), for a fixed order of blocks.
WithN R determined (and independent of the hyperbolic memory code used) the rest of this section will be focused
on estimating the number of automorphism movesN A, which does depend on the tiling and logical basis. We also
make the simplifying assumption that the ribbon extractor can measure any of theCoperand slotXlogicals in the
compute region for teleportation. This could be achieved, for example, by using additional inter-module couplers, or
instead by allocating additional routing space in the compute region (such that the ribbon extractor only needs to
measure a single logical there to access allCqubits). We leave a detailed study of this aspect of the compilation to
future work.
C. Coarse Upper Bound on the Number of Automorphism Moves
Before optimizing the number of automorphism moves, we will first compute a very coarse upper bound onN A. We
will be focusing primarily on the 8.8.8 modular hyperbolic color code with symmetry group△(4,4,4) 109 (the 8.8.8-400
code in Table S3), using the logical basis generated by the orbit of a single seed cycleγunderG + =⟨ρ, σ⟩. This tiling
is used to construct modular hyperbolic color codes with parameters [[3600,104,22]] atL= 3 and [[6400,104,30]] at
L= 4.
For this code, the seed cycleγhas trivial stabilizer inG +, so each cycle in its orbit can be written uniquely
asC x =xγfor somex∈G +. An automorphism move by a generators∈ {ρ ±1, σ±1}maps every cycleyγto

automorphismhx −1. Switching from measuringC x1 to measuringC x2 therefore requires a sequence of moves with

D. Minimizing Within-Pair Switches
We will call an automorphism that moves the extractor (i.e. changes which cycle is under the extractor) from a cycle
in one symplectic pair to a cycle in a different symplectic pair ajump. A jump takes at mostDbasic automorphism
moves. We will refer to an automorphism that moves the extractor from one cycle to its partner within a symplectic
pair aswitch.
Each load operation requires measuring the X q and Z q logical operators of the same logical qubitqstored in the
hyperbolic memory, where one of these is measured jointly with a compute region logical (e.g.M X qX c
in Figure S56).
Measuring these two logical operators consecutively requires a switch. Given that this can be a common operation,
it makes sense to optimize it. As we will now explain, for this code (using our symmetric basis for it), an appropriate
choice of extractor location can reduce the number of moves needed for a switch from 5 to 1.
In our basis, every symplectic pair has the same automorphism mapping one cycle to its partner: thei-th pair
consists of the cyclesα i =y iγandβ i =y iw±1γfor somey i ∈G +, wherew=σ 2ρ σ2. By Equation (S47), a switch
requiresM(y i, yiw±1) =|hw ±1h−1|moves, which is independent ofi. Forh=ethis is|w|= 5. However, sinceσ 4 =e
we havew=σ 2ρ σ−2, and so choosing the extractor locationh=σ 2 giveshwh −1 =ρ. With this choice, every switch
is a singleρorρ −1 move (as for the pair shown in Figure 4aof the main text). Of the 200 possible extractor locations
h∈G +, 8 have this property.
We can choose an ordering of ribbon measurements where the two measurements in each load operation are always
implemented consecutively (i.e.M Zq
immediately followsM X qX c
). Moving the ribbon extractor fromM X qX c
toM Zq
then always costs one switch (a single automorphism move). All other automorphisms cost at most one jump. This
leads to at most (4n−C)Dmoves for the stores, (4n−C)Dmoves for theM X qX c
in each load, and (4n−C) for the

3 NR automorphism moves, or

atL= 4 (757 per bit).
E. Grouping Qubits into Pairs
We can reduce the number of moves further by storing operand qubits that are accessed in close succession in the
same symplectic pair of homologically nontrivial cycles. Each symplectic pair of cycles (α i, βi) stores four logical
qubits, which we now use to store operand qubitsa 2i,b 2i,a 2i+1 andb 2i+1, choosing X q supported onα i and Z q on
βi for all four. This swaps the logical Pauli bases of two of the qubits relative to the convention in Section S5, purely
for notational convenience; equivalently, we could keep that convention and interchangeXandZin the teleportation
circuits of those two qubits, so that their joint measurement isM ZqZc
onα i and their single measurement isM X q
on
βi.
We refer to the loads and stores performed before a block (or at the end, when all qubits are returned to memory)
as astep. WithC= 3, the qubits in each step have at most three consecutive bit indices (e.g. before MAJ i we store
ai−2 andb i−1 and loadb i anda i), so each step involves at most two symplectic pairs. Moreover, at most one of these
pairs is acted on by both stores and loads, as can be checked for each type of step. We can therefore handle the pairs
one at a time while performing every store before every load, which frees space in the compute region: first a pair
with only stores (if any), then a pair with both, then a pair with only loads. On each pair, the stores and the joint
measurements of the loaded qubits both measureM X qX c
, acting onα i, and are followed by the measurementsM Zq
onβ i. Each pair therefore costs at most one jump to reach it and one switch fromα i toβ i (a pair with only stores
needs no switch), so each step requires at most 2D+ 2 automorphism moves. There are 2nnon-empty steps, giving
NA ≤4(D+ 1)n≈ D+1
3 NR. Here we have used the memory schedule forC= 3, for whichN R = 3(4n−3) (only six
more ribbon measurements than forC= 5), so that
T≤3αd(4n−3) + 4(D+ 1)κL n(S51)
cycles. Using the same assumptions as before, this gives 21,402 cycles atL= 3 (428 per bit) and 28,930 cycles at
L= 4 (579 per bit).

[PDF page 85]
66
F. Compute Region
The logic in the compute region can be implemented in parallel to the ribbon extractor measurements and auto-
morphisms, and has a negligible impact on the overall runtime of the adder. After a load’s joint measurementM X qX c
,
the subsequentM Zq
only determines a Pauli correction to the qubit in the compute region. The next MAJ i or UMAi
block can therefore begin immediately after the joint measurements, and the Pauli frame can be propagated through
its CNOTs once it has been determined. The Toffoli, implemented with a|CCZ⟩state, only needs the Pauli frame
before the Clifford corrections. Meanwhile, the extractor performs theM Zq
measurements and the automorphism
moves to the next step’s stores (which act only on the memory), taking roughly 2αd+ (D+ 1)κLcycles. This is
comparable to the duration of a block (≈4dplus the decoder reaction time), so only a small fraction of the compute
time remains exposed. We also note that the duration of the block can be reduced to just the reaction time using
AutoCCZ states and a sufficiently large compute region [37].
S9. ALGORITHMS FOR FINDING A SHORT CANONICAL HOMOLOGY BASIS
As explained in Section S5, finding a canonical logical basis for a hyperbolic code reduces to finding acanonical
homology basisof the underlying tiling: for an orientable surface this corresponds to a set ofgsymplectic pairs
of homologically non-trivial cycles. For the color codes we search for these cycles directly on the three-colorable
hyperbolic tilingT, whereas for the surface codes we use the base quadrilateral tilingT □. In either case the homology
basis search is the same combinatorial problem on a hyperbolic tiling. We therefore drop the color/surface distinction
for the remainder of this section and simply consider a finite, closed and orientable hyperbolic tiling denotedT.
We fix the following notation. We writeTfor the hyperbolic tiling andT ∗ for its dual, and denote the primal
graph byG= (V, E, F), with verticesV, edgesEand facesF. The dual graphG ∗ has one vertex per face ofT
and one dual edgee ∗ ∈E ∗ per primal edgee∈E, with a one-to-one correspondence between primal and dual edges
E∗ ↔E. Acycleis a closed loop of primal edges, and a closed loop of dual edges is acocycle. Two cycles combine
by their symmetric differenceγ i ⊕γ j (the cycle of edges belonging to exactly one of them, with edges common to
both removed). Under this operation the cycles form a group, and its quotient by the cycles that bound sets of faces
(the trivial cycles) is the first homology groupH 1(Σg;F 2). Taking the symmetric difference of a trivial cycle with
a non-trivialγleaves the homology class of the non-trivial cycle unchanged, so many different cycles represent the
same class. Two such cycles arehomologous, and there are 2gindependent non-trivial classes. We write [γ] for the
class ofγ.
We also recall the intersection number of Section S5. For two cyclesγ i, γj we write⟨γ i, γj⟩ ∈F 2, equal to 1 if they
cross an odd number of times and 0 otherwise. This number depends only on the homology classes, so homologous
cycles have the same intersection number with every other cycle. For a candidate set{γ 1, . . . , γm}we collect these
numbers into thecycle intersection matrixM∈ {0,1} m×m, the symmetric zero-diagonal matrix withM ij =⟨γ i, γj⟩.
Finding a canonical homology basis then amounts to selecting 2gcycles and reordering the rows and columns of the
corresponding 2g×2gsubmatrix ofMso that it equals thesymplectic matrix
Ω =
gM
i=1

0 1
1 0

∈ {0,1} 2g×2g .(S52)
A homology basis of 2gindependent non-trivial cycles can be computed efficiently, for example via tree–cotree
decomposition [38], and brought into canonical form by Gaussian elimination overF 2. This does not, however, yield
geometrically short cycles. Each cycleγ⊆Ehas a length|γ|given by its number of edges, and Gaussian elimination
corresponds to taking symmetric differences of cycles, which typically lengthens them. Conversely, Erickson and
Whittlesey give a polynomial-time algorithm for theshortest homology basis, but without the canonical constraint [39,
Theorem 4.3], We are not aware of an efficient algorithm that returns a shortest basis in canonical form directly, and
related problems are NP-hard [40, 41]. In any case, we need more than a short canonical basis: we need one that
is additionallysymmetrical, in the sense that its cycles lie in the orbit of a small number of seed cycles as described
in Section S5. In this section, we describe algorithms to find ashort canonical homology basisofTby enumerating
short non-trivial cycles from every vertex.
The symmetries we use are the orientation-preserving automorphisms Aut +(T) =G + =⟨ρ, σ⟩, that can be imple-
mented as fault-tolerant automorphism circuits (Section S7). Relating the basis cycles by these rotations means that
only a seed cycle needs its own ribbon extractor for logical measurement (Section S6), while every other cycle in its
orbit is mapped onto the seed by an automorphism circuit. The automorphisms map non-trivial cycles to non-trivial
cycles of the same length and preserve intersection numbers, so the orbit of a cycle consists of cycles of equal length
in potentially distinct homology classes.

[PDF page 86]
67
At a high level, our algorithms enumerate non-trivial cycles by graph search from every vertex ofT, recording the
length|γ|and class [γ] of each and keeping only the shortest representative per homology class encountered. We
use these as seed cycles. Starting from the shortest one or two, we generate their full orbit under Aut +(T) =⟨ρ, σ⟩,
producing a candidate set whose cycles are related by tiling symmetries. This ensures the resulting canonical pairs
map to each other by global rotations. We then compute the intersection number for all candidate pairs and extract
a subset forming≤gmutually compatible symplectic pairs by a clique or backtracking search.
Figure S57 outlines our algorithms for constructing a canonical homology basis of a hyperbolic tilingTof genusg.
Phase 1 builds a homology coordinate system via a tree-cotree decomposition and homology label assignment for every
edge of the primal graph (Algorithms 1–2, explained in Section S9 A). Phase 2 enumerates non-trivial cycles from
every vertex using breadth-first search (BFS) (Algorithm 3, Section S9 B). Phase 3 generates the orbit of seed cycles
under Aut+(T) =⟨ρ, σ⟩(Algorithm 4, Section S9 C). Phase 4 constructs a canonical basis ofk found ≤gsymplectic
pairs from the cycle intersection matrix via a clique or backtracking search (Algorithms 6–7, Section S9 D).

A. Homology Coordinate System
We start with the primal graphG= (V, E, F) of the hyperbolic tilingT. In order to classify the homology class [γ]
of any cycle we enumerate, we construct a mapϕ:E→ {0,1} 2g assigning to each edge a 2g-bit vector. The homology
class of any cycleγis then identified by the bit-vector label
h(γ) =
M
e∈γ
ϕ(e)∈ {0,1} 2g,

[PDF page 88]
69
obtained by taking the bit-wise XOR of the labels of its edges. The induced maphon homology classes is an
isomorphismH 1(Σg;F 2) ∼= F2g
2 : the 2 2g −1 non-trivial homology classes correspond to the 2 2g −1 non-zero elements
of{0,1} 2g, and the trivial class corresponds to the zero vector0∈ {0,1} 2g. Two cycles are homologous if and only
if they share the same label. We describe how we constructϕusing Algorithm 1 and Algorithm 2, closely following
Erickson and Whittlesey [39].
T ree-cotree decomposition (Algorithm 1). We begin by identifying a set of 2gedges ofGwhose associated
cycles form a basis ofH 1(Σg;F 2) via the tree-cotree decomposition [38]. We first find a spanning treeTofG. Every
non-tree edgee /∈Tcloses exactly one cycle, formed byetogether with the unique path inTconnecting its endpoints.
We then take a spanning cotreeC ∗ of the dual graphG ∗, restricted to the dual edges that do not cross any edge of
T. The edges lying in neitherTnor in the cotreeC ∗ are thegenerator edgesL={ℓ 1, . . . , ℓ2g}, and the cycle closed
by eachℓ i is a generator cycle.
There are exactly 2ggenerator edges, by a simple count. The spanning treeTuses|V| −1 edges, and the cotree
C ∗, being a spanning tree of the dual graph on its|F|vertices, uses|F| −1 edges. Every primal edge lies in exactly
one ofT, the cotreeC ∗ (as a dual edge), or the leftover setL, so
|L|=|E| −(|V| −1)−(|F| −1) =|E| − |V| − |F|+ 2 = 2−χ= 2g,
using the Euler characteristicχ=|V| − |E|+|F|= 2−2gof a closed orientable surface of genusg. This count matches
dimH 1(Σg,F 2) and since the generator cycles are independent, they form a basis [38]. Consequently every cycle on
Σg is homologous to a symmetric difference of generator cycles, so its homology class is the sum of the corresponding
generator classes. This makes the generators the natural basis for the homology label mapϕ, where each bit ofϕ(e)
records whether edgeecrosses the cocycle associated with one generator.
As described in Algorithm 1,Tis obtained by breadth-first search (BFS) from a root vertexr∈V. The dual graph
G∗ has one vertex per face ofTand one edgee ∗ per primal edgee. Removing the dual edges crossed byTleaves a
subgraph ˜E∗ =E ∗ \ {e∗ :e∈T}, and any spanning tree of ˜E∗ is a valid cotreeC ∗. The generator edges are then the
edges that remain:L=E\(T∪ {e:e ∗ ∈C ∗}).

Homology Label Assignment (Algorithm 2). Given the generator edgesLand cotreeC ∗ from Algorithm 1,
we assign a 2g-bit labelϕ(e)∈ {0,1} 2g to every edgee∈E, where bitirecords whetherecrosses thei-th generator
cocycle (the closed loop of dual edges crossing generatorℓ i). In Algorithm 2 each generator edgeℓ i is assigned a
unique bitmask with only thei-th bit set to 1. This bitmask is then propagated to all other edges by tracing the two
faces (dual vertices) on either side ofℓ i back to the cotree rootr ∗, and bitiis flipped for every other primal edge
crossed by this cocycle.
