We remark that Erickson and Whittlesey [39] find the shortest homology basis by re-running the tree-cotree de-
composition from every vertex as root, each time generating only the 2ggenerator cycles associated with that choice
of spanning tree, for a total ofO(gn) candidate cycles, wheren=|V|. Their choice ofC ∗ as the maximum spanning
tree weighted by BFS distance is essential for guaranteeing that the generator cycles produced at each root are as
short as possible. In our algorithm, we perform the tree-cotree decomposition only once, from a single fixed root,
solely to establish the homology label mapϕ. The homology labelsϕcomputed from this fixed decomposition are
then used throughout Phase 2, which enumerates a much richer candidate set by running BFS from every vertex and
collecting all cycles closed by non-tree edges, producingO(n 2) candidate cycles in total. The choice ofC ∗ therefore
has no effect on the lengths of the cycles we find in Phase 2.
Cycle Enumeration (Algorithm 3).We enumerate short homologically non-trivial cycles by running BFS from
every vertexv∈V. For each rootv, a BFS spanning treeT v is grown, and each non-tree edgee c = (u, w)∈E\T v
closes a cycle
γ= TreePath(T v, v, u)⊕ {e c} ⊕TreePath(T v, w, v),
whose homology label is computed ash(γ) = L
e∈γ ϕ(e). Cycles withh(γ) =0are trivial and discarded. Since BFS
produces shortest-path trees,γis no longer than any closed walk throughvande c. Running BFS from every vertex
producesO(n 2) candidate cycles in total. Only the shortest representative per homology class is retained and the
candidate setLis sorted by cycle length. Each cycle is stored compactly as a tuple (|γ|, h(γ), v, e c), where|γ|is the
number of edges inγ,h(γ)∈ {0,1} 2g \ {0}is its homology label,v∈Vis the BFS root, ande c ∈Eis the non-tree
edge that closes the cycle. The pair (v, e c) is sufficient to reconstructγuniquely: given rootvand non-tree edge
ec = (u, w), run BFS fromvto obtainT v, then recoverγ= TreePath(T v, v, u)⊕ {e c} ⊕TreePath(T v, w, v).

4. Ifh(γ)̸=0, the cycle is non-trivial. IfLcontains no cycle with labelh(γ), add (|γ|, h(γ), v, e c) toL. IfL
already contains a cycleγ ′ withh(γ ′) =h(γ) and|γ ′|>|γ|, replace it withγ. Otherwise discardγ.
5. SortLby cycle length|γ|in increasing order.
6. ReturnL.
Note:
The tuple (v, e c) is sufficient to reconstructγuniquely: given rootvand closing edgee c = (u, w), run BFS fromvto
obtainT v; thenγ= TreePath(T v, v, u)⊕ {e c} ⊕TreePath(T v, w, v).
C. Automorphism Orbit Generation
The candidate setLfrom Algorithm 3 contains, for each homology class it encounters, the shortest enumerated
representative. However, for the hyperbolic tilings we consider, we do not search for a canonical basis directly from
L. Instead, we exploit the orientation-preserving automorphism group Aut +(T) =⟨ρ, σ⟩of the tiling to generate a
structured candidate setL aut whose cycles are related by the tiling symmetries.
The motivation is architectural: the automorphismsρandσare implemented as walking circuits on the semi-
hyperbolic tiling, and logical operators related by these automorphisms can be mapped to each other by the cor-
responding circuits. A canonical basis drawn from the orbit of a seed cycle under Aut +(T) therefore yields logical
operators with this desirable symmetry structure.
Seed Cycle Orbit Generation (Algorithm 4).We select one or more short cycles fromLasseed cycles
and apply every elementτ∈Aut +(T) to each seed, generating a family of image cycles{τ(γ s)}. Recall that
Aut+(T) =G + =⟨ρ, σ⟩is the orientation-preserving subgroup of the quotient groupG, so that eachτacts by left
multiplication, mapping the tile labeled by the group elementgto the tile labeledτ g(Section S1 G). This permutation
of the tiles is straightforward to compute by a traversal of the Cayley graph of Aut +(T). Since automorphisms are
isometries of the tiling, each image cycle inherits the length of the seed. We then compute the homology labels of
the image cycles withϕ. The shortest representative per homology label is retained as before, producingL aut. In
practice we find that a small number of seed cycles can already generate a large fraction of the logical operators. For
several of the examples in Table S3, a single seed cycle generates almost all of them.
D. Canonical Homology Basis
Given the candidate setL aut produced by Algorithm 4 (or alternatively the candidate setLproduced by Algo-
rithm 3), we now describe algorithms to find a canonical homology basis.
Cycle Intersection Matrix (Algorithm 5).We compute the intersection numbers of the cycles inL aut (or
L). For each pair of cyclesγ i, γj in the candidate set, the intersection number⟨γ i, γj⟩ ∈F 2 records whether the two
cycles cross an odd number of times on Σ g, as defined in Section S5. This is a topological invariant of their homology
classes. We therefore only need to count crossings geometrically for the 2ggenerator cycles of Algorithm 1: every

[PDF page 91]
72
Algorithm 4:Seed Cycle Orbit Generation via Tiling Automorphisms
Input:
1. A setLof non-trivial cycles sorted by length, as produced by Algorithm 3;
2. The orientation-preserving automorphism group Aut +(T) =⟨ρ, σ⟩of the tilingT.
Output:A candidate setL aut containing, for each homology label occurring in the orbits of the seed cycles under
Aut+(T), the shortest such image cycle.
Method:
1. SetL aut :=∅.
2. Select one or more short cycles fromLasseed cycles.
3. For each seed cycleγ s and each group elementτ∈Aut +(T), applyτtoγ s to obtain the image cycleτ(γ s)
and compute its homology labelh(τ(γ s)).
4. For each image cycleτ(γ s): ifL aut contains no cycle with labelh(τ(γ s)), addτ(γ s) toL aut; ifL aut already
contains a longer cycle with the same label, replace it.
5. ReturnL aut.
other cycle is homologous to a symmetric difference of these, its homology labelh(γ) records which ones, and its
intersection numbers can then be inferred algebraically from those of the generator cycles. Collecting them gives the
cycle intersection matrixM∈ {0,1} m×m introduced above, wheremis the size of the candidate set.
Symplectic pair search (Algorithm 6, Algorithm 7).Given the intersection matrixM∈ {0,1} m×m, we
seek a maximum collection of mutually compatible symplectic pairs. A pair (γ i, γj) is acandidate symplectic pairif
Mij = 1, i.e. the two cycles cross an odd number of times. Two candidate pairs (γ i, γj) and (γ k, γl) are compatible if
none of the four cross terms between them is 1:M ik =M il =M jk =M jl = 0. A canonical basis then corresponds to
a choice of 2gcycles for which the 2g×2gsubmatrix ofM, obtained by selecting those rows and the corresponding
columns, equals the symplectic matrix Ω of Eq. (S52).
While a canonical basis can always be constructed from any homology basis by Gaussian elimination overF 2, this
corresponds to taking symmetric differences of cycles, which can increase cycle lengths and yields cycles that are
no longer images of a seed cycle under Aut +(T), so they lose the symmetry structure and cannot be mapped onto
one another by the automorphism circuits. The key constraint here is therefore that we must not alter the cycles
themselves. We must instead find the symplectic structure by selecting 2gcycles from the candidate set and ordering
them, so that the corresponding 2g×2gsubmatrix ofMequals Ω while the cycle lengths are preserved. This is
equivalent to the induced matching problem on the graph defined byM, which is NP-complete in general [42].
In practice, our solvers find complete bases for the smaller tilings we consider, while for larger tilings we often
obtain onlyk found < gpairs within our computational budget. We use two exact search strategies: a reduction to a
maximum clique search (Algorithm 6) and a backtracking search (Algorithm 7).
Algorithm 6 reduces the search to amaximum clique problemon acompatibility graphKthat we construct. The
vertices ofKare all candidate symplectic pairsP={(γ i, γj) :M ij = 1, i < j}, and an edge connects two vertices if
and only if the corresponding pairs are compatible. A clique of sizeginKcorresponds to a complete canonical basis
ofgsymplectic pairs, whereas a maximum clique of sizek found < gyields a partial basis. A maximum clique inK
can then be obtained using standard clique-finding solvers [43].
Algorithm 7 instead searches for the symplectic pairs directly. It builds up a canonical basis one pair at a time,
adding a pair of crossing cycles at each step and backtracking whenever the remaining cycles can no longer be extended
togmutually compatible pairs. If no complete basis exists it returnsF ailure; partial bases are obtained by searching
for fewer pairs. This provides an alternative to the clique reduction of Algorithm 6, and in practice either algorithm
suffices for the tilings we consider.
To summarize, we introduce computational methods to search for a symmetrical canonical homology basis. Enumer-
ating homologically non-trivial cycles, classifying them by homology class, and generating the automorphism orbits all
run in polynomial time. The computational bottleneck is the symplectic pair search: finding cycles whose intersection
submatrix matches the canonical symplectic form is equivalent to finding a maximum induced matching, which is
NP-complete in general [42]. Algorithm 6 and Algorithm 7 solve it exactly, but their cost grows with the genus. For
larger tilings, the search slows down and covering allgpairs becomes increasingly difficult, so we do not always find
a complete basis and instead obtaink found ≤gpairs. Some examples of the bases we find are given in Table S3,
and an extensive list of codes is provided in the extended tables section (Section S12). Even for the 8.8.8-400 code
[[400,104,8]], a single weight-12 seed cycle covers 100 of the 104 logical qubits.

Algorithm 7:Symplectic Cycle Pair Search via Backtracking
Input:
1. A setLorL aut ofmnon-trivial cycles{γ 1, . . . , γm}, as produced by Algorithm 3 and/or Algorithm 4;
2. The cycle intersection matrixM∈ {0,1} m×m, as produced by Algorithm 5;
3. The genusgof the surface Σ g.
Output:A canonical homology basisB={(α 1, β1), . . . ,(αkfound , βkfound)}ofk found ≤gsymplectic pairs,
where each pair (α l, βl) = (γil , γjl) is selected from the candidate cycles, orF ailureif no solution is
found within the time limit.
Method:
1.Preprocessing.Iteratively remove any cycleγ i that does not cross any other remaining cycle, i.e.
satisfyingM ij = 0 for all activej. Repeat until no further removals occur. LetA ⊆ {1, . . . , m}
denote the surviving indices. If|A|<2g, returnF ailure.
2.Row ordering heuristic.Sort the active cycles inAin non-decreasing order of their crossing degree
deg(i) :=|{j∈ A:M ij = 1}|, so that cycles participating in fewer candidate pairs are tried first.
3.Backtracking search.CallSolve(1,∅,∅,∅, g), whereSolve(t, R, C, F, g ′) is the recursive
procedure:
(a) Ifg ′ = 0, return the accumulated solution.
(b) If the number of remaining active cycles after positiontis less thang ′, returnF ailure
(pigeonhole pruning).
(c)Degree pruning.Among active cycles from positiontonward that are not inR∪C∪F, count
how many participate in at least one candidate pair with another available cycle. If this count is
less thang ′, returnF ailure.
(d) For each cycleγ i from positiontonward (skipping ifi∈R∪C∪F):
i. For eachγ j withj > i,j /∈R∪C∪F, andM ij = 1:
A. SetR ′ ←R∪ {i},C ′ ←C∪ {j}.
B. Compute the new conflict setF ′ ←F∪ {l̸=j:M il = 1} ∪ {l̸=i:M jl = 1}.
C. Recurse: callSolve(i+1, R ′, C ′, F ′, g ′−1).
D. If the recursive call succeeds, return its solution. Otherwise, backtrack.
(e) ReturnF ailure.
E. Lifting Homology Labels to Semi-Hyperbolic Tilings

S10. COMPUTING DISTANCES OF SEMI-HYPERBOLIC CODES
While the logical error rate under circuit-level noise with practical decoding is the most robust metric with which
we assess the codes considered in this work, another important metric is the code distance. Determining the distance
of the semi-hyperbolic color and surface codes is important both for filtering viable code candidates, as well as for
estimating the performance of codes (via extrapolation) at system sizes and error rates that are currently beyond the
reach of our decoders or Monte Carlo sampling budget. In our search for promising codes, we constructed over 10 4
tilings, and for each tiling we constructed up to 7 semi-hyperbolic codes usingL∈ {1,2, . . . ,7}. Furthermore, many
codes of interest have very large block lengths, some with as many asn≈10 5 data qubits. We emphasize that while
these codes can be large, they can still be dense and therefore useful if they comprise a large fraction of the storage
in a utility-scale quantum computer (and do not encode many more logical qubits than are needed).
Computing the distances of these large codes, not least doing so fast enough to filter through such a large database of
tilings, was beyond the capabilities of the distance finding methods in the literature [44], including even the graphlike
distance finding methods such as the stim methodstim.Circuit.shortest graphlike error[14]. Fortunately, we
were able to develop fast and accurate distance finding methods by adapting the methods for finding short homology
bases (see Section S9) while exploiting the symmetries of the tilings to dramatically reduce the search space without
loss of accuracy.
A. Monochromatic Cycle Distance Upper Bound for Semi-Hyperbolic Color Codes
For semi-hyperbolic color codes, the shortest logical error is not guaranteed to correspond to a monochromatic
substringα C of a homologically nontrivial cycle in the three-colorable tiling as defined in Section S5 A (i.e. a cycle
in a shrunk lattice of [1]). We will refer to the shortest error corresponding to a monochromatic substringα C (for
C∈ {R, G, B}) as the shortest stringlike error. The shortest stringlike color code logical, while not always minimum
weight, is a very useful upper bound for the distance in practice and is usually tight (as we will show). We now define

Running this search from every vertex across large semi-hyperbolic lattices (n≈10 5) remains computationally

labeling the base triangle that contains it. Left multiplication by an elementh∈Ginduces a global isometryA h
of the tiled surface, which acts as a code automorphism that preserves cycle weights, edge colors, and homological
non-triviality.

B. Restricted Lattice Distance Lower Bound for Semi-Hyperbolic Color Codes
While the weight of the shortest stringlike errord string provides an efficient upper bound on the color code distance,
d≤d string, a general color code logical operator can branch (since each single-qubit error anti-commutes with three

[PDF page 97]
78
stabilizers), potentially resulting in a lower physical weight. While we do find thatd=d string holds for the vast
majority of codes we consider, we do find some codes (mostly distance 2) where it does not. A simple example is the
Bolza code, which has distance 2 despite its shortest stringlike error having weight 4, as shown in Figure S59. In this
section we show that we can also efficiently compute a usefullower boundon the distance of the color code. When
this lower bound matches the stringlike upper boundd string, as is often the case, we can efficiently certify the true
distance.

Let us first define the restricted lattices [45, 46]. Recall that the base triangular tilingT △, the dual of the color
code tilingT, has vertices colored{R, G, B}corresponding to the faces ofT. Each edgee := (u, v)∈ T △ is assigned
a colorc(e)∈ {R, G, B} \ {c(u), c(v)}that complements the colors of the two vertices at its endpoints. Equivalently,
each edge inT △ is assigned the same color as the edge it crosses inT. For each colorC∈ {R, G, B}we form the
C-restricted latticeR C, whose edge set is all the edges of colorCinT △. Its vertices are the dual vertices of the other
two colors{R, G, B} \ {C}. The vertices of colorC(and their incident edges) are deleted, each replaced with a face
bounded by the remaining surrounding edges. See Figure S60 for an illustration of the restricted lattices.
Each data qubit corresponds to a vertexvinTand the corresponding triangular faceτ v in the dual triangular
latticeT △. Each data qubit is therefore incident to exactly one edge in each restricted latticeR C. We denote by
eC(v) theC-colored edge belonging toτ v, i.e. the edge inR C adjacent tov(see Figure S60). Since color codes are
self-dual, it is sufficient to consider onlyX-type errors, and thenZ-type errors can be handled similarly. We represent
a physical PauliXerror using a binary vectorx∈F n

in the tables in Section S12. For both color and surface codes we plot the runtime (measured using AMD EPYC

The restricted lattice systoles can be computed efficiently using essentially the same methods as described for the
upper bound computation in Section S10 A, using unweighted BFS searches on the restricted lattices after establishing
their homology coordinate system using a tree-cotree decomposition, and again restricting the BFS to source vertices
contained within a fundamental triangle to speed up the search without loss of accuracy. Wheneverd res =d string, the
code distance is certified exactly, using only efficient graph algorithms.
C. Distance Computation for Semi-Hyperbolic Surface Codes
For semi-hyperbolic surface codes, the minimum-weight logicalZ(X) errors correspond to the shortest homo-
logically non-trivial cycles in the primal (dual) cell complexT cell (Figure S40). We compute the code distance
d= min(d X , dZ) by exploiting the symmetries of the tiling in an analogous way. After establishing a homology
coordinate system onT cell and its dual via tree-cotree decomposition, we find the shortest non-trivial cycles using a
BFS.
As in the color code case, any homologically non-trivial cycle must cross between neighboring fundamental domains
of the underlying tiling’s symmetry group (whether a quadrilateral reflection group□(r, s, t, u) or a triangle group
△(2,4, q)). Global automorphisms therefore allow us to restrict the BFS source vertices to a small subset of boundary
verticesBcontained within a single fundamental domain (Figure S58b). This preserves exactness while reducing the
number of search sources fromO(L 2|G|) toO(L).

L. We writed CC for the exact distance returned by the connected cluster search [47], which is exhaustive and therefore exact
whenever it terminates within its budget. “All codes” covers every code at thatLand the conditiond res =d string certifies the
distance exactly. “Connected cluster completed” is restricted to the codes for which the connected cluster algorithm [47, 48]
was run (we restricted the search ton≤2000) and finished (we set a timeout of 300 seconds).
D. Performance Benchmarks for Distance Finding Methods
We benchmarked the runtime and accuracy of our distance finding methods that we have described in this
section, and compared them against the existing methods. The methods we compare against for the semi-
hyperbolic color codes are the connected cluster algorithm [47, 48], and the stim hypergraph search method
stim.Circuit.search for undetectable logical errors[14]. The connected cluster algorithm is exact but has
complexityn(∆−1) d−1 for a code with check weight ∆ and distanced. The stim hypergraph search method is a
heuristic with tunable parameters to adjust the size of the search, and only produces an upper bound on the dis-
tance. For the semi-hyperbolic surface codes, we compare our systole search method (Section S10 C) against the stim
methodstim.Circuit.shortest graphlike errorand connected cluster method. We refer the reader to [44] for
a comparison of a broader range of existing exact and heuristic distance finding methods for hyperbolic surface and
color codes withn <1200.
Our runtime comparisons are shown in Figure S61. Empirically, across the system sizes we benchmarked, our systole
search methods for both color and surface codes are an excellent fit to a runtime scaling oft∝n. In the limit of large
system sizes we expect the complexity to becomet∝ |B|n⌈2g/w⌉, whereBis the set of source nodes (restricted to a
fundamental domain). By contrast, the stim and connected cluster methods have a much steeper scaling withn(and
are exponential ind), and are therefore too slow to be practical for system sizes above around 10 3–104 qubits. This
demonstrates the importance of our efficient distance finding methods for evaluating codes with large system sizes
relevant to utility-scale quantum computation.
The connected cluster method, being exact, is very useful for evaluating the accuracy (tightness) of our color code
distance bounds for small system sizes. In Table S5 we compare our efficient distance bounds with each other for
32,689 tilings we constructed, as well as comparing them to the connected cluster (exact) distance for the subset
of codes we could run it on within a time budget of 300 seconds. We find that the distance is efficiently certified
(dres =d string) for a large fraction of the codes (88% to 100% of codes depending onL). When comparing to the
exact distance computed with connected cluster, we find that the exact distance matches theupper boundd string for
all codes for which connected cluster finished atL= 3 andL= 4, and with a>98% agreement forL= 1 andL= 2.
This suggests that the stringlike distanced string is an excellent estimate of the true distance for most codes, despite
being only an upper bound. Furthermore, in Table S6 we show that we only observe a loose boundd string > dat
L= 2 for tilings where theL= 1 code has true distanced= 2 and the loose upper boundd string = 4.
S11. ADDITIONAL NUMERICAL SIMULATIONS
A. Asymmetry ofXandZBases for Modular Hyperbolic Surface Codes with Even Side LengthL
For quantum hardware with approximately isotropic noise (noX/Zbias), it is generally desirable for a code to
perform equally well in both theXbasis andZbasis. For modular hyperboliccolorcodes this is guaranteed (at least
in a code capacity setting), since the codes are all self-dual. In this section we studyX/Zasymmetries of modular
hyperbolicsurfacecodes, including how this differs for odd and even side lengthL. As shown in Figure S62, for

FIG. S62. Stabilizer structure of modular hyperbolic surface codes derived from{4,6}regular tilings. Each blue plaquette
denotes aZstabilizer with support on its vertices, and similarly each red plaquette denotes anXstabilizer.a, For odd side
length (hereL= 3) the weight-6 stabilizers at the four corners of each base face alternate betweenXandZtype.b, For even
side length (hereL= 4) the weight-6 stabilizers are all of the same Pauli type (here all weight-six stabilizers areZ-type).
modular hyperbolic surface codes derived from regular tilings, the structure of theXandZstabilizers is identical
for oddL. In particular, the number ofXandZstabilizers of each size is identical, and theXandZstabilizers can
be swapped by applying a symmetry of the tiling. In contrast, for evenL, the structure ofXandZstabilizers is no
longer identical. This results in asymmetricXandZlogical error rates for evenL, as shown in Figure S63. In fact,
the overall logical error rate of an even-Lmodular hyperbolic code (for these regular tilings) barely improves on the
odd (L−1) code derived from the same tiling.
We now study this asymmetry in more detail more generally for quadrilateral tilings. Consider a quadrilateral tiling
of a closed hyperbolic surface with face configuration Va.b.c.d, for example constructed from a□(r, s, t, u) quadrilateral
group where (a, b, c, d) = (2r,2s,2t,2u). The vertices of each square have degreesa,b,candd(going clockwise around
the square). We label each vertex based on which corner of the quadrilateral fundamental domain it is in the image
of. Specifically, we label a vertex asa-type if it is in the image of the degree-avertex of the fundamental domain,
and similarly forb-type,c-type andd-type. Therefore, in a slight abuse of notation, ana-type vertex has degreea,
and similarly forb-type,c-type andd-type vertices. LetN a be the number ofa-type vertices (with degreea), and
similarly forN b,N c andN d. We now count the number of faces|F|, edges|E|and vertices|V|in the tiling. First,
since all faces are quadrilaterals we know that 4|F|= 2|E|. Since each face is incident to one vertex of each type we
also know that

TABLE S13. Modular hyperbolic color codes for side lengthL= 3 with quadrilateral modules (K= 2 base triangles per
module).M 2: number of quadrilateral modules.N M2: total physical qubits in the modular layout including both data qubits
and ancilla qubits.N M2 /k: physical-to-logical qubit overhead. Automorphism Bases: logical basis operator coveragek found/g
and generator seed weightsw(length of the shortest homologous dual cycle found for each seed) andw 1 (the same, restricted
to dual edges of a single colour, with one colour per basis chosen to minimise the total), showing Pareto-optimal bases with
≥85% coverage or the single highest-coverage basis if<85%. Cov.: maximum basis coverage percentage. Pair: mean number
of automorphism moves between measuring the two cycles of a symplectic pair with a fixed extractor. Cayley: mean and
maximum of the same over all pairs of basis loops. Both are right Cayley graph distances among basis automorphisms, with
the extractor placed to minimise Pair (then the maximum, then the mean), for the listed basis with the highest coverage.
kd2/n: code figure of merit.

TABLE S14. Modular hyperbolic color codes for side lengthL= 4 with quadrilateral modules (K= 2 base triangles per
module).M 2: number of quadrilateral modules.N M2: total physical qubits in the modular layout including both data qubits
and ancilla qubits.N M2 /k: physical-to-logical qubit overhead. Automorphism Bases: logical basis operator coveragek found/g
and generator seed weightsw(length of the shortest homologous dual cycle found for each seed) andw 1 (the same, restricted
to dual edges of a single colour, with one colour per basis chosen to minimise the total), showing Pareto-optimal bases with
≥85% coverage or the single highest-coverage basis if<85%. Cov.: maximum basis coverage percentage. Pair: mean number
of automorphism moves between measuring the two cycles of a symplectic pair with a fixed extractor. Cayley: mean and
maximum of the same over all pairs of basis loops. Both are right Cayley graph distances among basis automorphisms, with
the extractor placed to minimise Pair (then the maximum, then the mean), for the listed basis with the highest coverage.
kd2/n: code figure of merit.

[28] For readers familiar with modern code surgery methods, the construction we described is very similar tothickening, which
is a common technique used in various prior works on surgery [21, 23, 26]. A technical difference is that some surgery
checks in the first layer are wide (implemented with two ancilla check qubits), which act on two ancilla data qubits in the
second layer. This design choice helps ensure the deformed code has sufficient distance in the surgery protocol.
[29] In the modern graph surgery language, thisZoperator on the extractor is a ‘cycle check’. Normally we need to measure
these cycle checks, but in our case of the ribbon extractor this cycle check is a product of deformed stabilizers. [22, 23, 26].
